# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 6

## Acumen is establishing a $250m innovative and multi-country investment initiative to deploy impact-focused capital and targeted technical assistance off-grid solar companies.

The goal of "Hardest-to-Reach" (HTR) geographies is to address the energy access challenge. The initiative will focus on least developed countries (LDCs) in sub-Saharan Africa, particularly with electrification rates at or below the sub-regional average of 25%. Acumen invites interested parties to join this initiative and willing to enter at sufficient scale to meet the energy access and climate resilience needs of underserved segments.

### Climate change problem

Across the eligible Hardest-to-Reach (HTR) geographies, there are around 54 million African households that do not have access to modern electricity services. Even compared to their sub-Saharan African peers, these countries are being "left behind" on energy access and are not on track to achieve Sustainable Development Goal (SDG)7 by 2030. These communities are also among the world's poorest below the lower-middle-income poverty line™, and these communities are both fast-growing sources of new energy demand and highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The region is experiencing more heat stress, more frequent droughts, more intense floods, and extreme weather events including tropical cyclones. Nearly half of the bottom-twenty countries listed in the ND-Gain Climate Vulnerability Index® are HTR geographies. The average score for these countries on the Maplecroft Climate Vulnerability Index™ is 36.6, compared to a global average of 59.8. Maplecroft's Climate Vulnerability Index® Fig 1 (See below) shows an disproportionate incidence of climate vulnerability across Africa, where population growth is also at its highest.

Fig 1: Maplecroft Climate Vulnerability Index (2018)

### References

* World Bank DoCaT (n.d)
    * The ND-Gain Country Index™ summarizes a country's vulnerability to climate change and other global challenges in combination with the potential to improve resilience. Higher index scores are given to less climate vulnerable countries.
    * The Maplecroft Climate Change Vulnerability Index provides a single figure rating for climate exposure, sensitivity and adaptive capacity of each country. It is based on 100 indicators that measure the impact of extreme weather events, extremes and change with the current human sensitivity to those climate stresses and the capacity of the country to adapt to the impacts of climate change. Higher index scores are given to less climate vulnerable countries.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 7

## In the face of escalating climate variability and change, the delivery of climate-resilient energy access infrastructure and services is now more critical than ever.

The IEA Africa Energy Outlook projects that, for every person in sub-Saharan Africa to have access to electricity by 2030, thus achieving SDG.7, 6% of the current total population or 90 million people, including a significant proportion of women and children, will be left behind. This is because the IEA Africa Energy Outlook projects that both clean and climate resilient households in Africa’s LDCs will face the climate future with increasing uncertainty and risk to their livelihoods, health and wellbeing.

The interplay of several factors points to the need for an energy access solution that puts climate adaptation at the heart of its design.

## Inadequacy of existing infrastructure

The past decade of climate trends and events has displayed the fragility of existing network infrastructure against extreme weather shocks, with the significant damage and long periods without electricity supply. The combination of frequent power outages and prolonged blackouts with power outages come disruptions to household routines, diminished public safety, and halted economic activity, while the regularity of these incidents mean that households and small businesses are forced into costly coping strategies, ranging from the purchase of water tanks and diesel generators, to keeping expensive back-up inventories.

## Vulnerability of people without access

1. **IEA Africa Energy Outlook (2022)**
2. **World Bank Policy Research - Undercapitalized Potential: The business costs of unreliable infrastructure in developing countries**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 8

Households without any electricity access, meanwhile, are trapped in climate vulnerability as they lack the social, technological, and financial resources that are enabled and multiplied by energy access, such as early warning and post-disaster public notifications. For these households, energy access can be a key to resilience. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) believes that addressing the energy access challenge through OGS products can have a transformational impact on economic activity, which can boost economic resilience to climate shocks. Around a third of the total investment in GCF projects is expected to be directed towards energy access and services to help countries achieve their NDCs and boost enterprise. Usage of SHS in East Africa boosted monthly earnings by US$ 46 a month, around 14% increase on average income.*

## Dependence on nature

The Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD)* has pointed to the disproportionate dependence of the poorest and most marginalized on environmental resources to meet their basic needs, such as forests (for energy, materials and food), land (for farming and grazing) and water. The OECD also notes that the poor are often the last to benefit from electrification that investors and developers deploy in low-income communities, should be the lead beneficiaries of the most climate resilient, and the most supportive of community norms, even as the benefits of development unfold.

## Inequitable distribution of climate burden

On these considerations alone, entry-level distributed residential solar compares favorably to other alternatives, separate from its mitigation and energy security benefits. And though the continent has combined a population of 1 billion people, it is estimated that only about 20% of the countries and livelihoods across the continent is disproportionately high.* Such climate injustice already threatens to undermine development gains and inflict additional economic costs and social disruption. The continent will need to invest at least $500 billion annually by 2030.

The African Development Bank's African Economic Outlook summarizes several dynamics at play in the continent’s potential low-carbon transition out of energy poverty:**

* **Low carbon transitions** is Africa vary by country, but they could be transformational. These variations reflect differing ecological zones, climates, settlement patterns, economic structures, resource endowments and development priorities.
* **Africa is generously endowed with renewable energy resources**, such solar irradiation, which present significant opportunities for the green transition.
* **With Africa's limited lock-in to fossil-based energy technologies**, renewable energy technology opportunities could help the continent build a climate-resilient and integrated sustainable energy sector.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 9

## Intervention to achieve climate resilience and a just energy transition in the continent should be inclusive: "leaving no one behind."

Africa needs a decade of highly adaptive and resilience-promoting infrastructure investment to deliver on the promise of sustainable development by 2020 and in this century. Yet, across the African continent, only a small fraction of the required investments have been made. In countries such as Ethiopia, Kenya, Senegal,and Ghana, as well as Eritrea and Rwanda where there is still significant scope for development. A substantial scale-up of investment is needed throughout sub-Saharan Africa, as it totals $1 trillion annually to meet the continent’s energy needs.

The lesson from across the globe, historically, is that in a business-as-usual emissions trajectory, the drivers of greenhouse gas emissions, sometimes twice as large as the second most contributing economic sector. Still, today, power generation remains the largest GHG-emitting sector in developed economies across Europe and North America. The scale of the challenge to achieve climate resilience and inclusive development trajectories is highly material for mitigation outcomes, and showing that the continent can deliver on its promise of Africa’s Agenda 2063 is highly material for achieving the United Nations SDGs and the Paris Accord. A similar fossil-fuelled trajectory in sub-Saharan Africa would be catastrophic for our planet. At current emissions per person were to rise to only the current global average, the continent would emit 15.2 billion tons of greenhouse gases per year by 2050—roughly equivalent to all emissions from China and India today.

## Proposed Intervention

Acumen’s Hard-to-Reach energy access initiative seeks to mobilize a blend of institutional and donor funding to support private sector investment into the region, contributing to closing the energy access gap in sub-Saharan Africa towards achieving SDG 7 by providing more than 60 million people with clean, climate-resilient, off-grid solar energy products. Hard-to-Reach (HTR) will establish two separate financial vehicles to address the challenges of scaling up off-grid solar energy: a facility that will match the needs of off-grid solar companies and markets. This is designed to help-off grid solar companies to expand their reach in sub-Saharan Africa, while also providing them with the capital they need to be inclusive scale with cross-cutting climate impacts.

The first facility, the $60M grant-funded Market Support Facility (MSF), intends to deploy debt, equity, and grants using a patient capital approach that is consistent with the needs of off-grid solar companies. The MSF will provide a range of financial products in this new market with a range of capital investment options and technical assistance accompaniment. A second vehicle, the $10M HTR Off-Grid Solar Grant Facility (HTR OSG), will provide grants to support access to OGS players, with the objective of responsibly expanding the reach and impact of OGS technologies, while also promoting innovation and sustainability. To this end, HTR seeks to support companies with technical assistance and capacity development to ensure the sustainability of the intervention and its impacts beyond the life of the fund.

The H2R initiative is designed to overcome a number of market barriers that currently hinder more widespread climate-resilient energy access in sub-Saharan Africa. These are outlined in the table below.

| Market barrier | H2R response |
| --- | --- |
| Markets in H2R areas are too risky or not attractive for many investors. | Perceived risk may differ from the real risk. At the portfolio level, H2R will leverage deeper market research to best assess the risk of investing in H2R areas. In addition, H2R’s structured finance structure mitigates risk by grouping in investors with different risk appetites and investment horizons. H2R also aims to attract philanthropically-backed patient capital to support market entry for OGS companies. |
| OGS companies experience capacity barriers to expand into H2R areas. | The targeted use of set-up grants, equity and debt from the Market Support Facility aims to address management, marketing, and technical challenges that are preventing an OGS company’s difficulty and company's needs. The TA will seek to improve capacity and ensure responsible, sustainable growth (including through high standards for environmental and social performance). |
| There is a lack of flexible funding for OGS companies looking to expand into H2R areas. | Acumen intends to raise a $200M vehicle, specifically designed to invest via impact-linked finance in OGS companies. Acumen seeks to link the interest rate to impact-related KPIs to incentivize investors and provide better returns to their customers, thereby avoiding mission drift. The grant capital as part of this vehicle is intended to permit interest rates adjustments to further incentivise first-time energy access. |
| People in H2R areas can't afford OGS products or services; are unaware of their benefits; or are afraid to pay for OGS company credit. | Acumen incentivizes and facilitates OGS companies to set up, market and sell solar energy products via PAYGO financing. The program aims to provide affordable access to people in H2R areas, who can spend the cost of the solar product over a few months or years. Acumen, through Hard-to-Reach, is also working with governments and other stakeholders in target countries to address the affordability gap through demand-side measures such as subsidies and financial inclusion for customers. H2R aims to accompany OGS companies to implement strong consumer protection practices, aligned with GOGLA Consumer Credit Principles. |

## Protection Principles and work with domain experts where required to deliver specialist technical assistance.

### Climate results/benefits

By the end of the program, investments developed, financed, and accompanied by H2R will be able to provide:

- ~72.4M people with year-round access to clean, affordable, reliable and modern off-grid solar power.
- 120 MW of newly installed, decentralized solar energy generating capacity across H2R geographies.
- ~54.3M people with first-time energy access and improved climate resilience through decentralized solar power delivery.
- ~5.6M people with improved climate resilience through diversified income sources enabled by first-time access to a solar-powered product.
- 5.1M tCO2e in GHG emissions reduced or avoided (excl. black carbon mitigation impact).
- In addition, some 13,922 clean energy jobs, with 4,641 as sources of employment for women.

### Footnote
*^18 GGLA Consumer Protection Code consists of a set of principles and an assessment framework to enable companies to evaluate, document and improve their practices, and provide investors and other stakeholders with a framework to promote good consumer protection (CP) practice. The code establishes the de facto industry standard for CP that adds value to off-grid solar companies' investors and other stakeholders.*

Africa is the most vulnerable continent to climate change impacts under all climate scenarios above 1.5 degrees Celsius.* Exponential collateral damage in the face of climate change poses systemic risks to the continent’s economies, infrastructure investments, water and food systems, public health, agriculture, and livelihoods. These risks are particularly acute for Africa’s poorest populations, who are disproportionately poor. There are substantial disparities across Africa, with many countries in sub-Saharan Africa being the most vulnerable to climate change impacts.

Least Developed Countries (LDCs) and High-Reach-to-Tangent countries. All are highly vulnerable, and least ready under the NDI-GAIN indicators, ranging from 1st – 56th most vulnerable to; 127th to 176th most ready to cope with adaptation challenges.

Constraints to the supply and demand of electricity drive the energy access problem. Two drivers: household poverty, which limits a household’s ability to pay for electricity, and the lack of physical access to electricity infrastructure. The combination of these two factors means that people without access to electricity are particularly harmful to climate vulnerable populations. According to the baseline content can be presented through two indicators: 1) the number of people with no access to electricity at all and 2) the number of the global extreme poor population reside in this region*, with more than three quarters living below the $3.65/day poverty line across HDR program countries, compared to only one quarter for the global average.

This vulnerability has recently been confirmed by a large-scale climate survey of thousands of people across sub-Saharan African countries. The survey found that 88% of people feel that climate change is already affecting their lives, and 60% say they have experienced more frequent or severe weather-related environmental degradation to affect their income and source of livelihood, and 57% of people have already incurred economic losses from climate change. These findings demonstrate that climate change is no longer only a future threat, but a reality that many vulnerable people in Africa are living with already.

Climate Adaptation Context

The IPCC sixth assessment report shows that most regions in Africa have experienced a gradual increase in temperatures in the past 40 years which has led to an increase in the frequency of annual heatwaves. Rainfall patterns over sub-Saharan Africa have also changed, and there is evidence for significant inter-annual variability with some areas experiencing significantly higher rainfall totals while others experience significantly lower rainfall totals. Projections indicate that both mean temperatures and temperature extremes are likely to continue increasing across most regions of Africa in the future. Precipitation is likely to increase over the western Sahel and over parts of southern Africa, but it is projected to decrease over the eastern Sahel, and over parts of central Africa. The frequency and severity of heavy rainfall events are projected to increase throughout Africa, except in the northern and southwestern regions.

## Precipitation Change

- **Observed**
    - A severe lack of weather station data has led to large uncertainty around observed Climate Change patterns for the region.
    - There is evidence of an increase in drought conditions since the mid-20th Century. Some areas have seen precipitation levels have declined by 35% or more in most regions except the northwest.

## Mid-Century Projections

- **Observed**
    - The Gulf of Guinea has experienced more severe precipitation events and the frequency of storm events has tripled.
    - Extreme precipitation has led to flooding in some areas.
    - Precipitation has increased in frequency since 1950.

- **Projected** (under all scenarios)
    - Heat-related mortality risks will increase 4.6 times greater under 2°C GWL
    - Lethal heat days will increase from 50 to 70 days at 1.6 GWL and 100–150 days [2.5°C GWL]
    - Lethal heat days will increase by 4-27 days at GWL 2 and 35-39 days at GWL 3°C
    - Heat waves frequency is projected to increase while the duration of extreme heatwave events may last longer than current highs of 4.1°C GWL
    - Lethal heat days will increase from 60 days to 70–80 days (1.6 GWL) and 100-250 days [2.5°C GWL]
    - Projected to increase under all scenarios.

Temperature Change
Observed
• Mean annual and seasonal temperatures have increased by 1.04°C and 1.44% respectively since 1961.
• The number of hot days and increased heat stress has increased in the last four decades while the frequency of cold extremes and cold days has decreased.


Mid-century Projections

## Precipitation Change Observed
- The frequency of dry spell and agricultural drought in the region has increased since 1961.
- The number of consecutive dry days with rainfall increased by between 2.5–3 events per decade.
- The number and intensity of extreme precipitation events have increased over the last century.

## Mid-century Projections
- Mean annual rainfall in the summer rainfall region is projected to decrease by 10–20% during the period 2046–2070 under RCP8.5.
- The number of consecutive dry days with rainfall is expected to increase under RCP8.5.
- The frequency and duration of droughts are projected to increase over large parts of southern Africa at 1.5°C and even more significantly at 2°C.

The number of observed climate-related hazard events in sub-Saharan Africa has also increased in recent years (Fig 2) and at a faster pace than the rest of the world. Given the high poverty levels and dependence of most rural households on agriculture, climate extremes exacerbate the vulnerability of the region. The continent is already experiencing some of the highest rates of climate-related disasters faced by any continent due to its fragile states. An increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events has led to a significant increase in the number of people being affected by these events. Six times more people were affected by droughts in 2010–2019 relative to 1970–1979, while flooding impacted eight times more people over the same period. Furthermore, sub-Saharan Africa has experienced a significant increase in the number of climate-related disasters in the world on the ND-Gain index.

## Regional vulnerability to the changing climate and increasing climate hazards is exacerbated by non-climate drivers such as high poverty levels, weak infrastructure, limited access to modern energy sources, and fragile grid electricity and telecommunications networks. This lack of resilience exacerbates Africa's climate vulnerability.

The region’s energy systems have been unable to adapt effectively to extreme weather events, resulting in economic losses, disruptions to critical services, and social instability. Accordingly, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Sixth Assessment Report (AR6) considers energy generation as an important component of the systems transition of energy, water, and land use.

Energy system transitions are pursued together to exploit synergies and reduce costs. In the context of energy system transitions, the most feasible adaptation options support infrastructure resilience, reliable power systems, and efficient water use: "Energy generation diversification, including with renewable energy resources, can provide a more resilient supply of electricity, while large-scale hydroelectric[1]...can reduce vulnerabilities to climate change, especially in rural populations (high confidence)".

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 21

It is now well understood that extreme weather events induced by climate change are a major source of vulnerability. While these impacts are well tracked in developing countries, extreme weather events and their impacts tend to be underreported when they happen in Africa, meaning that we have a much lower level of documentation on the impact of extreme weather events in Africa compared with other regions.

Furthermore, even where events are registered internationally, they often are those affecting the more population-dense, and better connected areas. This means that the true extent of the impacts is not fully understood.

With this being the case, it is likely that the vulnerability profile of African countries understates the levels of exposure of the rural poor, who are least able to implement measures to protect themselves from this. EM-DAT provides a detailed look at extreme weather events impacting Africa in 2022 (see Figure 4 below).

### Figure 4: Extreme Weather Events in Africa, 2022

![Extreme Weather Events in Africa](https://www.greenclimatefund.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/figure-4-extreme-weather-events-in-africa-2022.png)

The energy access-climate-rural poverty contest

Today's climate change impacts are felt most acutely in developing economies. In sub-Saharan Africa, where 75% of the world’s LDCs are located, climate hazards such as floods, rising temperatures, windy droughts, storms, among others, are real and present threats to lives and livelihoods, inflicting tangible


---
**Page 26**
---

collateral damage and physical risks to infrastructure investments, water and food systems, public health and agriculture. The economies and livelihoods of most sub-Saharan African nations are driven by rain-fed agriculture and pastoralism. FAO reported that agriculture alone provided jobs for almost 50% of the total labor force of these countries.

At the same time, rural poverty is widespread in the target countries of H2R, and considerably exceeds the rate of urban poverty (see Figure 5 below). As mentioned, all H2R countries are LDCs with a high incidence of both poverty and climate vulnerability among the millions of smallerholder farmers and the communities that depend on them. This rural poverty is often more severe than in cities, which sometimes available in cities. In addition, as the Global Center for Adaptation (GCA) "State and Trends in Adaptation" report 2021 notes large parts of Africa are especially the drylands that cover 60 percent of the continent and water at a rate faster than the global average, putting half a billion people at risk.

Rural poverty is also exacerbated by the lack of access to basic services provided by electricity. This explains why spatial and temporal variations in rainfall and recurrent drought episodes have severe impacts on the livelihoods of people living throughout the continent.42

Up until now the continent has seen adaptation interventions that are primarily reactive rather than proactive, such as efforts focused on disaster relief or reconstruction and recovery following severe floods or a pandemic. The Africa-Led Consultation Dialogue Report on adaptation highlights the complementary need for a hazard-and risk-oriented approach to adaptation, which considers it holistically across factors such as climate change, conflict, and fragility. This approach is necessary in order to “enable preparedness not just address the climate risks but also the various shocks such as natural disasters, conflicts, and economic crises.” The GAC 2021 report points to new research which considers interlinkages between climate, conflict and energy. In the face of increasing climate stressors, there is the risk that certain energy systems are potentially misallocative and could play a contributory role in increasing the risk of conflict. The disruption of energy supply can also be a target for sabotage or attacks, particularly by non-state armed groups. Additionally, increased energy prices can have significant impacts on overall food security, contributing to drivers of conflict. As such it is recommended that countries should consider how they develop energy systems that are the most resilient and least vulnerable to the physical and human dimensions of climate change. Many of these adaptation-based criteria, the case for decentralized solar power, particularly residential solar, as a prominent part of the electricity mix, has become a competing one.

The ultimate measure of the effects of energy access on climate resilience will be to extent which off-grid residential solar electrification can be associated with reducing sensitivity and exposure, and increasing adaptive capacity during adverse effects of shocks and stresses related to climate (see Figure 2). Social vulnerability is also reduced by increased access to information and communication technologies. For example, supports early warning systems can prevent human and economic losses in case of extreme events, while extending rural electrification can enable adaptation and mitigate climate vulnerability. It based on the assumption that energy access will be associated with a reduction in fossil fuel consumption and increase in renewable energy supply. As a view of NAPs and NDCs highlights[1], low levels of energy access are considered to reduce resilience by increasing exposure to adverse effects of climate change, while increasing energy needs, rising increased emissions. It is therefore important for energy access measures to capture the full range of benefits that can be achieved through improved energy access. Energy access reduces reliance on biomass fuels, leading to less wood extraction and charcoal use - which currently exceeds tree production, and increases resilience against the climatic trend of soil depletion and tree loss (roughly 10% per decade). Improved energy access also enables more efficient cooking methods that affect the ability of people to access important health, or early warning information in the context of disaster risk reduction. The benefits of improved energy access are not limited to increased resilience but the impact of this improvement will be limited without basic access. Per capita energy consumption is also strongly correlated to economic output. At higher levels of energy consumption and through productive use of energy, energy access can unlock a wide range of social and economic development outcomes that unlock socioeconomic opportunities[2]. Adaptation impacts are also unlocked when we understand and capture the full range of benefits that can be achieved through improved energy access beyond just low- to medium-energy access alongside mobile telecommunications, TV or radio to enable early hazard warning.

Socio-economic development is one of the foremost strategies to reduce community vulnerability[3], and access to clean energy is a conversion of sustainable socio-economic development. Energy access as a whole is an important component in the achievement of SDG 7. It also plays a critical role in the role it play in providing the stepping stone towards wider access. Therefore, delivering enhanced, even basic energy services can be a key enabler for the achievement of adaptation-linked socio-economic development outcomes that drive resilience of the population[4].

At a practical level, lighting, cooking, heating, cooling, communication and information access services are derived from energy. A community's basic needs, economic activities and institutions (schools, health centers, etc.) can be supported by improved energy access. Energy access is also critical for education; for these reasons, long-term access to reliable energy builds self-reliance and adaptive capacity within

## Specific benefits of enhanced access include (see also Table 3 for examples):

- Enhancing energy, food and water security by improving self-reliance in the face of increasing climate-induced risks;
- Providing easier and lower-cost access to information and digital services which may enhance overall resilience against climate change impacts such as DRR;
- Improving the health of households and communities by e.g. enabling access to health-information, providing clean lighting without accompanying indoor air quality degradation, thus reducing climate-induced diseases (e.g. vector borne diseases) while utilizing mitigation measures to improve health and living standards;
- Diversifying jobs, particularly for women who can expand their opportunities as a result of time-saving achieved through greater household productivity, thus increasing their resilience to slow-onset events;
- Diversifying livelihoods and the local economy by boosting incomes, savings, and productivity of households and enterprises, also increasing their resilience to slow-onset events.

There is also a moderately strong link between rural poverty and access to electricity, with a correlation factor of 0.51. ## Attention to principles for demonstrating the climate rationale of adaptation activities

### Table 4: Response to H2R Submission to B3/05 requirements

| Classification | B3/05 Requirement | Acumen HDR |
| --- | --- | --- |
| (a) Identification | Adaptation proposals should show how the activity addresses current or future projected climate change risk and vulnerability, and how it will lead to a more responsive adaptation. Proposals should identify the systems at risk and the climate change hazard affecting them or expected to hit in the future. They should show how climate change has led, or will lead, to the specific risk or impact that the proposed activity aims to address. Proposals should also consider any non-climate factors that may be causing or exacerbating the vulnerability. This is important for understanding the interactions between climate change and non-climatic drivers. Vulnerability assessments can be used to identify groups, sectors and sub-regions most susceptible to the impacts of climate change. These assessments provide information to select and prioritize appropriate adaptation measures. | N/A |
| (b) Response: | Proposals should explain how the activity will reduce vulnerability or risk by protecting assets (e.g., livelihoods, ecosystems) and thus lessen the climate change risk or impact. Where relevant, a justification should be given why the proposed activity was selected over alternatives. Proposals should consider barriers (e.g., technical expertise, financial constraints) to the implementation of the activity and describe how the project aims to overcome these barriers. Proposals should apply a methodological approach for the quantification of the number of beneficiaries expected to result from the activity. | N/A |
| (c) Alignment: | Proposals should confirm alignment of the proposed adaptation activities with national or sub-national climate strategies (including their NAPs, NAPAs, long-term climate strategies, and adaptation. |

## Emissions Context

Sub-Saharan Africa currently accounts for less than 4% of cumulative historical global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. However, the region's economies and populations are growing rapidly, and emissions have been increasing at a rate since 1950 that is projected to continue unabated by a factor of 14. Today, Africa is home to 1.3 billion people; this number is projected to grow to 3 billion by 2060. More than half the population in Sub-Saharan Africa lives on less than $1 per day and has limited access to electricity services. In rural areas, access to electricity services is made possible through local kiosks which provide cell phone charging, refrigeration and other electricity-driven services. In off-grid areas, these kiosks and other local energy solutions are often the only means of accessing electricity for lighting, cooking, and people's periodic access to these service points constrains their electricity usage. As a result, off-grid communities have a higher per capita demand for energy than urban dwellers, and current household energy consumption does not reflect the real demand for energy services.

Due to the low base of current energy use on the continent, and particularly in H2R program countries, and the rapidly growing and youthful populations, the continent's future energy trajectory has real implications for global climate change. The continued reliance on fossil fuels by Sub-Saharan Africa's 1 billion people living in extreme poverty that relies on fossil fuels, could result in increased CO2 emissions that would offset a 20 percent cut in the emissions of China, the United States, India, Russia, Japan, and Germany combined.

Currently, the majority of rural households rely on emission-intensive biomass for their primary energy source. In Sub-Saharan Africa, the percentage of total wood harvested used as fuelwood varies from 90% in Africa, 62% in Asia, 50% in South America to less than 20% in Europe and North America. The use of biomass for energy is a major contributor to deforestation, alleviating the pressure on native forests while also reducing the share of agriculture, forestry and land

**Page 39**

In 2030, 73% of people were still living without access to (affordable, clean) electricity, of which 298 million people were in nascent or off-grid (OGS) markets where there is little commercial OGS activity. Approximately 70% of this number have the ability to pay but live in areas deemed too remote or too risky for grid expansion. When a market is deemed commercially viable and when the appropriate investment capital and technical assistance is provided to OGS companies, then they will likely be able to deliver clean energy access and adaptive capacity to low-income, underserved people and build sustainable markets. However, this is not happening in many nascent or off-grid (OGS) markets, often through an enabling environment, to operate and grow in currently untapped, “Hardest-to-Reach” (HTR) areas.

There are however multiple barriers that prevent access to alternative forms of energy access, such as OGS. There is a funding gap, as the OGS market for the most deprived customers is considered highly risky, with the potential customers often the poorest populations in a country, and there are few track records of companies successfully delivering clean energy services to these populations. This is largely due to a lack of internal capacity and inability to access finance. Markets are often either unregulated or, if regulated, have weak enforcement mechanisms. In addition, many OGS markets suffer from poor infrastructure, lack of access to capital, in turn leading to OGS companies being underfunded, and to markets stagnating rather than growing rapidly in response to the clear needs of the rural populations where energy access is lowest.

The Hardest-to-Reach energy access initiative aims to deliver energy access to 72.4M low-income customers in least developed and climate-vulnerable countries in sub-Saharan Africa. 54.3M of whom will be accessing electricity for the first time, and 18M will be accessing clean cooking solutions. The initiative partners, governments and donors, to establish two new funds, HRR MSF and HRR MEF, which will provide early stage and scaled-up funding to OGS companies. The engagement will also seek to lower barriers to entry for OGS companies by providing technical assistance and capacity building services; provide impact-limited capital and technical assistance to incentivize off-grid solar companies (OGS) to expand their operations in HTR markets, and to support the development of new business models and for new OGS to be established. Investee OGS companies can then provide more off-grid solar energy products, such as mini-grids or low-cost home customers via Pay-as-you-go mechanisms, providing many people with first-time access to modern energy.

HRR will be focused on learning how our interventions are able to drive impact at the household, enterprise and market level through a range of different measurement techniques, including feedback from customers (presented in the next section), as well as from other stakeholders. The initiative is designed to benefit all stakeholders. The knowledge of support will be supported through our partnerships with industry, government and utility private sector, creating the long-lasting impact of a demonstration effect and the blueprint for other areas to follow.

Hardest-to-Reach focuses on creating impact at the sector, enterprise and household levels in fledgling markets, and thus will seek to deliver a number of outputs by scaling the OGS markets in the participating countries.

## Sector-level: Advancement of the OGS section in H2R countries, and increasing provision of climate-resilient, clean energy across the energy sector of Program Host Nations through market creation, blended finance, partnerships

### Challenge
There is a significant investment gap in the sector due to the perception of the high risk and low return of underdeveloped OGS markets. Current OGS stakeholder efforts are fragmented, resulting in overlapping and uncoordinated efforts and missed opportunities for greater impact through collaboration. The use of


---
**Page 42**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 38

Blended finance to crowd in climate finance is constrained in part because the industry does not yet have a clearly defined narrative on how OGS products and services contribute to adaptation and resilience. In addition, the scale of many proposed initiatives in this space has been too small to attract commercial investors.

## Activities and Outputs

Acumen will establish the H2R blended finance structure to crowd in investors with different risk appetites. Acumen also will develop partnerships that convene different financials, TA providers, policy advocates, government, and OGS company investors in an effort to jumpstart the OGS market in HDR areas, support the creation of a favorable environment for other investors and businesses and share knowledge within the OGS sector. Specifically:

- **Activities 1.1 and 2.3:** comprise of fundraising and the establishment of the two funds that facilitates the investments, discussions and interest in the H2R regions.
- **Activity 1.3:** intends to drive partnership development designed to crowd in new investors, improve the level of knowledge sharing in the sector, coordinate the actions of various stakeholders, and make the initiative's impact sustainable.

Furthermore, the H2R initiative encourages more investment by improving the risk-profit return profile and track record of these markets. It does so by providing new market entrants with concessional finance and targeted technical assistance, and supporting the development of local networks and capacity. It intends to also increase the capacity of local distributors to become reliable partners to suppliers expanding in the region, and spur inclusive market development with a range of actors.

## Outcome

H2R markets are developed and scaled with greater engagement towards an improved enabling environment and market conditions. Markets are seen to be viable, with the right approach, preceding incentives and conditions to sustainably attract OGS companies, local participants and investors (philanthropic, development and private capital). The building blocks for OGS market advancement - such as the purchase of solar modules, installation of off-grid solar manufacturers and local distributors, consumer awareness efforts, policy engagement, access to finance and risk mitigation, and consumer subsidies, where needed - are developed in H2R countries.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 39

This leads to increased capital inflows (philanthropic, development, private) and to a better coordinated OGS sector in HSR geographies through blended finance solutions. Investment in the OGS sector will likely lead to clean, climate-resilient energy access for vulnerable people and increased installed capacity across the energy sectors of Program Host Nations.

The impact of OGS on climate change (mitigation and adaptation), and demonstration effect of the intervention, including replicable aspects, will also be better established through the generation and sharing of expert insights and empirical evidence.

## Enterprise-level: Fostering the sustainable and inclusive growth of OGS businesses

### Challenge
OGS companies struggle to serve the poorest and most vulnerable in HSR areas due mainly to the lack of flexible funding and the concentration of investment in a small number of large players, operating in a handful of well-understood markets. Companies also have limited capacity to serve riskier customers in new markets that lack distribution channels.

### Activities and Outputs
Acumen intends to manage the Hardest-to-Reach initiative combining two facilities with $250M under management; $50 million of market support capital is available via the philanthropically-backed Market Support Facility for HSR. The initiative aims at increasing the management of distressed assets as well as increasing awareness of OGS products. In addition, the Market Expansion Facility provides companies with $300 million of impact-indexed loans, providing targeted concessional capital. This impact-linked capital is designed to help companies retain a focus on entry-level solar products for lower-income customers and prevent mission-drift.

The provision of TA alongside investment aims to also help companies develop capacity in the areas of consumer protection, business development, gender and ESG. For Activity 1.3, 2.3 and 1.5, HSR emphasizes the support (financial and via TA) of enterprises at different stages, in particular to ensure sustainability and impact of the investments.

### Outcomes


---
**Page 44**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 40

As a result of the market development activities, H2R countries benefit from the sustainable and inclusive growth of OGS businesses, able to reach and impact the Hardest-to-Reach. The H2R initiative will seek to help new companies to enter the OGS market, help fledgling companies to grow to critical mass to be able to survive and continue to grow, and finance existing companies to scale up sales of OGS systems.

Demonstration and validation of climate-positive business models in these challenging contexts, through the support of the mechanisms within the Market Support Facility, allows for replication and scale of impact achieved through the Market Expansion Facility. Strengthened local management capacity and distribution networks will be developed with a focus on women entrepreneurs, while increased awareness of OGS solutions and positive customer experiences serve to build trust and stimulate demand. OGS solutions become more widespread, affordable, and cost effective.

In addition, jobs are created and sustained in the clean energy economy, with a significant portion of jobs being a source of employment for women.

**Household level:** Delivering 13.3M sales of off-grid solar projects and increasing the resilience and adaptive capacity of 54.3M people in Hardest-to-Reach areas and avoidance of 5.1M tCO2.

### Challenge

In H2R areas, many people are climate-vulnerable because they are located in areas that are highly exposed to the impacts of climate change and their sources of income are tied to activities which are weather-dependent, such as agriculture, while access to basic energy services is both costly and time-consuming. Furthermore, irregular and low incomes can be intermittent and they typically lack access to credit on reasonable terms.

Furthermore, these people are often living in unsupervised areas with no grid connection. As a result of their low asset bases, insufficient income and lack of supporting infrastructure, people in H2R areas may be unable to adapt to the effects of climate change. The result is that many people living in H2R areas have to rely on fossil fuels to power their day-to-day activities, such as light, charging and cooking. Fossil fuels such as kerosene are highly polluting, releasing large amounts of carbon emitted, and are notoriously expensive and unreliable. They are also made more vulnerable by not having access assured to communication tools to keep abreast of weather, health and market information.


---
**Page 45**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

Finally, women and children are likely to be disproportionately affected as they often spend more time at home with the harmful and emissions-intensive fossil fuel source which can lead to respiratory illnesses, or with candles which can lead to fires and burns.

## Activities and Outputs

All fund activities contribute to saving impact at the household level. Critically, products and services supplied through HGR investments intend to support households resilience/adaptation (see Box 1). Amman incentivizes and facilitates OGS companies to set-up, and market and sell solar energy products via PAYG financing to low-income people in HGR areas. This means that unelectified people are able to access clean energy pathways through the SHS model, which is a pay-as-you-go financing and installment payment plans. Furthermore, after a payment period of 12-36 months, households should own their SHS outright for the remaining lifetime of 24-48 months. Companies are expected to monitor impact by collecting monthly disaggregated sales data as well as representative sampling on product accessibility, use and benefits.

## Outcomes

Clean energy pathways are created for 72.4M people in HGR areas. This leads to increased resilience and adaptive capacity of the 54 M beneficiaries accessing energy for the first time, including through improved energy security, income diversification and access to climate information. Data shows that a portion of customers will be able to use their solar home systems (SHS) as an early warning system against extreme weather events such as droughts and floods, and also as a means of communication in case of climate change . Beneficiaries will also be able to readily access climate-related information through the use of ICTs such as mobile phones and radios, allowing them to prepare for upcoming weather events. Furthermore, some people should benefit from a safer home and better health due to reduced use of lighting sources that can cause fires and burns or are harmful to respiratory health (see box 2).

OGS use can also lead to reduced vulnerability in the context of climate change via a less climate-vulnerable energy supply. OGS is less vulnerable to weather events, grid failures and energy sources because it does not rely on fossil fuels or diesel generators. In the event of displacement due to a climate-related natural disaster, a person and their household are more able to retain access to electricity (with the benefit of being clean and safe). Furthermore, OGS should take less time to repair or replace, meaning that people with SHS are able to access energy more quickly following a weather event.

Finally, displacement of emissions-intensive lighting sources is intended to lead to 5.1M tCO2 being avoided.

## Introduction to Hard-to-Reach

Hard-to-Reach (H2R) is an energy access initiative dedicated to reaching more than 70M of the 286M people who live in H2R countries without electricity. Using benchmarks from the GOGGLA Market Trends Report 2022, approximately 190M people in H2R countries could afford an entry-level off-grid solar connection, but are at risk of being left behind by business-as-usual market approaches to electrification.

[GOGLLA Market Trends Report (2022)](State of the Sector (2022))


---
**Page 49**
---

B

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 45

HRR aims to unlock first-time clean and resilient energy access for these communities. These are challenging markets with electrification rates of or below the sub-Saharan Africa average (48%), where investors have not been willing to enter to serve low-income segments. Our approach is to incentivize and accompany private sector actors in scaling up their investments into off-grid solar solutions, by leveraging segments by mobilizing capital at scale from various investor bases in off-grid solar. This approach is based on Acumen's deep knowledge of the energy access challenge, relationships with players in the off-grid space, and a proven track record of successfully scaling up private sector investment to achieve impact.

HRR will target 16 sub-Saharan African countries with electrification rates of 48% or below and high poverty levels (above 50%). Out of this total, all eligible countries are considered least developed countries and they hold around 54 million African households that do not have access to modern electricity services. On average, 78% of the population living in these geographies lives below the lower-middle-income poverty line.

All 16 HRR countries are eligible countries for Acumen's H2R program as part of H2R preparation. In depth country studies have been conducted across a number of H2R countries. In the first window of the Market Development Facility (MDF), Acumen has worked with governments, development partners and guided the development of investment approaches and ensured policy alignment. Ongoing research and engagement in these countries will help to ensure that HRR achieves complementarity and coherence with other initiatives.

At Acumen our approach is always to use the right kind of capital to tackle the right kind of problem. Hard-to-Reach expects to use a diversified approach to inject capital and technical assistance to match the needs of original investors, as well as to support new entrants in these challenging H2R geographies. "Start-up to scale-up" or "whole-of-life cycle" financing is needed to support entrepreneurs to set up in these markets and then scale up their operations, and thereby achieve their impact potential. This will be achieved through a range of financial mechanisms, tailored to the different stages of operators' evolution and the different risk appetites of investors.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Component 1: Sustainable Market Creation in H2R countries (Market Support Facility)

A philanthropically-backed Market Support Facility aims to support the development of local players and regional companies to enter these new markets. This $50 million facility is expected to support companies with the flexibility to set up operations through grants, equity and debt, and the technical assistance needed to navigate unfamiliar territory of high risk countries. The MSF is specifically designed to provide the right type of funding and technical assistance for each company.

## Component 2: Sustainable Market Expansion in H2R countries (Market Expansion Facility)

A subsequent blended finance-backed $200 million Market Expansion Facility aims to provide impact-linked concessionary loans to scale up companies' operations and settle in H2R countries. A blended finance structure is used with the primary instrument expected to be impact-linked loans, an innovative financing tool that links interest rates to specific performance metrics related to poverty reduction terms of first-time energy access and poverty level of the customers. The interest rates should range between a market rate and a zero percent, depending on the impact performance. The impact-linked incentives are designed in such a way as to ensure that the facility is only used for delivering specific impacts and minimize the length of concessionality to the required level for delivering specific impacts. The objective of this approach is to provide companies with an incentive to deliver specific impacts at scale through KPIs, which will likely allow us to adapt, strengthen and maximize impacts along the way. Certain KPIs will also be used to ensure companies manage their growth sustainably and inclusively, disintermediating sales growth at the cost of portfolio quality, for example.

MEF also seeks to invest in accounts receivable facilities, which should allow OGS companies to finance themselves rather than rely on third party investors, making them self-sustaining. Fundraising for the MEF is ongoing with a target of $20 million and we are currently working closely with our partners to raise this amount. Our intention is to deploy loans only to the borrowers for operations in NOL countries. Assuming there are no exclusions, our intention is to have a single set of criteria when selecting borrowers, and that all investors will make their own independent decisions on whether or not they wish to participate as lenders. MEF investors will participate in each financing transaction entered into by MEF pro rata to their commitments to MEF.

See sections C.1 and C.2 for a summary of MSF-MEF breakdown by financing source and component.

## Component I: Intended Activities:

### Activity 1.1: Acumen engages stakeholders (partners, donors, govt) towards development of H2R markets, by fundraising & establishing HSR MSF ($50M)

Acumen intends to set up the philanthropically-backed Market Support Facility (MSF). We seek to develop and manage the strategy, including grantmaking, for the MSF. The MSF will be a vehicle for implementing investments in excess $50M for MSF across several donors, including a $10M repayable grant from GCF. Acumen engages relevant stakeholders (partners, donors, government) throughout. This includes engaging with potential partners and donors to identify investment opportunities; working with government entities and ministries, town halls with project affected communities, and other engagement strategies with relevant public and private sector organizations.

In relation to the overall management of MSF, the MSF will have a sole member, the AE. The MSF will set up a management committee to oversee the activities and progress of the MSF, currently contemplated to be the CEO of the AE. The AE is expected to be an independent director who will be responsible for making grant and investment decisions, currently contemplated to be the President and CFO of the AE; together with a Board member of the AE. The MSF team is expected to consist of a director of investment, a director of operations, and a director of finance. The MSF may also have an independent director who will limit the term of the LLC or have it necessarily be required to unwind at the end of the program. Depending on the specific terms and conditions of the vision for the H2R project, the entity may have continued utility, which is hard to predict at this time. However, we may choose to liquidate the entity if there is no further need for the entity at such time.

**H2R aims to develop partnerships and engage with stakeholders (government, investors, opcos, capacity building programs) to improve coordination, address market barriers and share knowledge in H2R markets. Hard-to-Reach seeks to work with and invest in established off-grid solar operators – manufacturers, distributors, and service providers – that have a strong track record of providing reliable services at scale and their reach to locally-rooted value chains that will serve these populations over the long term. By encouraging partnerships between established off-grid energy players and local distributors, H2R aims to build robust and competitive markets in underserved areas.**

In addition to developing new partnerships, H2R also seeks to leverage existing relationships with stakeholders through knowledge sharing initiatives. Through these partnerships, coordinated knowledge sharing may help address market barriers to investment. We aspire to generate a high-level REACH Partnership convening at the country level coordination committees to facilitate knowledge sharing and efficiently use resources.

**Sub-Activity 1.2.1: Acumen seeks to support the development of a high level REACH Partnership between OGS leaders, practitioners, and stakeholders committed to sharing knowledge and insights.**

o THESE REACH partnership convening will likely provide the team the opportunity to align with other initiatives, such as the Global Solar Council (GSC) and the International Solar Alliance (ISA), among others. The aspiration is that by sharing insights from our investment activity with stakeholders across the solar sector, we can help generate an enabling environment for others to participate in impactful ways in OGS sectors in H2R markets.

Hardest-to-Reach MSF will monitor and evaluate investments and follow them closely throughout the Mesp of the loan, equity investment, or joint venture. ### Outcomes of Component 1 and 2

#### Outcome 1: Advancement of off-grid solar markets in H2R countries
H2R intends to extend financing and technical assistance to OGS companies in sub-Saharan Africa, who will then sell their products directly to customers. Many of them will be accessing energy for the first time.
- By generating investment in Hardest-to-Reach OGS markets, investing in a diverse portfolio of companies, creating new OGS customer bases, deploying international technical assistance, knowledge sharing with partners and other stakeholders, H2R aims at increasing access to off-grid solar power and enabling sustainable expand Hardest-to-Reach OGS markets, leading to more OGS companies and increased competition.
- H2R will also provide a platform for the exchange of best practices between investors and off-grid experts to research market development in H2R markets, and determine the market growth that can be attributed to H2R. We will define the metrics to activate and expand markets and measure our contribution to the market activation and expansion.
- We will also identify and engage with potential partners and other stakeholders to activate and support the development of these H2R OGS markets. By defining these market activating “topping” activities, we hope that by 2017, we can have an impact; we hope to create a blueprint for other impact investors seeking to make market level changes.

#### Indicative Metric: Hardest-to-Reach activates 9 tipping points across 3 H2R countries

## Outcome 2: Establishment and Growth of OGS companies in sustainable and gender- poverty inclusive markets

HBR seeds new ventures and backs existing players in challenging markets, supporting the establishment and growth of these nascent ventures and markets through partnerships and productive engagements with government agencies, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and other stakeholders. HBR also facilitates connections and certification through clean energy technologies. By supporting stronger and more viable businesses, creating a local investment history, and incentivizing impact, we hope to create sustainable and impactful solutions for the most vulnerable communities.

We seek to demonstrate that OGS companies operating in Hard-to-Reach markets can build climate resilient, sustainable, and inclusive businesses with technical assistance. HBR companies will receive technical assistance from ESG and climate TA, including market analysis and enterprise development support. Consumer protection activities focus on enhancing consumer protection policies, procedures, and sales strategies for companies, and monitoring portfolio quality to manage credit risk and consumer protections.

On gender equality, OGS companies will be supported in developing gender action plans, improving gender policies, and collecting sex-disaggregated data. Additionally, technical assistance funds will be allocated to support companies in building climate resilience, enhancing e-waste policies and procedures, and raising female customer awareness of OGS products. ESG and climate TA focuses on ESG action plans for companies, support companies in enhancing e-waste policies and procedures, and building climate and ESG knowledge.

## Outcome 3: OGS customers (and their households) benefit from enhanced energy security

OGS products reduce the vulnerability of people's energy supply to climate change. OGS is less vulnerable to climate-change-related weather phenomena than grid-connected energy sources because they are not dependent on a single source or infrastructure that can be damaged, destroyed, or replaced, meaning that people with SHS will be able to access energy more quickly following a weather event.

# MSF Structure

- **MSF** is intended to raise grant and repayable grant capital to build capacity for entities to establish and bolster a market for clean energy access for individuals in least developed countries. Target size is $50 million of which a majority has already been raised and for which several pilots are in planning.
  - MSF will request to provide funding in the form of a partially repayable grant. MSF’s repayable grant shall be used for all activities of the MSF, including grants and investments; such GCF funding toward grants and investments is hereinafter referred to as “GCF Investment Proceeds”.
- **MSF** is expected to provide technical assistance grants and potentially recoverable grants/convertible notes/equity, engage in joint ventures and provide impact indexed loans to incentivize established solar home systems manufacturers and distributors to enter LDC markets, and to bolster existing distributors in LDCL.
  - Any joint ventures are expected to contain mechanisms for ownership to transfer to the counterparty once the ventures are financially self-sustaining.

Joint venture agreements will incorporate grants to cover set up and other initial costs.

The operations of MSF are overseen by a management committee consisting of the Chief Strategic Initiatives, Chief Financial Officer and General Counsel of the AE. The management committee seeks to ensure that the operations of the MSF are compliant with GCF requirements, applicable laws, regulations and any other commitments made by MSF are subject to review and approval of the global Investment Committee of the AE.

- Members of the AE will operationalize the MSF include the General Counsel, the Chief Strategic Officer, the Chief Financial Officer, a Director, an Associate Director, and the Legal Counsel of the AE.
  - **MSF** is expected to build capacity within entities to be a viable pipeline for MEF borrowers. MSF will take the lead during the initial stages of engagement with these potential borrowers with its high risk grant and repayable grant capital, reducing the risk for MEF.

- **MSF** expects to have an investment period of 7 years.


---
**Page 65**
---

MEF Structure

- MEF is intended to bring together senior, mezzanine and junior equity capital to incentivize investors with different risk profiles to provide clean energy access for individuals living off-grid in the least developed countries.
- MEF also intends to raise grant capital to enable further interest rate adjustments payable by borrowers as a reward for first-time energy access, among other metrics, which will be collected and analyzed annually (using 3rd party verification of certain metrics).
- MEF is designed to make loans for working capital to borrowers providing clean energy access in HCR countries with incentives to borrowers to enable first time clean energy access for their customers.
- MEF is expected to use performance incentives within the loan structure to incentivize businesses to achieve specific impact targets, in the form of interest rate adjustments to the extent of achievement of such metrics.
- Borrowers may receive impairment adjustments based on certain predetermined metrics. Such metrics will be collected and analyzed annually (using 3rd party verification of certain metrics, such as lean data studies), and result in an impact index score that will be aligned with an interest adjustment.
- In addition to impact adjusted loans, the MEF expects to lend to accounts receivable financing facilities, to support cash and inventory management of OGS companies operating in our target geography. Current average loan term is 1 year to 2 years.
- Target MEF size = $300 million, with indicative allocation across layers as follows: senior - $60 million; mezzanine - $60 million; junior - $60 million and grant - $20 million. This proposal requests GCF to provide $50 million for junior equity and $5 million for non-repayable grant capital.
- The above structure is designed to incentivize capital with different risk tolerance, noting that mission-oriented and concessional capital is expected to provide the mezzanine, junior and grant capital while more commercially oriented capital is expected to provide senior capital.
- MEF is expected to have a 7 year investment period which may be extended for up to two one-year periods
- Investment decisions for MEF will be made by an investment committee designated by the GP
- MSF is expected to provide pipeline borrowers to MEF. To mitigate conflicts of interest that may be perceived to bias MEF decisionmakers, 1) underwriting criteria has been developed to vet the viability of


---
**Page 66**
---

The prospective borrowers, and 2) the investment committee and LPAC will be apprised of borrowers that have also received MSF funding for transparency and the opportunity to discuss any concerns.

- The non-reimbursable grant will flow from GCF to the AE via the FAA entered into between the AE and GCF. The money in turn will flow to the MEF via a subsidiary agreement to be entered into between the MEF and the AE. No additional money will flow to the borrowers; they benefit by virtue of an interest rate adjustment, say less to the MEF. The MEF retains the grant capital and is able to use it for operating purposes to the extent of the adjustment offset.

## Track Record

Acumen is an AE that has three funding proposals in implementation. The first program is KawiSafi Ventures, a venture capital fund focused on creating clean energy access in East Africa, and is at the end of its investment period, having successfully deployed $10 million to 24 companies by the end of Q3 2022; impacted 110 million low-income individuals and offset 24 million metric tons of carbon dioxide and kerosene. The second program is Acumen resilient Agriculture Fund, a venture capital fund focused on providing concessional capital to smallholder farmers in East Africa. The fund is at the middle of its investment period, having successfully deployed $50.2 million in investments as at end of Q3 2022; impacted approximately 148,000 low-income individuals and offset 679 metric tons of carbon dioxide. The third program is Acumen Solar Energy Fund, a debt fund focused on providing concessional capital to support businesses in the solar home system sector to survive the impacts of COVID- and is in the middle of its investment period, having successfully deployed $10 million as at end of Q3 2022; impacted approximately 548,000 low-income individuals.

Acumen has a strong track record investing in small and medium-sized enterprises that serve low-income communities in developing countries. Acumen’s investments have created over 679,000 jobs and generated $1 billion worth of goods or services for the world tackling poverty. As at the end of 2021, Acumen had deployed approximately $148 million into 151 companies, impacting approximately 443 million lives. A significant sector focus for Acumen has been the off-grid solar sector and Acumen contributes to many sectoral initiatives, including with GOGLA.

Manager: as executing entity, is expected to be by a wholly owned subsidiary of Acumen. ### Why GCF needs this project/programme

- **Addressing the unelectified populations requires affordable solutions, which themselves require adequate, abundant capital.**
    - Perceived risk and small size of these markets means that capital there is typically directed to higher-risk projects, anchored by the most influential concessional investor focused on climate change adaptation (GCF) as the only way to overcome this impasse and bring energy to people who are vulnerable to climate change.
    
### GCF's investment in this project is critical

- GCF’s concessional capital will play a catalytic role to mobilize capital at scale and channel it toward serving low-income and climate-vulnerable customers in LDCs. Public and private capital directed to the energy access sector has been concentrated in a few lower-risk markets, such as India, China, Brazil, and South Africa. In 2019, western, central and southern Africa accounted for 71% of SSA’s unelectified population, yet received only 35% of concessional investment. Similarly, while 84% of GCF’s energy access investment dollars are directed to low-income regions, they are not always directed where the need is greatest.
    - Most investors only operate in markets that are later mature or proven to yield financial returns. For this reason, progress has been confined to a few investor-major countries.

- The affordability of clean energy transitions will depend on reducing the cost and improving the availability of capital. Addressing the remaining unelectified population demands a 12

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 44

## Shift towards more challenging market environments and thus keeping financing costs low will be vital to enabling clean energy markets transitions while keeping them inclusive and affordable.

### However, capital is substantially more costly in emerging and developing economies than in advanced economies. Nominal financing costs are up to seven times higher than in the United States and Europe, with higher levels of riskier scenarios. This points to a relatively high bar for projects to raise debt finance and offer sufficient returns on equity.

#### OGS companies have therefore not been able to expand into these riskier markets, because their cost of financing is too high; financing doesn't exist, or they don't have the capacity to expand into nascent markets. Sales of off-grid solar products are very low or non-existent in many of these geographies, even though there is a significant need for energy access and a large number of people living in underserved communities.

##### For example, OGS sales in Burundi over the last 3 years were less than 0.1 percent of total SSA sales in that country. In contrast, OGS sales in Ethiopia over the same period represented more than 45 percent of all sales in that country. The average across SSA markets is approximately 27 percent. This means that the cost of financing for OGS products in Burundi over the last 3 years was 16 percent of the total SSA sales, despite the country making up over 8 percent of the population.

#### The GCF will enable H2R initiatives to channel much needed financing and technical assistance to OGS companies to support them to enter riskier markets and grow sustainably. The concessional component provided by the GCF will allow OGS companies to access affordable capital for both short-term and longer tenors. Companies will therefore be able to access affordable capital and should not suffer from over-capitalization, which would make it too costly to expand into these markets in the form of cheaper products.

##### Without a concessional component, OGS companies would have to charge potentially prohibitively high prices to their customers, many of whom will be earning below $3.20 a day. If this were the case, then the H2R initiative would not be able to have an impact on the people who need energy access most.

## GCF's financing package

### H2R is creating a paradigm shift in African energy markets by unlocking finance from investors that would otherwise find these markets too risky. GCF's concessional capital will be vital in providing companies with concessional financing and mobilizing different types of investors with different risk appetites at scale to tackle the challenge of scaling up clean energy access.

With GCF's investment, however, Acumen will be able to shift the paradigm and develop a new frontier for energy investment by creating new markets that have the potential to bring through the lifeline of the initiative.

### What GCF brings to H2R

The GCF is the world's largest dedicated climate fund, and as such brings significant credibility to the Hardest-to-Reach initiative. In addition to the direct investment into the initiative, GCF's involvement will have the following benefits:

- Provide a framework for mobilizing support from relevant governments, with nationally designated authorities on each project for continued engagement and alignment.
- Mobilize investors by enhancing both the risk profile and reputation of the H2R initiative
- Enhance stakeholder engagement with the initiative through its dedication to country ownership
- Strengthen the climate rationale of the initiative through its robust investment criteria and integrated results management framework
- Have strong signaling effects to the market of the importance of energy access in increasing the adaptive capacity of beneficiaries

There are a number of other funds and facilities that invest in energy access in SSA, such as the Energy Access Fund, Access to Clean Power Fund, Facility for Energy Inclusion Off Grid Energy Access Fund and Africa Go Green Fund. However, none of these funds have a specific objective to support off-grid solar energy companies' expansion into niche, harder-to-reach markets with low electrification rates. Hansiko and Reach plugs that funding gap by delivering capital to the markets most often overlooked by investors.

HGR is also unique in its five-stage approach. Through the Market Support Facility, HGR can provide a flexible range of financial products to support market development. The HGR Expansion Facility provides grants for companies to cover the start-up costs of expanding into a new market, which is a differentiating feature from the Market Expansion Facility. HGR aims to provide a more sustainable business model that incentivizes a focus on sales in last-mile markets and access to credit. This represents HGR's approach to addressing the challenges particularly high-risk. HGR intends to furthermore provide TA support to companies as needed through both facilities in order to build capacity and strengthen markets for the long term.

By investing in recessed off-grid markets with few if no examples of off-grid solar players operating at scale, the Hard-to-Reach initiative seeks to be a pioneer in untested markets, making operating companies more attractive to other investors and more sustainable beyond the life of the initiative. HGR will seek to achieve this by providing concessional capital, technical assistance, market access, and investment, demonstrating the viability of these markets and replicability of the investment approach, and ensuring the long-term interest of beneficiaries, companies, investors, and the public sector.

### Sustainability

The initiative intends to provide investees with targeted accompaniment in the areas of consumer protection, enterprise development, and ESG. Technical assistance supports capacity building, can act as a catalyst for market growth, and should be provided on an ongoing basis to ensure sustainable responsible growth over the long term. Additionally, in order to develop a sustainable off-grid solar market in Kenya, HGR will support companies to establish local distribution networks. HGR aims to source and invest in eligible local entities, with the objective being to ensure that local businesses also receive the concessional capital and the technical assistance needed to operate in high-risk markets. **Replicability**

The proposed approach has replicability in-built. Two separate financing facilities are proposed with different investment risk profiles, and will invest with different capital blends to match the needs of the company at each stage of its development. The Market Expansion Facility (MEF) deploys impact equity/like capital, and grants using a patient capital approach that is consistent with Acumen’s core investment principles. The other facility, the Market Development Facility (MDF), will deploy debt or equity/like capital. The Market Expansion Facility (MEF) deploys impact linked debt that aim to help responsibly expand the reach of investments in the long-term. As H2R will share lessons learned and insights widely, successful models can be copied by other investors. For example, other investors can apply impact-indexed loans to bridge the “last mile” or “financing gap” - the stage of enterprise development where businesses face real challenges raising capital to validate and prepare their business models for growth and build out the systems and infrastructure that are expected to enable them to scale effectively and responsibly.
