If the project/programme is expected to support capacity building and technology development/transfer, please provide a brief description of these activities and quantify the total requested GCF funding amount for these activities, to the extent possible. N/A

The H2R intervention seeks to provide capacity building activities in the form of technical assistance to companies and intermediaries developing or seeking to shift off-grid solar markets in Host Nations. The Technology Assistance Facility (TAF) is embedded within the H2R Support Facility. By integrating capacity building into the initiative through the TAF, H2R aims to develop a more robust and sustainable market for off-grid solar products and services suitable on H2R markets. As a result, these markets should become stronger and more competitive, paving the way for other GGS businesses to thrive even beyond the life of the initiative.

TA will be provided for four main areas: Consumer Protection; Enterprise Development; ESG; and Gender. The intervention seeks to provide technical assistance to companies in addressing challenges, risks and barriers of delivering energy solutions to underserved segments; to grow sustainably and inclusively with an eye on protecting consumers; to access capacity building for mitigating business sustainability, particularly for locally-owned and managed companies; to identify and mitigate gender-related barriers that women face as users, entrepreneurs and members of the workforce. H2R aims also to support the development of off-grid solar markets with a gender equity lens by providing training for female groups on technical skills, financial literacy, leadership, entrepreneurship, and business leaders, entrepreneurs, valued workforce and consumers. Similarly, H2R aims to strengthen ESG

### Low-Emission Sustainable Development Pathways

The economies and populations of sub-Saharan Africa are growing rapidly and while they account for less than 4 percent of cumulative global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions to date, by 2050 a quarter of the global population will be African. Given this demographic shift, coupled with the need for countries to adapt to an increasingly warming planet, it is critical that all countries transition to low carbon-intensity and affordable modern energy and, even where energy consumption is currently low, embark on clean energy development pathways. The rapid growth in demand for energy in Africa will have profound impacts in the medium and long term. Data shows that developing economies


---
**Page 83**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 79

(excluding those now account for roughly 40 percent of carbon emissions,** and will propel almost all the growth going forward)

Sub-Saharan Africa produces, on average, only 0.8 tonnes of CO2eq per person per year—compared to a global average of 4.8 tonnes.*** However, highly developed and coal-dependent South Africa (85 percent electricity from coal in 2019) is the largest emitter per capita in sub-Saharan Africa, followed by countries of concern for large nations such as Mozambique (29% electrical), DRC (19%), and Uganda (2%)—and to some extent, Botswana. These are all countries with electrification rates below the regional average of just over 40%. The region’s total emissions are estimated at around 350 million tonnes of CO2eq per year. A recent modeling illustrates Africa's low-carbon SDG 7 targets and their correlation to SDG 13 as the region electrifies, with a mix of renewable energy sources and fossil fuels. This would result in an additional 486 million tonnes of CO2eq emissions, with a technology mix that is 45 percent decarbonized renewables.*** If African emissions per person were to rise even to the current global average, the continent would emit 12.5 million tonnes of greenhouse gases per year by 2030—roughly equivalent to all emissions from China and India today.

The H2R initiative seeks to connect Africa to SSA’s energy transition towards sustainable development goals. By providing access to clean energy through solar energy, other renewable sources, such as hydro systems, customers gain trust and familiarity with the technology and payment models and opt to use similar technologies in the future, even as their energy consumption grows. This aligns with the idea of low-carbon development pathways that are inclusive and sustainable.

    - Impact Potential (iv): 1,920 jobs (6,841 direct)
        - **Follow-on investments:** 13,922 jobs (6,841 direct)

## Impact Potential (v): anticipatory, absorptive and adaptive resilience for households via OGS characterization (see below)

### Energy System Resilience through decentralized solar

Access to electricity is considered a key part of adaptive capacity: one of the three principal factors which determine climate change vulnerability in a region, along with sensitivity and exposure to hazards and impacts. Adaptive capacity, the characteristic which allows people to cope with these hazards and impacts, includes access to information and communication technologies (ICTs), social networks, and financial services. Anticipatory capacity, the characteristic which allows people to plan for and invest and/or change behavior and habits in advance of events, includes disaster preparedness, including access and the ability to use ICTs, and early warning systems. Absorptive capacity is the ability of a system to absorb these, as it plays a key role in enabling all these components. As such it plays a significant role in making livelihoods less vulnerable in HLR target countries.

Limited power access in turn limits economic activity which rely on agriculture for a number of reasons: First and foremost, limited access power means the inability to reliably access information and to communicate. This is a crucial weakness during natural disasters, which are expected to continue to increase in frequency and intensity due to climate change. Second, limited access to electricity can also limit access can contribute to increasing household income by increasing working hours through access to light and increasing opportunities for home based business, while direct access to power reduces the cost and time associated with using kerosene or solar photo voltaic panels for lighting a long term fuel.

Energy systems have been compromised both by exogenous and endogenet climatic dimensions, with resulting economic losses, disruptions of services and impacts to wellbeing. The threat to the reliable provision of energy is exacerbated by the centralization of power generation and where distribution is interconnected, including in Africa. This has led to a number of countries experiencing significant disruption to their energy systems facing the strain of climate change. For example, following low rainfall and droughts in 2015-16 in southern Africa, power generation reduced by 75 percent at the Kariba dam in Zambia, and by 85 per cent at the Cahora Bassa Dam in Mozambique. This led to a significant reduction of electricity supply across neighboring countries as a result. As Malawi, Zambia and Zimbabwe all experienced droughts during this period, there was also a significant impact on their agricultural sectors, national economy and reflected in reduced GDP growth. Furthermore, in 2014 Cyclone Idai caused significant damages to Mozambique's energy sector, and resulted in an estimated loss of $47.9 million. The cyclone damaged or destroyed 35 substations, 68 transformers, 100 kilometers of transmission lines, 10 power generation plants, 30 sub-stations, 4,400 transformers. Similarly, Malawi lost 30% of its electricity to Tropical Storm Ana in February 2002 because of damage to the 130 MW Kapchira hydropower plant. The cyclone also damaged or destroyed 5 substations and 6 transformers. In Zimbabwe, Cyclone Idai in May 2023, leaving the country at 2/3 of its energy generation capacity for over a year. # Demonstrate the ways in which traditional utility scale energy systems are vulnerable to the effects of climate change

The IPCC has therefore recognised the role that decentralized renewable energy can play in increasing the resilience of energy systems, as it helps to diversify energy supplies and reduce pressure on the grid. Decentralized energy also has a role in reducing the vulnerability of rural populations to climate change. In fact, decentralized renewable energy systems are often more resilient than centralized systems because they are less vulnerable to natural disaster-induced electricity disruptions by households in climate-vulnerable areas. The research points in particular to the attributes that make decentralized renewable energy easier to be rebuilt (renewable energy sources are usually available locally and can be installed in a short period). By increasing the distribution of energy solutions and adopting technical improvements to reduce systems' climate vulnerability, we are able to see more adapted and resilient energy systems in HDR countries.

## Anticipation, absorptive and adaptive capacity for households through characteristics of OGGS

Energy access impacts the ability to anticipate, avoid, plan for, cope with, recover from and adapt to (climate) change-related shocks and stresses. These capacities can be improved by increasing the resilience of energy provision in the face of natural disasters and climate shocks, which are increasingly frequent and severe. In these cases, OGGS can continue to provide a household with energy even if the grid has been compromised because of increased demand or damage caused by an extreme weather event. Recent data from the World Bank shows that access to electricity is a key factor in improving the quality of life and the potential social and economic development of access to electricity. Off-grid solar can play an important role in improving the quality of electricity access here as well as deliver significant climate mitigation and adaptation benefits. Renewable energy systems are generally seen to provide greater gains than fossil fuels in terms of CO2 reductions.

### ARIA 2: Health, wellbeing, food and water security

Improved health, wellbeing and quality of life through energy access

In least developed country contexts, in particular, energy access is an enabler for poverty reduction that is crucial for enhancing people’s livelihoods and wellbeing. According to the IPCC and research by the UK’s Foreign, Commonwealth & Development Office (formerly DFID) there is growing evidence of the role of private sector investment in providing clean energy solutions to households and businesses, enabling greater private land and assets, less hazardous household lighting, such as via open flames, and cleaner air in the absence of black carbon emitted from kerosene lanterns. Energy access can also reduce the impact of climate change on people’s lives by improving their ability to cope with extreme weather events. For example, energy access enables early warning messages or public safety announcements before and after a climate event. Thus, enabling access to electricity is not only an enabler for poverty reduction but also for disaster risk reduction. Energy access can enable specific applications such as information communication technologies, and by driving socio-economic development (i.e., improving incomes, health outcomes and access to education), is highly impactful in reducing the vulnerability of people living in developing countries. According to a World Bank, a Lean Data survey of off-grid energy customers in developing economies, found that 51% of people felt that their quality of life had improved significantly after buying an off-grid product in general, compared to greater than 60% for those who bought solar home systems (SHS). The same survey also found that on this metric, where 60 Decibels surveys of PEI portfolio customers found that 64% of all off-grid energy customers felt their quality of life had improved significantly after buying an off-grid product in general would incorporate a number of different factors, it indicates how significant an impact energy access has on people’s lives. Finally, residential solar PV requires neither water nor additional land to generate electricity, meaning that it does not put pressure on land use and water systems, with broader positive impacts on food security and biodiversity.

Even though it can be concluded that the impact of energy access through SHS on livelihood resilience in SSA is conditional and limited, solar lamps and SHS are nevertheless an important milestone on the route to initial last-mile electrification in rural areas of sub-Saharan Africa. ### Paradigm Shift

The HDR initiative will deliver a paradigm shift by:
1. Nurturing and developing companies to serve underserved off-grid solar markets; and by;
2. Unlocking the provision of - and investment in - clean, climate-resilient decentralized solar, that will
3. Power low-carbon economic development for the long-term.

One of the key aims of Hardest-to-Reach is to transform the African energy sector by building (and eventually scaling up) off-grid markets where there currently are none or they are fledging. The initiative will seek to de-risk these markets through a combination of financial and technical assistance, with regional average, where investors have not been willing to enter. HDR's blended finance structure is transactional in nature, with the aim of providing concessional debt at 50% below market rates, or riskier. The initiative intends to de-risk these markets by providing new market entrants with impact-linked concessionary finance and targeted technical assistance, and seeks to support the development of local networks of companies that can provide a range of services to customers in these markets. HDR will crowd in additional capital to achieve scale in these markets. In the scale phase, HDR's impact-linked debt facility is designed to incentivise OGS companies to maintain their focus on low-income, first-time energy access customers.

A blend of capital is needed to achieve this. Data from Shell shows that approximately $11 billion will be required in debt into SHS enterprises towards SDG 7 in non-established markets. Further a further $2.8 billion of

The 16 target countries for Acumen’s Hard-to-Reach initiative signed the Paris Agreement and submitted Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC). All 16 countries have pledged both conditional and unconditional contributions to climate action, with varying levels of ambition depending on their development scenario. The unconditional targets are expected to be met by countries with their own resources while conditional targets require external support from the international community.

Two of the 16 countries have set their conditional mitigation targets at over 50% emission reduction by 2030, namely Malawi and Togo (at 51% and 11% respectively). The lowest conditional ambition is found in Uganda with a target to reduce emissions by 4.8% below business as usual.

#### Sustainable Development

Modern society depends on reliable and affordable energy services to function smoothly and to develop equitably. Carbon-intensive development patterns have historically relied on inexpensive and energy-dense fossil fuels, which are also the primary source of greenhouse gas emissions contributing to climate change. However, the transition towards a low-carbon economy is essential for sustainable development. Energy access through the power of energy access will enable broader sustainable development outcomes; good health (SDG 3), education (SDG 4), decent work and economic growth (SDG 8), affordable and clean energy generation (SDG 7) and action climate (SDG 13). Acumen’s intervention is also partnership-focused, envisioning a more coordinated investment ecosystem to transform the prospects for growth in the off-grid solar sector (consistent with SDG 17).

**Page 90**

The HDR model also has a number of broader sustainable development impacts. Firstly, off-grid solar products can improve people’s health, safety and wellbeing. For those trapped in energy poverty, particularly the rural households in HRP target countries, fossil-fuel based energy sources such as kerosene lamps are common. These lamps produce toxic smoke that is harmful to human health because of household pollutants, and can cause burns and fires. This disproportionately affects women and children who spend more time at home. Replacing fossil-fuel based energy products with off-grid solar products has the potential to improve people’s health, safety and security as customers will have access to better quality lighting123 which “can prevent falls at night, help empower women by allowing them to work longer hours in their homes, and reduce the risk of fire”124. Lanterns were once responsible for 70 percent of fire incidents and 80 percent of burn injuries in energy poverty households in India alone. The introduction of off-grid solar products has led to a wide range of success of off-grid solar technologies. There is still a task to do to ensure no household has to rely on these fuels for lighting. Meanwhile, where households have replaced kerosene lanterns with single use oil cell lamps, the number of fire incidents and injuries caused by these lamps has increased significantly125. Additionally, fuel combustion releases hundreds of pollutants into the air, including carbon monoxide, formaldehyde, benzene, toluene, acrylamide, and other volatile organic compounds which can enable people to generate income through their use126. This can be directly (use in a business) or indirectly (task-shifting activities to the evening because of better quality light). This can help increase people’s economic opportunities by enabling them to work longer hours and earn more money, as they will have wallets and credit for the first time, leading to the financial inclusion of low-income people and helping to build their capacity. The ability to use solar products in the evening also enables women to earn income because of land-based collateral requirements. Fourthly, the local distribution networks that will be created and maintained by service providers will be a source of livelihood opportunities and employment opportunities, as they could employ many people in the rural areas127. The increased income they receive is expected to be an important contributor to their increased resilience and adaptive capacity. Lastly, off-grid solar products can also provide access to education for children who live in remote areas; they will require additional human capital to support their growth and sustain their level of service. Jobs will be created for finance personnel, office and administrators, product sales staff, and technicians for installations and repair.

## Gender

**Gender Reach** intends to have significant benefits for women both by creating more opportunities for women to participate in the GIS industry and ensuring access to energy.

Previously, H2R expects to improve the health and safety of women and expects to also lead to their financial empowerment. The project will work with the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and UNDP to train women as women* will and aim to support women-led businesses. H2R also seeks to engage companies and the GIS industry in gender equality initiatives, including through a partnership with the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) and the World Wildlife Fund (WWF) and with the Project Technical Assistance Facility. Additionally, H2R intends to diligence and monitor Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEARH) across investments operations and engagement with companies and other stakeholders.

Investees are expected to address opportunities for growth in their Gender Action Plans, and with the support of H2R, will be encouraged to report on gender equality performance. The project will use disaggregated data for certain indicators, which improves industry-wide knowledge on how women engage with energy products and services, and allows for the greater consideration of gender throughout the GIS sector.

The H2R team developed a gender assessment in accordance with industry best practices, guidance and support from gender experts, community input, and following the GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment informed the Project’s Gender Action Plan and Gender Lens Investing Strategy.

These documents can be found in Annex 8.

The investment gap is at the root of both the energy access gap and the lack of adaptive capacity faced by countries in sub-Saharan Africa. Despite the tremendous progress made by off-grid solar companies in improving energy access in sub-Saharan Africa, a substantial number of financially viable customers in other regions have been underserved for years with market-based interventions. OGS companies were estimated to have cumulatively raised US$1.5 billion as of 2019 from an increasingly diverse investor base as the industry has matured and moved beyond its early days. The geographic bias in the flow of funding into the off-grid solar sector, between 2012-2019, has flowed to a small number of companies that are based outside Africa, with Kenya being one of the largest recipients of capital. Additionally, as OGS start-ups evolve and mature, they have increasingly focused on entrenching their market position and attracting higher-value customers in the most active markets at the expense of expanding into new markets with a higher actual and perceived risk.

As the two Figures show, the regional disparities in access to clean energy are large. The vast majority of people without access to electricity. Thus, it is imperative to direct more resources, financial and otherwise, to where they are needed the most.


The overall outlook of investments has been adversely affected by the pandemic, with foreign private investment falling some 18% in 2020. Significant financial commitments are required to close this gap and governments are unable to carry the fiscal burden alone. One estimate suggests that $35 billion is needed annually for adaptation through 2040, while another study shows that investments cumulatively show a need for an estimated $331 billion in investment for adaptation through 2030, with per capita spending on adaptation increasing from $8 to $67 by 2030. The private sector is increasingly going to developing economies in support of clean energy, particularly given the size of developing economy populations. Within sub-Saharan Africa a concentration of investment flows into now-proven market leaders, which are often based outside the region. This can be attributed to political instability, macroeconomic uncertainty (because of inflation and exchange rates), policy and regulatory issues, and the lack of infrastructure in many countries, as well as the need for adaptation to climate change. ## Benin Country Context

Benin is a low-income country, with a population of more than 13 million people and a population growth rate of 2.8%. The rural electrification rate in Benin is 59%, while the urban electrification rate stands at 73% which is lower than the national average of 84%. Only 60% of the population has access to electricity. This is disaggregated with 58% and 91% urban and rural electrification respectively. As the country’s economy grows, energy demand is anticipated to increase; thus, there is a need to match the energy supply to the growing energy demand.

## DRC Country Context

DRC is a low-income country, with a population of more than 96 million people and a population growth rate of 3.2% per year. According to the World Bank Group, poverty in DRC is high and stands at five percent above the Sub-Saharan African average of 48%, leaving 79 million people without access to electricity. 19% of the urban population has access to electricity compared with only 5% of rural areas. In 2021, DRC had an installed electricity capacity of 2.8 GW comprising 99% hydropower and 1% non-renewable sources. The national electricity grid powers 19% of the population living in urban areas but only 1% of those living in rural areas.

## Burundi Country Context

Burundi is a low-income country, with a population of more than 12.55 million people and a population growth rate of 2.7%. In 2019, Burundi’s national electrification rate stood at 11%, relative to the sub-Saharan African average of 48%, leaving 11 million people without access to electricity. There is a 66% concentration gap between urban and rural areas. The country has an installed capacity of 320 MW, which was sourced from 98% renewables: 14% oil, 1% coal and others; and 2%. Of the renewables, 98% is biomass and 2% hydropower. Hydropower alone provides electricity to 9% of the population, which translates to 75% of those with access to electricity.

**Zambia Country Context**

Zambia is a low-income country, with a population of more than 18 million people and a population growth rate of 2.5%. Zambia's primary sources of energy include wood fuel, hydropower, coal, and solar. In 2017, the national electrification rate was 37%, relative to the sub-Saharan African average of 46%, leaving 11 million people without access to electricity. The urban electrification rate in Zambia is 85% compared with an average of 93% for sub-Saharan Africa and a global average of 93%. In rural areas, the electrification rate was 6%. Zambia has an installed capacity of 3.3 GW, comprising 80% hydropower, 11% coal, 7% heavy fuel oil (HFO), and 2% solar.

**Gender Context**

The link between energy access and gender equality is compelling. Energy poverty has a strong gender dimension, as the lack of access to energy inhibits a woman's freedom from household drudgery, her freedom to allocate time, to access economic opportunities, and enjoy improved household living conditions. Women in rural areas are disproportionately affected by energy poverty in terms of reduced risks of pulmonary and respiratory diseases, caused by long-term proximity to fumes from fuel combustion. Access to clean cooking solutions such as biogas or solar cookers can help accelerate the transition to a low-carbon economy by promoting clean energy use and addressing energy poverty, while also advancing women's economic empowerment. However, multiple barriers limit women's access to energy services in rural areas, including lack of awareness about available resources, limited collection and use of off-disaggregated data to better understand the needs of women as energy consumers, and limited opportunities for women to enter the workforce and thrive in the clean energy economy.

Overall, evidence shows that women have less access to off-grid electricity than their male counterparts, and they are more likely to be excluded from the benefits of electrification. #### Annex B: The Gender Country Assessments outline these national-level dynamics in detail.

**Energy Access Companies**

Equity investors push for profit margins, creating pressure on energy companies to shift in focus toward multi-purpose and higher-end SHS and away from entry-level products and first-time access as a result. Companies are often reluctant to pursue more first-time access customers in unfamiliar territories and have historically been slow to enter new markets. This is because they need to overcome significant hurdles that need to be overcome in a completely new market such as finding good local management structures, building relationships with the community, and navigating an unfamiliar regulatory system. While the challenges faced by OGS are of a similar nature across Sub-Saharan Africa, they will be probably more pronounced for companies operating in countries with limited awareness of a market or expansion destination. OGS companies in these countries also face geographic barriers such as vast distances between the location of targeted customers and the capital city or economic hub where the company is headquartered. The viability gap analysis can identify three gaps that can be segmented into three broad categories—company-specific constraints, market constraints and further constraints related to political risk. Company-specific constraints refer to internal issues which are an outcome of the OGS company's strategic and market positioning and includes issues with existing capital structures and lack of management bandwidth. Market constraints on the other hand are specific to the target market and include low electricity access rates, high energy tariffs, low mobile penetration rates, different fiscal systems and political risk. Funding constraints refer to the inability to produce off-grid products that can be leveraged by OGS to expand into more challenging markets. These viability gaps are presented in the table.

# Visibility Gap

## Nature of constraint

### Company-specific
These include:
- Lack of management capacity to expand.
- Limited market coverage.
- Seasonal overhangs.
- Conflicting organizational priorities.

### Market-specific
These include:
- Low population density which increases distribution costs.
- Inadequate infrastructure.
- High vulnerability gap.
- Low mobile money penetration.
- Insufficient consumer awareness.
- Few suitable distribution partners which necessitate costly in-house distribution infrastructure.
- Political instability.
- Inadequate understanding of credit risk in new markets.

### Funding-specific
These include:
- Lack of local currency facilities and elevated foreign exchange risks.
- Concentration of funding towards a small proportion of OGS companies.


## Hardest-to-Reach's investment approach enables capacity building of OGS enterprises by addressing key visibility gaps, through patient capital that meets the needs of companies, alongside targeted technical assistance. This is achieved in two ways: (1) through the provision of long-term financing for OGS space including vertically integrated companies and pure manufacturers who leverage distribution channel partnerships to access markets; and (2) through the creation of a new financial product with a unique structure of the Market Expansion Facility loans rewards first-time energy access sales, improving the economic incentive to serve off-grid tenures. The market expansion facility is designed to help OGS businesses overcome these structural inequities in respect of these challenges, with greater difficulty to access challenge being a challenge for the majority. Just 6 percent of startups that second more than $1 million in revenue have access to finance, compared to 40% of those earning less than $500k. Distributors revealed that three quarters experience access to finance as a key barrier to growth, with 54% specifically seeking debt finance.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 93

nascent and challenging emerging markets. In addition, there are those for whom commercial access to energy access will not be a viable option. Some $4.5 billion is needed just to close the affordability gap to be able to reach SDG7, and the pandemic, inflation and the energy crisis have set back global progress on reaching the lowest cost of energy access.

Payment-as-you-go models, such as PAYGo, can enable longer upfront investment and payment over time, making systems affordable for poor customers. PAYGo increases by 40% the number of people who can afford an entry-level (Tier 1) solar product. Even in the short term, the PAYGo model enables customers living on less than $2 per day to access clean energy services such as kerosene or diesel generators, to solar energy, with material GHG emissions avoidance impacts.

In addition, a climate justice component is tied to the needs of communities living in energy poverty. Low-income people and households in remote regions have borne at least 80% of the change in global temperatures over the past decade. They are also the most vulnerable to extreme weather events and will suffer the first and last benefit from the global capital allocated for renewable energy and climate-friendly investment. Governments and climate investors have bypassed them, particularly in the most remote areas where it is more expensive to provide clean energy services. These communities offer better returns on investment. Through Hard-to-Reach, Acumen will invest where others won't, to bring remote, low-income people access to clean, renewable solar energy; helping them adapt to a changing climate and build the resilience needed to face climate and economic shocks.

According to PWP (2017), the energy sector is considered a focus area in 45% of the NDCs in all total Non-Arctic countries. Based on data from UNEP, the energy sector is the most important sector for sub-Saharan Africa. The prioritization of the energy sector in most NDCs is likely to reflect both its relevance as an emission source and its potential for cost-effective mitigation and adaptation measures. All developing countries include mitigation measures within the energy sector in their NDCs.

In the early part of the investment period, Hardest-to-Reach will seek to work with OGS companies with different business models (i.e., vertically integrated and pure-play distributors) to enter or expand their reach into the electricity market in Benin. The countries below provide an example. They represent an addressable market of approximately 147 million people across 20 countries, which are all committed to reducing emissions by 35% compared to business-as-usual scenarios.

Alignment of these countries to the mission of HDR is strong across existing policies such as Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), national electrification strategies, and National Adaptation Plans (NAPs).

- **Benin:** In Benin, the national electrification rate is 33%. In 2020, the country adopted a new electricity code that provides for the opening of the electricity market to private investors, who can now invest in generation capacity. The government has committed to increasing its renewable energy share from 1% to 5% by 2030 and to reducing emissions by 47% compared to business-as-usual scenarios. Benin contributes 0.06% of global GHG emissions and its Government has committed to reducing emissions by 20% by 2030 compared to the reference scenario, accounting for a 15% reduction in emissions from energy use.

In Benin's National Adaptation Plan (NAP), specific measures are outlined for improving the climate resilience of the country. These include increasing access to and the use of early warning systems, which is based on higher levels of energy availability. The plan also mentions the adoption of quality standards for energy infrastructure and equipment, which includes solar-powered water pumps.

- **Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC):** The DRC has a national electrification rate of 9%. The government aims to reach a target of 65% electrification by 2025. Currently, the DRC contributes 1.3% of global GHG emissions and its Government has committed to reducing emissions by 47% compared to business-as-usual scenarios. The sectors targeted for this reduction are forestry, agriculture, energy, and waste. The DRC's National Adaptation Plan (NAP) aims to increase

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 95

## Guinea
The national electrification rate in Guinea is 46%. Guinea aims to achieve universal energy access by 2030. There is the potential for off-grid solutions in the country, and one of Guinea's main challenges is to ensure that these solutions are affordable and accessible to communities and the private sector at the rural level. In its NDC, Guinea outlined an unconditional emissions reduction target of 9.7% by 2030 relative to the business-as-usual scenario, and an emissions intensity reduction target of 15%. The country has a strong potential for investment in hydropower for 58% of total electricity generation, meaning its energy sector is vulnerable to climate change impacts. Guinea's main challenge is the lack of access to water resources on the Koulikoro River in western Guinea by 30-50 percent by 2100. This is a major concern given that several dams along the river provide much of the country's hydropower. Water has therefore been highlighted as an important adaptation priority.

## Sierra Leone
Sierra Leone has a national electrification rate of 26%. The government has a target to reach a 92% electrification rate by 2030 and recognized that 42% of the population would be without access to electricity. Sierra Leone's NDC outlines an unconditional emissions reduction target of 15% relative to the business-as-usual scenario, conditional on external support of 70% of the required investment in energy infrastructure. The country has a strong potential for investment in renewables, in particular promoting off-grid stand-alone solar investments through technical advice and knowledge sharing with other countries. Sierra Leone is also a member of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) with a strong focus on mobilizing financial institutions to create investment packages and counterpart funding.

## Uganda
Uganda has a national electrification rate of 29%. Uganda has made huge progress in closing the energy access gap, as electrification rates grew by 3% per year between 2010 and 2018. Uganda has made electrification a national priority and aims to achieve a national electrification rate of 75% by 2030 through the implementation of its National Electrification Initiative. The Government estimates there are potentially 2,962,525 rural households that could be electrified through the provision of solar home systems and up to 18,000 households that could be electrified through grid extension. Uganda's NDC outlines an unconditional emissions reduction target of 37% by 2030 relative to the business-as-usual scenario, conditional on external support of 70% of the required investment in energy infrastructure. The country's energy sector is also highly vulnerable to climate change as it is dominated by hydropower. Energy supply can be disrupted due to changes in rainfall patterns and transmission lines such as those damaged from droughts, and damage to power plants and transmission lines from floods and landslides. Energy is therefore an adaptation priority under Uganda's NDC.

## Zambia
The national electrification rate in Zambia is 37%. The country is targeting universal energy access by 2030 through the implementation of its National Electrification Strategy, which aims to connect 93.6% of the population to SHS by 2030. Zambia's NDC outlined an ambitious unconditional emissions reduction target to reduce GHGs by 25% compared with BAU by 2030 and a commitment to reduce energy intensity by 48%. Energy is one of its key areas of emissions reductions. The energy sector is also vulnerable to climate change, as 84% of its electricity generation is from hydropower. Energy generation has


---
**Page 100**
---

be adversely impacted by droughts and floods over the past two decades, and the country suffers from power shortages due to reduced river flows. Energy was therefore identified as a priority sector in its NDC.

The list of countries in which we operate will naturally evolve throughout the life of the Market Support Facility (MSF). The following is an updated list of countries that have expressed interest in using this model to address their energy needs and are currently in the process of developing their first host-to-reach markets in sub-Saharan Africa: Benin, Burkina Faso, Chad, Guinea Bissau, Lesotho, Malawi, Mozambique, Niger, Somalia and Togo.

In the project development phase, the AE has developed the Host-to-Reach Stakeholder Engagement Plan (H2RSEP). This plan includes significant desktop research on civil society organizations, and other relevant stakeholders including NDAs, indigenous groups, women, and other vulnerable groups. As of April 28, 2023, we received 16 NOLs from Benin, Burkina Faso, Burundi, Chad, DRC, Guinea, Guinea Bissau, Lesotho, Malawi, Mozambique, Niger, Sierra Leone, Somalia, Togo, Uganda, Zambia. The AE has also engaged with stakeholders in Madagascar and Tanzania. These countries all expressed significant interest in the project, but haven't been able to submit NOLs yet due to logistical challenges related to travel restrictions. We anticipate receiving NOLs from most, if not all of these countries before the publication deadline. Additional countries may be added to the programme after initial Board approval, provided they submit an NOL and the addition is supported by the AE.

Additionally, the AE has begun hosting stakeholder town halls with our first town hall in Sierra Leone. We engaged 10 different groups in that meeting, received project feedback, and strengthened relationships in Sierra Leone. We anticipate holding in-person and virtual stakeholder town halls for stakeholders in each H2R country.

The AE has developed improved capacity to engage relevant stakeholders as reflected by the H2R Stakeholder Engagement Plan, Acumen's Stakeholder Engagement Plan for GCF-Funded Projects, and Acumen's recent re-accreditation with GCF.

# D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Describe how the financial structure is adequate and reasonable in order to achieve the proposal's objectives, including addressing existing market barriers, ensuring that the project is viable without crowding out private sector and public funding. Refer to section B.5 on the justification of GCF funding.

## Hardest-to-Reach's Market Expansion Facility is expected to be funded by capital from a range of investors, including private sector financiers, Development Finance Institutions (DFIs), governments, and other public entities. The facility will provide access to energy services for the most vulnerable types of investors with different risk appetites at scale for the same purpose, providing first-time energy access to millions of people in developing countries. The initiative anticipates that it can substantially preserve investor capital, not to produce significant returns. The HDR initiative anticipates preserving investor capital by ensuring that the facility provides a sufficient level of appropriate liquidity to firms that are in the pioneer gap. Lack of return of invested capital (including some losses) should indicate the sector's preservation or development would be deemed a successful outcome.

Hardest-to-Reach has set a fundraising target of $200 million for the overall MEF, including of the potential GCF contribution. The facility is expected to leverage at least 5 times this amount in private capital from a significant number of energy access companies, Government agencies, DFIs, and even commercial investors. The $100 million investment into the facility will likely allow Acumen to co-invest $145 million of co-financing with other investors, including those from the private sector. GCF is mobilizing nearly three times as much capital for the MEF as it has committed.

The Hardest-to-Reach initiative is also very effective and efficient in terms of its impact. The $200m raised for both the Market Support Facility and Market Expansion Facility is targeting the provision of electricity to 72.4 million people, many of whom live below the poverty line. The initiative intends to provide electricity to millions of people who currently have no access or limited access to energy services because reliable energy access will have on people's lives.

As a further marker of efficiency, we note that the debt investment amount well exceeds the total GCF project contribution. Without GCF’s participation, Hardest-to-Reach may be able to proceed with an initial trial, but it will likely be unable to meet more than a fraction of the demonstrated demand for liquidity. If GCF is not able to contribute funding, there is a high risk that many companies will not maintain operations, as many investor entities consider such terms too risky. The resulting lack


---
**Page 102**
---

access would be lost that current customers are therefore at risk of switching back from solar to kerosene or diesel, and the huge potential of the sector to drive sustainable development and reduce emissions is curtailed. Electricity access for 72.4 million people will also be put at significant risk.

An important aspect of the efficiency and effectiveness of the investment is the establishment of a performance monitoring system that will track the achievement of the project’s objectives, as well as the number impacts across the 12 year lifespan of the initiative, but it is assumed that the impact of the HCR funds will continue after the funds themselves cease investing and the programme ends.

### E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Besides the arrangements (e.g., annual performance reports) laid out in Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), please give a summary of the project/programme specific arrangements for monitoring, reporting and evaluation of the H2R Project. This should include how the results will be reported to the relevant stakeholders and the responsible activity. Please also summarize the types of interim and final evaluations. Describe Accredited Entity (AE) project reporting relationships, including to the National Designated Authority (NDA). The AE is expected to report on project progress, implementation challenges and financial status at least quarterly. The AE will provide a summary of project performance against key indicators, including the reporting on project indicators, implementation challenges and financial status. Please note that interim and final evaluations are expected to embed an assessment of programme effectiveness and impact. The evaluation results should be reported in accordance with the guidance provided for the three-point scale rating. Refer to the guidance note for the summary requirements and factor in additional evaluation activities under this section accordingly.

H2R expects to engage in thoughtful and extensive monitoring, reporting, and evaluation of the Project.
Monitoring
The H2R team intends to monitor the H2R Project in accordance with the project Funded Activity Agreement and the Accreditation Master Agreement. The Management Team has developed a curated list of Key Performance Indicators to monitor throughout the Project to ensure it is accomplishing the outcomes set out in the Funding Proposal.

The Project team expects to assign a relationship manager to each investor to ensure that Portfolio Companies are being fully and appropriately monitored throughout the duration of the loan. The RM seeks to monitor the Portfolio Company for compliance with the conditions and covenants


---
**Page 149**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 145

of the investment obligation, company-level KPIs impacting the performance of the Project, climate results and impact performance. The investors should be expected to monitor legally binding conditions and covenants of the term sheet, company-level KPIs, climate results, and the impact performance of the Project on an ongoing basis. This will inform adjustments to project design, implementation, and potential changes to monitoring and evaluation tools.

The Project team intends also to utilize several sources for ongoing Project monitoring. First, the team expects to work with a consumer impact advisory board (CIB) that includes representatives from civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders. The CIB is expected to provide feedback on the Project’s progress against its KPIs and provide input on how to improve the Project. Second, the H2R team will conduct regular stakeholder consultations to monitor project performance and impact. This data is expected to inform Project implementation and strategy.

Finally, the H2R team expects to monitor the Project through extensive stakeholder engagement. The H2R team wants to share Project results with stakeholders to get their feedback on an ongoing basis to ensure that their voice informs our monitoring practice and understanding of the Project impact.


Reporting

The Project expects to report financial, impact, and climate results to a number of stakeholders on an annual basis. Investors are expected to receive reporting on a quarterly and annual basis detailing the Project activities, financial statements, climate results, and impact data. GCF will also receive reporting via the APR. Reporting requirements are expected to be established in legal agreements between donors, investors, and the project. H2R will provide an annual progress report to the MEF. The MEF is required by law to receive an annual audited account of the MEF within 120 calendar days at the end of the relevant accounting period. Donors will receive an annual MSF report that includes impact and ESG reporting.


---
**Page 150**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 146

H2R expects to engage NDAs on an annual basis. The team expects to share a Community Report with NDAs and seeks to engage with them on an annual basis to review project activities and seek feedback. NDA feedback will inform project activities and potentially strategy.

H2R also expects to share an annual Community Report with relevant stakeholders including industry associations, NGOs, Civil Society organizations, academia, and other institutions representing underserved communities.

When H2R MSP works with third party advisors with local market knowledge on deals they follow the Project level reporting requirements and have the proper arrangements to collect and verify Portfolio Company reporting, financial, ESG, gender, and impact data, and climate results. Third party consultants supporting technical assistance may also have a reporting expectation.

Portfolio Company reporting requirements are expected to be detailed in the term sheet and shared through the diligence process. Portfolio companies are expected to report financial and impact data on a quarterly basis. The H2R team expects to send investors reporting templates that need to be filled out and sent with evidence to H2R prior to the annual reporting deadline. The team intends to then verify and compile company-level reporting to inform Project level reporting.

Portfolio companies are expected to report on financial performance on a quarterly and annual basis. H2R will pay special attention to financial metrics like cash flows, cash balance, working capital needs, sales data, collection rate, write-off ratio, and PAR rates. Portfolio companies are also expected to report on ESG Action Plans, Gender Action Plans, consumer protection, and other relevant data. H2R companies are also expected to report on impact KPIs related to sales, sex-disaggregated beneficiaries, emission reductions, and other relevant data.

Evaluation


---
**Page 151**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 147

H2R expects to implement independent interim and final evaluations aligned with the GCF Monitoring and Evaluation Framework and the Evaluation Policy.

Acumen's evaluation team intends to hire an independent evaluator to implement each evaluation to ensure our commitment to evaluator’s best practices: impartial, fair, objective, robustness and credibility, and relevance and participation. The team will develop an evaluation project scope ahead of seeking a partner. Acumen's evaluation team is expected to evaluate the scope of the project. Acumen will use the AE fee to pay for the independent evaluator. H2R intends to use a mixed methodology approach to learn about the project. H2R intends to disclose the interim and final independent evaluations to GCF.

Additionally, the team is exploring the development of long term evaluation projects for H2R. As this project has a multitude of opportunities for developing unique insights to share with the broader climate and impact investing community, H2R is developing a strategy to determine what types of long term evaluation projects are feasible, appropriate, and important to conduct for the betterment of these markets, to demonstrate the paradigm shift of the project, to ensure the replicability of H2R, and to reach an interested audience to scale the project impact.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), sanctions, product liability, environmental, reputational, and other risks that may impact the project. The following table represents how Green Climate Fund has described the proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional rows if necessary.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and Operational | High | Medium |

## Selected Risk Factor 1: Execution

### Description
Energy access companies that are newly entering a volatile market, may not be able to execute on first-time energy access goals, and may struggle to generate high volumes of quality sales. Last mile distribution is also challenging in these markets due to the presence of informal business operations and external market, political, currency, and macro level risks.

Additionally, Acumen is expanding into new markets that it has not invested in up to date. Inexperience in the market landscape may lead to longer due diligence or missed investment opportunities. Companies with a strong track record in these markets also may have some gaps in navigating the market landscape in H2R markets leading to longer due diligence or missed investment opportunities.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The chief mitigant for execution risk for investors will be to ensure that companies have the funds and accompaniment they need to build capacity. The team engaged with many off-grid solar companies about the challenges of entering new markets, including the lack of experience in market research gathering or scaling in H2R markets. Following the information gathering phase, creating realistic, data-based projections is critical for successful project execution. The Technical Assistance (TA) and market research. The Market Support Facility, with its focus on building capacity and stable operations well ahead of scale-up, offers capabilities to the ambition of the project, demonstrating an acknowledgment that scaling up is not immediate but rather a complex and hazardous. That being said, this project is ambitious and high risk (and in our view, necessary for the world's poor).

## Selected Risk Factor 2: Macro

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | High | High |

### Description

Hardest-to-Reach markets often face significant economic risks, political volatility, regulatory inconsistency and legislative unpredictability. These risks do not exist in isolation; some even have the potential to be correlated across a given region. A major crisis or series of interlocking risk events could derail progress on energy access.

Moreover, global trends have been very impactful to volatile markets and unstable political environments. Inflation, a potential global recession, and the ongoing effects of the Covid-19 pandemic have created even more uncertainty for H2R markets.

Macro and global risks have had significant impacts on potential off-grid solar customers. Rising inflation has increased the cost of living, making it harder to save money or allocate expenditures that are affordable for people in poverty. Covid-19 created lock-downs and supply chain issues that contributed to unemployment in some markets and harmful shipping delays for OGS companies.

## Mitigation Measure (a)

Targeted technical assistance may support legal evaluation to understand standards. Political risk insurance can be a valuable tool to hedge some of these risks. The team has conducted multiple political risk studies on H2R countries, which will be summarized in the investment memos and shared with the Investment Committee along with strategies for aligning the risk aligned to the context of specific investments.

It's also worth considering that H2R will have a broad portfolio, diversifying risk across a range of geographies.

## Selected Risk Factor 3: Mission Drift

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |


---
**Page 154**
---

# Description

Energy companies that feel under pressure to repay loans and show growth may opt to sell more to higher-income segments, negating some or all of the company's proposed additional impact.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The use of impact-linked grants and loans, which will incorporate a measure of the depth of poverty of a customer base, will be used to incentivize companies to target lower income segments. The impact-indexed loan facility will be structured as a blended finance instrument with concessional terms for low-income customers.

Additionally, Acumen will conduct due diligence on each Hard-to-Reach investment to ensure that companies are capable of properly utilizing HDR funding. The IC will be instructed on ensuring that the minimum level of support of each investment. Term sheets will stipulate the use of funds for both loan or equity investment.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Distortion

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Low |

### Description

The act of choosing a subset of companies to fund, some of which may operate in existing sub-Saharan African markets, could distort the future market for off-grid solar and crowd out less well-connected competitors. The risk of distortion is that concessionary debt going towards energy companies could distort the overall energy access debt market.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

While there are existing companies operating in Hard-to-Reach markets, there are not many of them, and few if any that have reached a significant scale serving the most challenging reaches of the country. By providing concessional funding to these companies, the risk of distorting an existing market remains low.

As evidence from ODI suggests, the risk of crowding out other possible (private) sources of finance is less relevant in low-income markets where there are few private sector players and where access to concessional financing is limited. Companies that have access to international capital markets at affordable rates in the absence of the concessional investment will be able to provide energy services at a lower cost than those companies that do not.

The risk of market distortion is also mitigated by the fact that concessional funding will be provided in terms of first-time energy access and poverty level of customers, which will likely ensure that the benefits of concessionality pass down to the low-income, first-time energy access customers (in terms of increased supply of off-grid products) and minimize a potential for market distortion.

Hardest-to-Reach is an integrated life-cycle financing to accompany companies to expand into new, challenging markets and to support their sustainable growth over the longer term through supportive capital line of sight working capital financing which is linked to impact outcomes. Over the life of the two vehicles, two pools of technical assistance will be provided to support companies with respect to market entry, business planning, capacity building, and stakeholder coordination efforts - among other things - the sustainability of the intervention is enhanced and impacts beyond the life of the fund continue in a self-sustaining way.

# Description

Some companies that receive H2R investment are early-stage startups, and may have inefficient management or governance structure which may result in ineffective decision making and the funds allocated to these companies may not be used as intended. This could lead to key executives or change in group share-holding structures. The materialization of this risk could result in ineffective decision making at a company's level and the company might not achieve desired outcome from H2R investment. For example, if a company that receives H2R investment is unable to reduce its GHG emissions, households accessing renewable energy, and retained employment, may fail to materialize.

The pandemic has also created a new risk of customers defaulting on their payments but owing to ongoing effects of the COVID-19 pandemic, customers' inability to pay and/or management of companies receiving H2R investments.

# Mitigation Measure(s)

The facility manager has a well-designed due diligence process which involves detailed review of ownership, governance, management and business operations along with interviews of senior management. In case any weakness is identified at the assessment level the borrowers are advised to resolve these issues appropriately along-with these strong covenants are also incorporated to improve the governance structure of the company. The facility manager will also ensure that all relevant documentation. The screening process described in the ESMS is meant to ensure that H2R investments are made only with companies who have a good governance structure and management team. Continuous monitoring and oversight described in Section E, should mitigate the risk of attenuated impact H2R expects to have related to poor governance structures. In addition, the facility manager will also identify those who may have inefficient or weak governance structures. Companies who are identified by the facility manager as having inefficient or weak governance structure than other investors in the H2R portfolio could be invested with the support of relevant advice with local market expertise. If H2R finds that a company has particular governance risks, the manager may decide to create conditions subsequent to address these risks.

## Selected Risk Factor 11: Excluded Activities

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |

### Description
Hardest-to-Reach is reaching new markets and investing in early-stage companies with limited track record who may engage in prohibited practices. If regulatory entities or investors find that a company engages in prohibited practices, the company or individual could face criminal charges, shutting down of operations, loss of license, and other consequences.

The materialization of this risk can have serious consequences such as blockage of funds from investors, regulatory or criminal proceedings against the funds/investors, sanctions and may also cause monetary losses.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Hardest-to-Reach has developed an Exclusion List to screen out potential Investors engaging in any prohibited practices. If a company is found on this list, it will not be considered for investment activity, do not fund any activity, and do not support any activity on the Exclusion List. If a company does engage in activity on the Exclusion List then they cannot receive H2R financing. H2R investors will also agree to a binding legal agreement that prohibits engagement in Exclusion List activities.

## Description

The imposition of ambitious, time-bound impact goals could create incentives to push the number of loans at the expense of quality. This would have a deleterious effect on the credit risk management of energy companies and other borrowers, as well as the ability of financial institutions to manage their portfolios. Companies may also respond by adopting aggressive collections techniques at the risk of breaching relevant consumer rights and legal protections. This could create overindebtedness among low-income households, and other consumer protection-related concerns.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

For PAYG companies, businesses viability is dependent on more than sales volume. Cash flow only comes from a pool of receivables if they are of sufficient quality. Assessing risk management capacity, monitoring portfolio health, and incorporating collections rate into the index for impact-linked loans will likely be essential to ensuring that these companies can continue to operate in a sustainable manner.

The HGR due diligence process is designed to flag the types of weaknesses in governance and business processes that make poorly managed groups more vulnerable to financial distress. The HGR due diligence process will seek to identify any CP weakness/challenges faced by the potential investee. Investors will be supported to fulfill and grow beyond their current best practices in their treatment of customers, in order to safeguard impacts and respect the rights of consumers.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 159

## Key Project Level Environmental and Social Risks

The project has four primary environmental and social risk concerns:

- E-waste
- Consumer Protection
- Forced Labor
- DGS Company ESG Readiness

### E-waste

While there are many strong environmental benefits to Hardest-to-Reach, the project team recognizes both environmental and social risks for the project. One of Hardest-to-Reach's primary products is solar panels. Off-grid customers will be among the first-time buyers in the HBM markets. These markets have weak and nascent waste management sectors. Moreover, many African countries lack adequate infrastructure to manage e-waste. Some distributors may have weak or immature e-waste policies, procedures, and procurement. Additionally, some markets in Hardest-to-Reach are at a nascent stage with few players and little coverage.

### Consumer Protection

There are two primary social risks for Hardest-to-Reach: First, pay-as-you-go (PAYGo), products sold on credit, create risks for customers who can face issues such as over-indebtedness, poor credit, and delinquent payments if they fall behind on their payments. PAYGo also allows many customers who might not afford to pay for a solar home system up front the opportunity to access electricity with smaller recurring payments.

Additionally, the team has concerns around consumer privacy and protections for PAYGo and other solar home system customers. While many companies are now GOGLA consumer protection signatories, customers may face threats from malicious third parties. Infringing on customer data protections may open off-grid solar customers to identity theft, fraud, and other issues surrounding identity theft.

### Forced Labor

Secondly, the H2R management team is concerned about forced labor in the solar supply chain. Human rights investigators have found evidence of forced labor in some regions where there are solar manufacturers. Advocacy groups warn that marginalized people may be facing mass detention in certain


---
**Page 164**
---

regions. Many of these marginalized people face dangerous working conditions that may be exacerbated by ongoing global trends. Advocacy groups and human rights officials expect these global factors to create downward pressure on manufacturers to increase forced labor practices under worsening conditions.

### OGS Company ESG Readiness

The project team expects that Hardest-to-Reach will invest in companies with varying degrees of ESG readiness, ranging from those with no or minimal ESG policies to those with strong and comprehensive policies across a number of ESG issues. Several larger pipeline companies have indicated that they have strong ESG policies and procedures, while smaller companies may not yet have such policies.

Experience has demonstrated that early-stage SHS companies, especially last mile distributors, may not have robust ESG policies and procedures, nor would they have strong organizational capacity to mitigate ESG risks. These companies have a small footprint so their risks are marginal to the project success and impact on beneficiaries.

The Hardest-to-Reach ESMS includes the ESIA, which has a more extensive report on project and company level environmental and social risks and impacts.

### Environmental and Social Risk Mitigants

To ensure the Hardest-to-Reach project achieves its mission and impact, the team has designed a through ESMS aligned with GCF Policy, Acumen Policy, and industry best practices. The ESMS shares the project development, management, and monitoring and reporting policies and procedures. All investments will be screened against the project Exclusion List before being accepted for due diligence.

The project team will diligence companies with a variety of ESG diligence tools including base-line questionnaires, site visits, company evidence in the form of policies and procedures, desktop research, a climate resilience assessment tool, and supplementary questionnaires.

### The ESG Findings

The ESG findings will be memorialized in an ESG due diligence report and the investment memo. The Investment Committee is expected to consider ESG factors in their deliberations. The ESG due diligence report will also provide the information needed for developing the term sheet binding, time bound, ESG Action Plan.

The project will offer Portfolio Companies technical assistance tailored to supporting company-specific ESG, consumer protection, gender, and business readiness needs. This will include: needs diagnostics, workshops, direct support, and policy and procedure guidance and resources.


---
**Page 165**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 161

The team will monitor and report on ESG strategy across the portfolio and the project to relevant teams.

Hardest-to-Reach will also ensure that the team has the organizational capacity to identify and mitigate risks across the project and investments. The team will hire a Climate, ESG, and stakeholder engagement officer who will be responsible for ensuring the implementation of the ESMS.

The team will also ensure that they listen to stakeholder voices so that communities affected by the project can continue to shape and improve the project design and implementation.

### Indigenous Peoples

The program intends to have a landing page on the AE website that will share information about the project including the ESMS. We seek to provide guidance on how indigenous communities can request support from the GCF independent Redress Mechanism and/or the GCF Secretariat's indigenous focal point at any stage, including before a claim has been made. Additionally, the team seeks for H2R investments in areas with high indigenous populations to be prioritized. This is especially important for investors that directly engage with indigenous communities.

Acumen, as the AE, and the H2R team have reached out to indigenous organizations to learn from, share the project design, and ask for input. We have also asked NDA to connect our team with indigenous communities. We seek meaningful engagement with indigenous populations who may be impacted by the project. This will allow us to provide information to indigenous communities about project activity and direct them to GCS expanding in areas that have high indigenous populations so that they can benefit from access to affordable, clean electricity opportunities.

Acumen have conducted a thorough and robust gender assessment for the Hardest-to-Reach project aligned with the objectives of GCF’s Gender Policy. Acumen's Gender Sensitivity Policy for GCF-funded projects. The gender assessment informed the development of the Hardest-to-Reach Gender Lens Investing Strategy and the Hardest-to-Reach Gender Action Plan. and are detailed in Annex B.

As part of a HTR pilot project, Acumen hired a lead consumer insight surveying business to survey women and men in the Hardest-to-Reach markets. The findings demonstrated similar impacts for men and women. Both groups felt the need and importance of using SHS products to attain electricity.

The team also conducted extensive stakeholder engagement across the Hardest-to-Reach markets including speaking with companies, government entities, industry associations, NGOs, and civil society organizations. The HTR team spoke with local gender experts and civil society organizations from multiple HTR markets on local gender challenges and opportunities that informed our gender lens investing strategy and gender work.

The HTR team worked to do project-level gender assessment that included 14 country assessments. The team generated two reports for each of the remaining Hardest-to-Reach markets. The team relied on desktop research, industry experts, gender experts, OGS companies, and survey data to inform the gender assessments, GJU strategy, and Gender Action Plan.

Findings

Barriers to gender equality in OGS sector
The link between energy access and gender equality is well recognized. Energy poverty has a strong gender dimension as the lack of access to energy inhibits women's well-being, and their access to economic opportunities, and it strongly affects women’s living conditions and time-use.

One of the key barriers in the market relates to lack of sex-disaggregated data for the OGS sector both at the consumer and workforce levels.
Gender and social norms limit women's access to technologies and their abilities to enter the workforce. Companies have a limited understanding of the business case for gender inclusion in their businesses, and of how to make their practices more inclusive. Several companies we spoke with shared that they are beginning to build institutional capacity on gender and want to build inclusive policies and procedures into their operations.


---
**Page 167**
---

### Serving women with OGS technologies

Overall, evidence shows that women have less access to off-grid electricity than their male counterparts, and they mostly use pico-solar products and lanterns or other lower-tier technologies. An analysis conducted by Value for Women on 59 companies working in the energy sector found that only 25% of companies had a gender equality policy, with only 10% of these policies specifically addressing women as customers who are women. In HVR country contexts, prevalent gender norms are likely to affect access to off-grid electricity and other renewable energy technologies. These norms can also inhibit women's rights to own land and other assets, making it challenging for women to access credit and purchase renewable energy technologies. PPGY companies may reach a lower percentage of women customers due to additional barriers related to mobile ownership and mobile money usage among women.

### Women’s participation in OGS companies’ workforce

ESMAP and GOGLA recently surveyed 37 OGS companies from 20 different countries and found that on average, only about 15% of workers are at senior levels. According to a previous GOGLA study, it was noted that only 27% of women work in the OGS sector. An analysis conducted by Value for Women found that only an average woman represents 33% of the workforce of energy companies.

The country assessments indicate that several barriers limit women’s participation in the workforce and in the economy more generally, with prevailing gender norms, gaps in education levels and discriminatory policies and practices affecting women's productive activities and economic empowerment in several countries.

### Hardest-to-Reach Gender Work

Hardest-to-Reach seeks to build OGS markets with a gender lens, and strong commitment to gender equality. H2R is also committed to supporting companies to build their gender capacity to support their operations and strategies. Additionally, H2R is committed to mitigating risks of sexual harassment, abuse, and exploitation across the project, investees, and beneficiaries.

All companies will undergo a gender assessment to determine their gender strengths and weaknesses. Opportunities for growth will be captured in a binding Gender Action Plan. All companies in the H2R portfolio will have to create a GAP that will be monitored over the company engagement with H2R. Companies must commit to strong anti-sexual harassment policies, non-discrimination policies, and their best effort to collect sex-disaggregated data.


---
**Page 168**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 164

HCR intends to provide companies with technical assistance to support building and executing UGRs, creating strategies to hire women, and to retain female customers. HCR will also have technical assistance to train women for jobs in the off-grid solar market. Additionally, HCR intends to build women in HCR market awareness of OGS products and local climate hazards by partnering with gender experts on improving includes marketing and customer retention activities.

HCR will monitor and report on the gender assessments, GAPS, sex-disaggregated data, and other KPIs in the GAP and logic framework.

Lastly, HCR will continue to engage female stakeholders and communities from marginalized or unheard communities to ensure that their insights inform the project design, implementation, and management.


### G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the project/program's financial management including the financial monitoring systems, financial accounting, auditing, and disbursement structure and methods. Refer to section B-4 on implementation arrangements as necessary.

Articulate any procurement issues that may require attention, e.g., procurement implementation arrangements, and how they will be addressed in the respective proposal, articulation of procurement risk assessment undertaken and how that will be managed by the AE or the implementing agency.


#### 1. Accounts Payable / Purchases / Expenses

##### 1.1. Initiating and Authorizing:

a) When purchasing supplies, property, equipment, and services from an external source, the Program.

i) Will ask the AE's General Counsel for potential conflict of interests.

ii) Shall neither solicit nor accept gratuities, favors, or anything of monetary value from contractors, potential contractors, or similar service providers.

iii) Will follow the Procurement Policy including consulting the AE for any goods or services > $100K USD, awarding the winning bidder, documenting the decision justification, and the evaluation of each vendor following the contract term. **The Program's Market Support Facility (MSF) will seek to engage service providers with track records of delivery in alignment with the above policies and the**


---
**Page 169**
