# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 165

## G

### AE’s procurement standards: Service providers must demonstrate capacity and execute around the scope of work developed by the MEF team and shared with the AE for review, where relevant. All procurement decisions will be reviewed by the MEF Director.

**The Program's Market Expansion Facility (MEF) will seek to engage service providers with track records of delivery in alignment with the above policies and the AE’s procurement standards. Service providers must demonstrate capacity and execute around the scope of work developed by the MEF team and shared with the AE for review, where relevant. All procurement decisions will be reviewed by the MEF Director.**

### iv) When required by investors, the Program may publish an RFP for a specific project.

#### b. Vendor selection is dependent on:
- i) The results of a standardized background check. Each vendor and its related entities are also subject to Patriot Act searches - each search reviews international sanctions, embargoes, terrorist lists, criminal history (e.g., arrests, indictments, and convictions), and financial history (i.e., foreclosures, bankruptcies, etc.).

- ii) The different investing strategies the Program will employ (Market Support Facility and Market Expansion Facility), and require sectoral expertise and operational infrastructure.

### 1.2 Recording and Posting Accounts Payable: All invoices will be reviewed and processed at the end of each week. Each invoice is submitted to the Program’s accounting platform for review and approval and must have an associated vendor record in the system. Before approving, the appropriate finance employee will review all invoices, wires, checks and account coding.

### 1.3 Reconciling Accounts: The Program reconciles all bank, petty cash, and investment accounts monthly as follows:
- a) Once available, the Finance Associate (or designate) reviews all relevant bank statements.
- b) The Finance Associate (or designate) will complete the reconciliation within seven days.
- c) The following is the general outline to be used to reconcile accounts:

#### i) Comparison of bank statements and cash receipts, verifying dates and totals deposited.
- ii) Comparison of bank and/or wire transfer dates received against dates sent.


---
**Page 170**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 166

#### ii) Investigations of items rejected by the bank (i.e., returned checks or deposits).
#### iv) Cancellation of canceled checks and the disbursement journal (check numbers, payee, and amount).
#### v) Examination of canceled checks for authorized signatures, irregular endorsements, and alterations.
#### vi) Accounting for the sequence of checks from month-to-month.
#### vii) A review and proper mutilation of void checks.
#### viii) Investigations, write-off or reissue/stop-pay checks outstanding for an extended period.

## 2. Expense Reporting Procedures

### 2.1 Principles and Procedures
a. The Finance Associate Director will be responsible for compiling monthly and year-to-date expense reports. Expense records are reported to the general ledger and accounting records.
b. Quarterly financial reports analyzing the Program's financial position and the effectiveness of its management, will be presented to its Limited Partners as required.
c. Records to be maintained include copies of contracts, invoices, changes to contracts or budgets, proof of payments, and allocation tracking.

## 3. Expected Contractors

The Program may contract consultants for several purposes. Technical Assistance consultants will be hired for expertise in ESG | Consumer Protection; Gender Equity; Business development (as it relates to underrepresented groups); and/or Market Expansion.

The Program's Market Support Facility (MSF) will focus on early-stage investments, employing equity, equity-like index loans, grants, and joint venture financing options. Launched in January 2023, the MSF is a $15 million facility that provides flexible funding to early-stage companies. The company selected both a local market advisor and a separate small-ticket debt advisor. In each case, the firms distinguished themselves from other providers through their unique value (i.e., their extensive experience in providing equity-like lending to early stage off-grid energy companies across the Program's target geographies).

Conversely, the Program's Market Expansion Facility (MEF) has both a separate investment vehicle structure and a separate debt facility. The MEF will focus on later-stage investments, employing larger ticket sizes, utilizing impact index loans (working capital), and receivables financing, and will be structured as a pooled investment vehicle. With an expected launch in January 2024, the MEF will run through the Program's final year of operations. In addition to the market advisors mentioned above, the Program is considering contracting with certain service providers to support fund management, such as an administrator and back office and support in risk assessments. While

## G4 Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission of the proposal. However, the accredited entity may request that certain portions of the document are not disclosed to the public. The Information provided in confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied if the disclosure of any part of the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information.

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- X With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, will be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity must provide two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - Full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly together with a confidentiality notice regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy.
  - Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP211

## Proposal name: Hard-to-Reach

### Accredited entity: Acumen Fund

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

#### 1. The Hard-to-Reach (H2R) programme is designed to supply off-grid solar (OGS) energy to low-income populations in 16 least-developed countries (LDCs) in sub-Saharan Africa.

#### 2. The H2R programme aims to develop an OGS ecosystem in LDCs with low electrification and high poverty rates. This will be achieved by supporting OGS companies, investors, and other stakeholders to establish markets for OGS energy.

#### 3. Although the OGS market is thriving in many parts of Africa, there is reluctance among commercial banks to finance projects in LDCs due to perceived higher risks compared with more established markets. The H2R programme aims to address this by providing a combination of grants and loans from different investment risk profiles. This design is intended to help OGS companies expand into H2R countries.

#### 4. The H2R programme will be implemented through the Market Expansion Facility (MEF), which has received a USD 10 million reimbursable grant from GCF. The MEF aims to support and improve the capacity of local players and regional competitors in LDCs, as well as to provide grants for OGS companies to develop their business models.

#### 5. To raise USD 145 million across commercial banks, investors, and other stakeholders, Acumen has raised USD 20 million from a junior equity tranche and USD 5 million of non-repayable grant capital from GCF. The MEF aims to mobilize private finance for OGS companies in LDCs by providing grants and settling in H2R countries.

#### 6. Interest rates will be adjusted based on results achieved in terms of first-time access to clean energy, with the aim of attracting additional investment financing from GCF in the form of grant and junior equity expected to mobilize private finance to support OGS companies and build a sustainable OGS ecosystem in the challenging markets for just-energy systems.

### 1.2 Impact potential

#### Scale: High

#### The programme has both mitigation and adaptation impacts. The mitigation impact is estimated at 5.3 megatonne of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO₂e) reduced over the lifespan of the programme, and an adaptation outcome, the project expects to reach 45 million beneficiaries.

#### The mitigation outcome is achieved by providing OGS products to people to achieve clean energy access (tier 1+). 120 MW of decentralized OGS energy generation capacity shall be newly installed. Currently, the majority of rural households rely on kerosene and biomass as their primary sources of energy.


---
**Page 202**
---

their primary energy sources for lighting, cooking, heating, etc. In the baseline scenario, a large portion of households do not have access to electricity due to lack of grids and low-income levels, which limit a household's ability to pay for electricity. The programme reduces the need to rely on expensive kerosene lamps and other polluting energy sources.

5. The greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation impact was calculated using the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change registered clean development mechanism methodology (UNFCCC CDM M01-02). This includes avoided emissions from lighting systems and AMS-LL ("Electrification of rural communities using renewable energy"). Assumptions were made that each OGS unit has a lifetime of 5 years, with the number of units to be financed per area, as well as the expected lifetime of products are presented by the programme. The assumption is that the average household size in the region where emissions have not been included by the programme in the calculations, which is conservative especially for kerosene lamps high polluting carbon sources. The assumption of the regional electricity access rate on reaching full product and fund placement. Overall, the projected GHG emission reductions are deemed to have been calculated in a conservative manner.

6. The expected adaptation outcome is 54 million direct and indirect beneficiaries. Indirect beneficiaries are calculated by multiplying OGS sales by factor S, which corresponds to the average number of people per household. Direct beneficiaries are defined as households that are people with first-time access to electricity. Rural electricity access in programme countries is 11 per cent on average.

7. The increase in resilience to climate change through the programme is based on the following expected outcomes: (i) most vulnerable people and communities would still have access to energy services, even if they were affected by extreme weather events or climate change due to owning OGS products; it was calculated that 1.6 million people could benefit from enhanced resilience in this way; (ii) households with access to electricity are less likely to be displaced due to climate hazards within the total projected number of H2B beneficiaries; (iii) health, water and sanitation services will be more accessible for all communities through the more securely access information including extreme weather event and health warnings; health infrastructure can also be powered by OGS products. In addition, households with additional devices, such as radios or mobile phones, allow access to climate-related information. Seventy-five per cent of the total direct beneficiaries counted which 54 million are beneficiaries with first-time access to electricity and therefore counted as beneficiaries in the adaptation outcome) are expected to access electricity power for the first time with an OGS product. This results in a significant increase in energy security, especially for the most vulnerable people and communities' benefit from increased and/or diversified income sources. Energy services can be used by households to diversify their income base through access to light and increasing opportunities for home-based business. A 0 Decibels household survey conducted in 2015 found that 48 per cent of respondents reported using OGS to increase their income base. Based on the 54 million first-time customers of OGS, this results in 5.4 million households profiting from increased and more diversified incomes.

The total number of 54 million direct beneficiaries is expected to be reached by all three aforementioned impact areas. The programme thus has a potential impact on increasing the climate resilience of rural communities through access to energy services in participating countries.


* *Decibels, (2015). Why Off-Grid Energy Matters.*


---
**Page 203**
---

13. **Paradigm shift potential** Scale: Medium

The programme promotes the entrance of companies in the African energy sector into off-grid markets where they have not been willing to enter up to now because the current finance structure has the ability to crowd in investors that would otherwise find the markets too nascent and risky. The programme also provides a platform for companies to enter contracts with impact-linked concessional finance and targeted technical assistance. Last-mile distributors have a crucial role to play in delivering OGS products, but capital is currently not flowing into these markets because of the lack of demand from end-users, who are mainly small clients and markets currently only marginally covered by private sector providers.

14. Companies will be encouraged to enter these markets with the aim to increase their business in these markets, thus resulting in positive multiplication effects. The programme supports companies to build solid operational platforms in challenging markets, and to grow in such markets through a combination of concessional finance, technical assistance, and a growing population of underprivileged clients.

15. Accessing knowledge and evidence-based insights gained through the programme. This can result in increased capital inflows and additional investments on the ground. The programme's demonstration of linking energy access and adaptation, as well as showing that OGS is a viable business model for companies to enter markets with concessional funding for OGS ecosystems in more difficult markets, demonstrating the viability of challenging markets and providing evidence-based insights into how to scale up these markets will sustain the growth of these markets in the long term and adding to a replication effect.

15. Scaling up the programme would depend to a large extent on the sustainability of markets where companies are operating. The experience with other energy access programmes shows that companies have been reluctant to enter markets with high levels of default among their customers. The Acumen project Participation in Energy Access (PEA) found that companies operating in such markets were experiencing significantly reduced sales and collections, coupled with disrupted supply chains, limited cash reserves, and significant financial losses. This is a common experience across the sector.

16. The experience of many development activities is that successful interventions do not replicate automatically. On-ground demonstration of successful intervention is a positive first step but does not guarantee replication. The programme does require activities to include all relevant stakeholders and to actively manage and support the market environment in order to ensure that companies can compete with enterprises to avoid inflow of competition. Overall, the planned programme activities in the area of knowledge management and outreach are not considered to be comprehensive enough


---
**Page 204**
---

## Sustainable development potential

### Scale: Medium to High

The programme is aligned with Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) [7] (affordable,
reliable and modern energy for all). The programme will contribute towards SDG-6
[improved educational outcomes], SDG 1 and E (economic savings and income generation), and SDG 13 (climate action).

One factor that could limit sustainable development impacts, or even cause negative impacts, is the local currency rate risk from MEF on-lending to OGS companies. The ITAP is conceived as a long-term investment in the energy sector with a focus on sustainability and an end-customer perspective. In the funding proposal, it is written that "Acumen is exploring the feasibility of providing a loan guarantee for the ITAP through the HCR MEF". According to
ITAP, Acumen wrote that "[In the meantime, the HCR MEF would make loans in US]. The target borrowers are OGS companies with a strong track record and a proven ability to manage their multinational operations, aware of the impact of currency fluctuations on their borrowings." This essentially means that the ITAP will be providing a loan guarantee for the OGS companies
that are hardest-to-reach consumers. Given current volatility in currency markets, such a pan-on-can potentially have devasting impact on the ability of those consumers to pay. Alternatively, if the OGS company is unable to repay its debt, it could result in a default and/or bankruptcy of the
company's financial viability. For the very sophisticated OGS companies who truly understand the local currency risk, this is not an issue.

In terms of benefits, OGS products can have positive impacts on people’s health and safety. Traditional kerosene lanterns are harmful to people’s respiratory health because of household air pollution. The use of OGS systems for lighting purposes in developing countries
is related to particulate matter and carbon monoxide and disproportionately affects women and children. According to a study conducted by the World Bank, 2014-2015, 48% of OGS customers reported health improvements after acquiring an OGS system.

OGS systems can potentially help to preserve ecosystems and reduce deforestation for energy usage with OGS systems.


---
**Page 205**
---

2). OGS products can enable people to generate income through their use, either directly (e.g., expanding business hours or offering new services such as cell phone charging) or indirectly (e.g., shifting tasks or activities to the evening because of better quality light). In the case of households, this could mean more time for children and women to engage in productive activities primarily of women and children, allowing more time to be dedicated to other activities. The programme will also provide a new source of income for informal vendors by enabling them to extend formal banking systems through usage of the PAYGO financing structure to provide OGS products, allowing people to build their credit histories.

20). There is potential to create around 14,000 additional green jobs as a co-benefit. An estimated 640,000 of these jobs could be a source of employment for women. However, at least some of the jobs might not be an additional one-to-one substitutes for jobs held before (e.g., vendors of kerosene lanterns).

23). The programme will target the indicator’s benchmark of having 28 per cent of its customers in low-income households by 2019. In addition to this, the programme also intends to engage companies and the OGS ecosystem in developing more opportunities for women and girls to participate in the energy sector, including through training and mentorship schemes, allowing for the generation of knowledge on the engagement of women with energy products and services useful throughout the OGS industry.

24). The economic and social potential of the programme is considered medium to high. It can make critical steps to improve the livelihoods of the poorest in a sustainable manner. Tackling the current crisis could strongly improve the sustainability potential of the programme.


### 1.5 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

25). Across the 16 African countries included, there are around 4 million beneficiaries that do not have access to modern electricity services – representing roughly just under 10 per cent of the total unelectrified population of sub-Saharan Africa. On average, 79 per cent of the population in these countries live below the poverty line and 58 per cent of them are living in areas climate vulnerable contexts in terms of worsening heat stress, more frequent droughts, and increased flooding. The situation is further exacerbated by the fact that many countries listed in the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) climate vulnerability index are also among those with the lowest levels of economic development. This vulnerability is exacerbated by non-climate drivers such as high poverty levels, weak infrastructure systems, lack of emergency response capabilities, and fragile grid electricity and telecommunications networks.

26). At country and company level, limited investment can currently be attracted to the market for OGS products in sub-Saharan Africa. The number of companies operating in this space between 2012-2019 has slowed to larger and more established, first-generation companies whose primary location and focus is East Africa, with Kenya capturing the lion’s share of the capital. This trend is likely to continue as these companies are able to attract higher margin customers, leaving out the rural, poorer markets of LDCs with a higher actual and perceived risk.

27). International financing going to developing economies in support of clean energy focuses within sub-Saharan Africa on non-proven markets, and risks leaving other countries and regions behind. These include issues such as limited access to finance, regulatory issues, institutional weaknesses, and lack of transparency contributing to a less favourable


---
**Page 206**
---

1.6 **Country ownership**  
*Scale: Medium*

The prioritization of the energy sector in most nationally determined contributions (NDCs) is likely to reflect both its relevance as a large emission source and the availability of various cost-effective mitigation measures. All developing countries included mitigation measures related to energy efficiency, renewable energy, and energy access in their national low-carbon development strategies and national adaptation plans.

*Note:* The GCF has engaged with 16 programme countries: Benin, Burkina Faso, Burundi, Chad, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Guinea, Guinea Bissau, Lesotho, Malawi, Mozambique, the Niger, Sierra Leone, Somalia, Togo, Uganda, and Zambia.

The programme is currently in its initial phase. The GCF has held initial stakeholder engagement meetings. The programme plans to engage with stakeholders, especially national energy sector agencies, as well as other relevant government entities or other public actors would also be important to engage, including ministries related to energy and trade and industry, and regulatory agencies. To date, the programme has engaged with over 90 different stakeholders in 16 countries.

The GCF is currently implementing a three-year Open Capital Advisers (OCA) phase in early 2023. Open Capital Advisors, on behalf of the programme, carried out five in-country visits to key energy sector agencies and other relevant government entities or public actors in each country. Senior members of the programme team joined Open Capital Advisers for three of the trips.

The country ownership is assessed by IFCAP at medium level. The programme aligns with the country’s NDCs, but there are limitations on the scale of investment that can be made available to the energy sector in 16 countries so far has been limited. Stakeholder and country participation and involvement in this phase is regarded as a priority for the programme.

### 1.7 **Efficiency and effectiveness**  
*Scale: High*

The programme intends to leverage additional capital of factor 3 relative to the GCF contribution through factor 4 including the MFU and the MEF. Government agencies, foundations, DFIs, governments and even commercial investors have indicated interest in layering their capital with first loss grant capital. If primarily government agencies, foundations or DFIs are involved, there is a risk of repayment from the private sector will, however, presumably be fairly limited.

The total investment amount shall exceed by factor 3 on initial project investment amount, based on anticipated reinvestments.


*International Energy Agency (2021). Financing Clean Energy Transitions in Emerging and Developing Economies. Paris: International Energy Agency.*


---
**Page 207**
---

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Based on the assessment above, ITAP endorses this funding proposal.

The ITAP notes that the programme is entering a potentially high-risk market and that, in light of the lessons learned from previous GCF investments, the proposed approach proposed, and the measures taken, as well as the experience of the AE in this type of venture, ensure that risks are managed appropriately. The ITAP also endorses the use of an insurance-based risk mitigation approach.

The role of GCF is to provide risk finance to push the borders beyond an existing small pilot scale. This programme has a high potential for replication and impact with a high import and replication potential. Therefore, the programme ticks all the boxes of the investment criteria set out in the GCF Investment Policy.

The ITAP recommends that the AE structure the logical framework to be able to track progress towards adaptation outcomes 3 and 4. The user surveys planned to track the number of beneficiaries is a good idea but may not be sufficient for this purpose, as it raises questions to track enhanced access to climate-relevant information, or to communication media in general. It also raises concerns about the potential perishability of the OGS systems in the face of any extreme events experiment (outcome 3).

The ITAP recommends that the GCF concessional capital be utilized to take local currency risk and not to provide a hedge against volatility. The AE should consider taking such a risk onto OGS companies, or worse, onto end customers. The hedging products may be expensive for the AE, but they are likely to be even more expensive for the end user and customer who will bear that volatility (which may be borne by someone). Such a hedge ultimately also addresses concerns from potential limited partners on the financial sustainability of the programme. It is important not to use concessional capital as a substitute for additional capital, but it is equally important to utilize concessional capital to pay for “insurance” products, which minimize risk for the company and customers operating in hard-to-reach markets.

**Page 208**
---

# Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings

## Proposal name: Hardest-to-Reach

## Accredited entity: Acumen Fund

### Impact potential

Acumen is pleased to note that the impact potential is considered 'high'. The commentary captures the current context of climate change and its effects on communities, particularly in low-income countries where there is a pressing need for low income communities where this project will reach. As referenced, the 50 cities targeted by the program are among the most vulnerable to climate change (representing over 20 per cent of the total population in the participating countries), and many will receive entry level solar home systems.

Monitoring the adaptation outcomes of the program is a core consideration of the program design. Acumen's experience of incorporating user surveys has shown their effectiveness in capturing a range of impacts, especially when combined with other monitoring methods such as focus group discussions (FGDs) or survey questionnaires. Survey questions can reveal patterns of household access to climate-relevant information through QoP-oriented indicators (e.g., frequency of access, type of information accessed), and non-QoP oriented indicators (e.g., post-test of access, frequency of access, information subject matter) and subjective experience of the user. The use of a mixed-methods approach allows for triangulation between qualitative and quantitative data on the frequency of access to climate information, for example, is a specific and measurable indicator to inform future program design.

The impact potential is also considered 'high' in terms of the following dimensions: 1) immediate impact as well as verification capacity; in terms of dimension 3c, resilient energy access, through durability in the face of external shocks, particularly to protect customers, as well as H2R investments and our ability to achieve resilience in the face of extreme events. In terms of dimension 4b, long-term impact, Acumen will be able to monitor changes in household consumption patterns during and after a climate event. Acumen's experience with user surveys has shown their effectiveness in capturing a range of impacts, especially when combined with other monitoring methods such as focus group discussions (FGDs) or survey questionnaires.

### Paradigm shift potential

Acumen notes the TAP's balanced and helpful assessment of the program's paradigm shift potential. As highlighted by Acumen, H2R investments have not traditionally been able to play a greater role in reaching hard-to-reach markets and communities but with the help of a blended finance structure which effectively mitigates the real and perceived risks associated with investing in these assets, Acumen is confident that this program will be able to make significant strides towards achieving impact through this approach, including through the knowledge sharing component.

Acumen's experience has shown that the sector is well positioned to capture the immediate and long-term impact of the program. The sector's lessons from the shock of the COVID pandemic should be seen as a source of resilience for the sector in terms of its ability to adapt to external shocks. H2R investments have been hit by a decrease in raw materials and decreased incomes among their predominantly rural and economically disadvantaged customer base, which has led to an increase in default rates on loans. The sector is well positioned to face from external shocks, particularly to protect customers, as well as H2R investments, and our ability to achieve resilience in the face of extreme events.

The program's design includes a blended finance structure that will provide technical assistance facility support to companies to build up their capacity and long-term viability. TA entities will be able to provide technical assistance on a range of topics including: 1) business development; 2) market entry; 3) access to finance; 4) policy and regulatory support.

Acumen is aligned with the view that broad stakeholder engagement is needed to enhance the potential for widespread adoption of H2R technologies. Acumen will be able to leverage its experience as a leading energy access practitioner and a knowledge sharing platform, with expertise in areas including capacity building - including technical assistance - or all stakeholders (companies, financiers, and governments) and policy and regulatory support to create enabling environments.

The guidance on currency risk is noted. Looking across the sector, we also note that the viability of different local currency hedging approaches is being explored and the RES approach will benefit from any lessons being shared.

As highlighted, with the support of the GCF funding, H2R can generate significant adaptation benefits by providing first-time energy access and strengthening the climate resilience of 8 million people.

Acumen also notes the clear mission alignment between H2R and GCF to deliver a break from a business-as-usual electrification trajectory and to reach people not covered by traditional approaches of investment.

# Introduction

This document provides an assessment of the barriers and opportunities for advancing gender inclusion in Hard-to-Reach (H2R) energy countries. The assessment includes an overview of the enabling environment to clean energy access, women's participation in the off-grid solar ("OGS") sector workforce, and the key barriers to advancing equality in the sector. This document outlines key entry points for the H2R initiative, followed by detailed in-country assessments for the sixteen target H2R countries.

## Barriers to gender equality in the OGS sector

The link between energy access and gender equality is well recognized. Energy poverty has a strong gender dimension as the lack of access to energy inhibits women's well-being, and their access to clean cooking fuels and improved lighting can improve living conditions at home. Off-grid energy technologies have a direct benefit to women's health, particularly in terms of reduced risks of pulmonary and respiratory diseases. Women's participation and leadership in the OGS sector is also critical for increasing the adoption of off-grid solar systems, promoting urban energy use and addressing energy poverty, while also advancing women's economic empowerment. However, multiple barriers limit women's ability to access OGS technologies and to fully participate in OGS value chains as clients, consumers, employees and entrepreneurs.

One of the key barriers is the market failure due to lack of disaggregated data for the OGS sector both at the consumer and workforce levels. The current lack of disaggregated data available about customer segments and demand for products leads to missed opportunities to reach women customers and meet their needs. In addition, the lack of data on women-owned enterprises and the potential for productive uses of energy prevents increasing the number of women customers and the tendency to design business strategies and projects that could transform their economic opportunities.

Historical inequalities of distribution and control of assets impact women's ability to access OGS products, and affordability is a well-recognized barrier to accessing OGS technologies (for both women and men). Women are more likely than men to be excluded from land tenure laws, resulting in women's lower access to financing, to mobile phones and mobile money.

Gender and social norms limit women's access to technologies and their abilities to enter the workforce. Intra-household decision-making and mobility constraints inhibit women's ability to be aware of energy products and to purchase them. Women may be discouraged from seeking employment or starting a business in the OGS sector because in some contexts these may not be considered suitable opportunities for them, on because they are discouraged from interacting with men due to social and gender norms.

Companies have a limited understanding of the business case for gender inclusion in their businesses, and of how to make their practices more inclusive. OGS companies often do not strive to reach women customers or to hire women because they are unaware of the positive


---
**Page 213**
---

Page 209

impacts on their business performance, and they may see this as an extra cost rather than a strategy to meet profitability and business goals. Further, companies' policies often do not recognize gender equality as a priority and few companies have equal opportunities policies or are intentional in addressing gender equality in their strategies to attract, recruit, and promote women at all levels. Even when companies have an explicit gender strategy or they intentionally strive to be inclusive of women, this may include hiring more women into technical roles, working designing products and services for female customers and women entrepreneurs, and ultimately attracting gender-lens investments.1

The sector lacks appropriate and dedicated training and mentorship opportunities for women to enter and thrive in the solar/Micro. Because of prevailing gender norms, women may be excluded from training opportunities and may not be considered suitable for employment opportunities in technical roles, as well as sales and distribution roles. Further, male-dominated workplaces are not attractive to women and pose additional risks to gender-based violence and sexual harassment.

Serving women with OGS technologies

Overall, evidence shows that women have less access to off-grid electricity than their male counterparts. Women tend to use price-sensitive products and lanterns or other lower-tier technologies.2 SHS customers are predominantly rural men living under the median poverty line of $31.00.3 In a survey conducted on 35,000 customers of 49 OGS companies in 17 countries, 60 Decibels found that only 32% of customers are women.

An analysis conducted by Value for Women on 59 companies working in the energy sector found that only 8.5% of off-grid solar companies collect data on customers, and half of them reported that they serve 46% of customers who are women. A small group of companies (6) also collect data on end-users, and they reported serving 60% women among their end users.1 In another study conducted by ESMAP and GIZ in 2019, it was found that only 35% of companies said that women buy solar lanterns and smaller products mostly on a cash basis.4 In a survey conducted with 37 OGS companies, ESMAP and GOGLA found that about 40% of companies collect sex-disaggregated data on their customers.5 Another study by Value for Women found that only 12% of customers are women.6 Another report indicates that the share of women in PAYGo solar customers is as low as 25%.7 Based on the limited and fragmented available data, it can be concluded that,

Table 1: Available data on women served by clean energy and off-grid solar companies

These studies indicate that the sector overall lacks sex-disaggregated data, resulting in a limited understanding of gender-related barriers to access and limited knowledge on the impact that these technologies have on women's lives. The limited knowledge about customer segments and women's needs within OGS and clean energy companies may also contribute to this gap.

In addition, men are the main purchasers of energy products, even when women are the primary end-users; this results in skewed consumer data and products that do not always reflect the needs or preferences of women.


In HCR contexts, prevalent gender norms are likely to affect access to off-grid solar technologies, limiting companies' abilities to reach women. Discriminatory gender norms inhibit women's rights to own land and other assets in most countries, making it challenging for women to access credit and purchase renewable energy technologies. PAYGo companies may reach a higher proportion of women than OGS companies because they are more likely to have mobile ownership and mobile money usage among women (see Table 2). Overall, in Sub-Saharan Africa, the gender gap in mobile ownership is 30%. This gap has grown from 22% in 2017 since ownership by men increased at a faster rate than that of women. The gender gap in mobile money use among women is 45%.

Further, the gender gap in mobile internet usage in the region stands at 37%. For the countries for which sex-disaggregated data is available (e.g., Uganda and Zambia), the low rates of access to electricity in HCR contexts are even more pronounced in female-headed households.

## African Indigenous People, which can be defined as "nomadic and semi-nomadic pastoralists and hunter/gatherers who live in situations of marginalization and discrimination,"[29] are faced with additional challenges in accessing resources and opportunities. In HCR countries, indigenous communities face the same challenges as other vulnerable groups but also face additional challenges due to lack of state recognition. They heavily rely on nature and land for their livelihood and are often located in forests and conservations areas, facing eviction from their land. Further, African indigenous people are disproportionately affected by gender-based violence (GBV) and sexual violence. Women face additional challenges such as sexual violence (e.g., Pygmies in DR). In West Africa, political instability and conflicts disproportionately impact women and female-headed households. Women and girls are particularly vulnerable to GBV and other forms of violence. In some populations, they are victims of systemic attacks and kidnappings, and are forced into slavery.

In the context of a changing climate, indigenous communities and minority groups whose lives depend on the health of their natural environments are increasingly more vulnerable to shocks, while their specific energy needs are often overlooked. Without addressing these needs, the transition towards renewable energy will not be inclusive for SSNIPs.[30] Empowering Indigenous populations, women, youth, and other traditionally marginalized people is an essential part of protecting rights and creating jobs. In the context of HCR, OGS technologies can provide first access to information and resources, including land tenure, financial services, traditional business models and energy programs. Their additional vulnerabilities (i.e., geographic remoteness, lower access to information and resources, higher poverty rates, discrimination) should be considered when developing strategies for OGS product dissemination and when they consider hiring in the communities they serve.

In conclusion, it is safe to assume that product reach to women and other vulnerable communities in HCR markets will be slightly lower than average, or compared to countries where the OGS sector is well established (e.g., Kenya). The HCR initiative provides an important opportunity to fill some of these gaps. It is recommended that companies work with OGS initiatives by requiring investee companies to collect and track sex-disaggregated data on sales.

## Women's participation in OGS companies' workforce

Data on women's participation as employees and leaders in the sector is scarce and fragmented. ESMAP and GOGLA recently surveyed 37 OGS companies from 20 different countries and found that on average women constitute 41.7% of workers across all levels.[36] In contrast, a previous


---
**Page 217**
---

### GOGLA study indicated that only 29% of women work in the OGS sector. An analysis completed by Value for Women found that on average women represent 32% of the workforce at energy companies. Similarly, in the renewable energy sector in general women hold roughly 32% of jobs. Another recent study based on 64 surveyed companies operating in SSA found that women represent 37% of the decentralised RE workforce, and that they are more likely to be employed in lower-level positions than men. In the decentralized RE sector in Sub-Saharan Africa, 10% of women had higher-paying science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM)-related jobs, and 16% held non-STEM technical positions.

Based on the available market data, it can be concluded that globally women represent about 33% of the clean energy workforce across all levels and this figure can be used as a benchmark for female workforce participation.

Considerable work remains before OGS companies will reach a gender balanced workforce, and this is especially true with respect to increasing the participation of women in leadership and


---
**Page 218**
---

management positions.34 The key challenges affecting companies operating in these contexts relate to workplace policies and practices, social norms and gender stereotypes and legal barriers.

The country assessments indicate that several barriers limit women's participation in the workforce and in the economy more generally, with prevailing gender norms, gaps in education levels and access to political and economic power, and limited and economic empowerment in several countries. Among H2R countries, only Uganda presents data for Distributed Renewable Energy (DRE) labor force participation of women, with 27% of women employed in DRE companies compared to a regional average of 15%. Women are more likely to be found in private sector energy companies compared to regional and global averages (42%), however these women are reportedly concentrated in administrative roles rather than technical or managerial positions. In the Democratic Republic of Congo, women's participation is considerably lower, especially in markets such as Togo, Guinea or DRC where gender norms prevent women from undertaking many productive activities and where the OGS sector is still in its nascent stages. Women make up only 10% of the workforce in the mining industry in the DRC, and of the few women who do attend university, only a small segment completes a STEM degree, which is needed for higher-paid and more senior jobs in OGS companies. Once women enter the job market, they are often paid less than men with similar qualifications. In some countries, women are not suited for technical roles. Companies that intentionally recruit women for these positions face pipeline-related challenges and need to address such gender and social norms.

Women entrepreneurship is also hindered by gender norms and discriminatory policy frameworks. In most of the assessed countries, women are predominantly represented in the informal sector, in some cases due to no legal or formal rights to register a business or open bank accounts, and they largely lack access to formal financing.

The H2R initiative brings opportunities to mandate that companies collect and annually report a sex-disaggregated staff footprint, with data on female versus male employment at all levels. Such data can be also used to showcase the business case for the inclusion of women across all levels of the CGI workforce.

Stakeholder Engagement

As part of the process of understanding the needs and perspectives of women in Hardest-to-Reach geographies, we have engaged with over 25 individuals, notably as a Stakeholder Consultant for the Gender Equality and Gender Monitors. The mission had representation from over 11 sub-Saharan African countries, as well as from specialist gender off-grid solar organisations. An example of the latter is Smiling Through Light (STL), a Sierra Leone-based social enterprise which helps women in rural areas to produce and sell their own goods such as handcrafted baskets and sell solar products. STL focuses on the last mile which creates employment and income opportunities in underserved areas.


---
**Page 220**
---

We also launched a customer survey for a pilot investment in Benin where we listened to and learned from 85 women about their experience with OGS products. These women shared their opinions on the barriers they face when it comes to purchase, and challenges with the products. Their challenges and hopes mirrored what we heard from government officials, NGO leaders, and other important stakeholders.

The following insights were gained from our consultation and studies:

- The sector needs more support of women entrepreneurs: The view was expressed that, in order to address the challenges and inequities women face when accessing OGS products, it would be impactful to have more women in leadership within the sector. This could be achieved by investors backing more women-led businesses and by the provision of training to uplift women’s entrepreneurial skills.
- Awareness of off-grid solar among women in some areas is still low: This is particularly the case in remote areas, where there are no distributors serving the local communities. In these areas, women have less access to gain better access to electricity with OGS and have fewer OGS alternatives than men. As a result of our consultations, conversations with gender experts and surveying of female OGS customers, we recommend that companies with technical assistance generating gender-inclusive marketing strategies.
- Off-grid solar products are very important as entry level electricity in rural areas: Hardly any women in the surveyed communities have access to electricity at home, and will value if they are made aware of the various products and uses. 80% of women in our survey shared their lives would improve with access to electricity. 50% said that their lives would be improved by OGS products.
- There are grassroots women-led initiatives which are seeking NGO and donor support: The consultation shone a light on some women initiatives which women are engaged in, and grassroot-level organizations are part of the wider scope of the H2R initiative. We are hopeful the H2R’s uplift of the OGS sector can have a positive knock-on effect, in particular by raising awareness of OGS.

Key entry points for the H2R initiative

1) Elevate women in the OGS sector as customers, employees and entrepreneurs. The country assessments indicate gender gaps in access to energy and employment opportunities, and a comprehensive programmatic approach is necessary to create opportunities for women at all levels.

2) Do no harm from a gender perspective. This entails evaluating potential investors' companies’ risk identification and mitigation with respect to negative impacts on women as part of its Environmental, Social and Governance standards. H2R’s Environmental and Social Risk Management Plan will establish gender-specific screening criteria.

3) Provide clean and affordable energy to women-headed households and those living below poverty benchmarks that indicate women are predominantly affected by energy poverty and that poor and vulnerable households are mostly women-headed. The H2R initiative can impact women's lives by requiring investors to collect and track disaggregated data on customers reached and by supporting companies to implement strategies to better serve women, ultimately reducing gender gaps in access to clean energy technologies.

4) Track investments in women-led businesses and in businesses that have gender-forward business practices. Investors have a clear role to play in applying a gender lens to the investment process, including through the use of gender equality indicators such as the gender balance of companies, their commitment to equality, and by measuring the progress of investees in reaching women customers. The H2R initiative can encourage companies to recruit, retain and advance women at all levels within the company, from boardrooms to executive offices, and in sales, deal structuring and in post-investment support. Companies that are intentional in addressing gender gaps in access to OGS technologies and opportunities can apply business practices that build capacity for women to participate in their sector’s capacity to enter and advance in the sector, and at the same time reach women customers.

5) Positively impact organisational culture by supporting portfolio companies to adopt and/or expand gender-inclusive policies and business practices. To increase the attraction, retention, and advancement of women in the OGS workforce, companies need to have in place inclusive policies that address issues such as sexual harassment, discrimination, and wage parity, which are considered necessary requirements to operate in H2R contexts where wage disparities persist and where gender norms and perceptions around women’s roles in business still exist. Other policies and practices such as flexible working arrangements, parental leave policies, flexible return to work after parental leave, etc., are necessary to create woman-friendly workplaces and to attract and retain women at all levels within the company, including those who own or run their business, particularly in product design and market research and customer engagement areas (sales, distribution, after-sales), as well as their supply chains. Such practices will enable


---
**Page 222**
---

companies to understand women’s needs and to better serve women customers, ultimately meeting sales targets and contributing to H2R’s program impact.

The H2R initiative will mandate companies to assess the level of gender inclusion in their business, and support them in developing actions and strategies to become more inclusive. The gender equality action plan (GAP) developed within 90 days of investment (see Gender Action Plan for details) will provide detailed information about which policies companies are expected to develop or update, as well as the steps to increase women’s representation at all levels of their organizations, including in leadership positions.

Evidence shows that companies that undertake action to close gender gaps and that integrate intentionality into their strategies and business processes have been successful in attracting further financing. The H2R initiative will provide a platform for companies to share best practices and it will help mobilize finance to enter these challenging contexts and to reach underserved markets.


6) Provide dedicated training and employment opportunities.
At the ecosystem level, the H2R initiative will address the lack of training and mentorship opportunities by supporting women-dedicated training programs that aim at closing skills gaps. The H2R initiative should also focus on establishing a network of local organizations to provide training and mentoring services to unemployed actors to increase awareness of job opportunities and to foster the creation of internships and mentorship programs. The initiative can also enhance collaboration by convening stakeholders such as women’s groups, women’s associations, women entrepreneurs, and other key local actors in engagement workshops or events together to create a conducive working environment for women in OGS markets.


7) Facilitate OGS sector transformation.
Asienim has a long record of providing training with a gender lens. In H2R, Asienim will use insights to build the case for the benefits of investing in human capital development and taking a gender lens to OGS sector facilitation. There is a tremendous opportunity to gather insights on the strategies companies will adopt to close gender gaps in the OGS sector, from reaching customers to hiring women in their workforce, and disseminating lessons learned to the wider sector.


Country-level assessments
The assessment of the sixteen H2R countries identified context-specific challenges for advancing women’s participation in the OGS sector. These countries present some of the lowest electricity access rates in some of the world’s poorest poverty areas. Disparities between men and women persist in the areas of economic opportunities, health, education, political participation, and empowerment. Gender norms and customary laws shape women’s lives and their ability to access assets and resources and to participate in economic activities.

### Benin
ECOWAS Commission (Ecowas) is implementing a project entitled: Promoting Agricultural Competitiveness through Solar Energy (PAC SOLAR) in Benin with the aim to enhance women’s access to renewable energy, increase their income and employment opportunities. The project will provide solar panels for irrigation, charged with the job of cleaning the solar panels regularly, and maintain solar PV installations.

### Zambia
The Ministry of Energy conducted a Gender Assessment of the Energy Sector in Zambia in 2018, and is in the process of developing a Gender Mainstreaming Strategy and Action Plan. Zambia’s Energy Regulatory Board (ERB) has also committed to participating in the Gender Equality Index for the energy sector being developed by the National Association of Regulator Utility Commissioners (NARUC) with funding support from the U.S. Agency for International Development (USAID) and Power Africa to improve gender equality in the energy sector.

### Mozambique
The Mozambican Women Of Energy is a platform for women in the energy sector, focusing on areas of action for gender equality in this sector.
- BRILHO programme, an SNV - Netherlands Development Organisation program in partnership with TechnoServe that aims to increase the number of women with access to electricity and improve their socio-economic status. It also promotes the inclusion and empowerment of women in the sector as collaborators and leading business owners through technical training of companies on gender-focused company policies and practices.

### Sierra Leone
Sierra Leone is a part of ECOWAS and is implementing its policies for gender mainstreaming in Energy Access. Under ECOWAS, The Ministry of Energy is also responsible for the implementation of the PAC SOLAR project. The Solar Engineers Training Centre has been established to undertake rural electrification projects and to provide solar training to literate women. Between 2014 to 2017, 50 solar engineers were trained in the centre. As a result of this training, some were connected in 3,500 households. The Barefoot women have also provided solar connections to 60 community clinics in northern Sierra Leone, which earlier lacked connection and now provide electricity for medical equipment in these clinics.

### Guinea
Women Fund for Gender Equality (FGE) developed a training program on renewable energy technologies to promote sustainable livelihoods for women and in partnership with a local grantee organization Partenariat - Recherches Environment - Medias (PREM), trained 300 women in Guinea’s Triasol Islands how to install, maintain and repair solar water pumps. The project also supported regular maintenance of the installed systems by providing spare parts and training on troubleshooting.

- The Lumire program, implemented by local NGO FVS-Amade in partnership with UNICEF since 2015, works with Village Savings and Loan Associations (VSLAs) to provide women with access to solar lanterns for lighting their homes at night. This purchase of solar lanterns by the NGO FVSAmade, which sells them either directly or through VSLAs.
- In 2020, the Republic of Burundi released a grant amounting to USD 100 million from the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA) for this project.
The project's Technical Assistance facility has a significant gender focus.

o **Education:** Establish a 100% scholarship program for 40 women studying STEM-related courses, supported by the General Director for Energy, who wish to pursue careers in the renewable energy sector. The program aims to provide leadership training and targeted mentorship programs for female talent in this field.
- Employment: Enabling 30% employment of women in overall jobs through mini-grid development by supporting private sector companies in mapping out their business plans (e.g., installation, maintenance, and operation of solar panels and small-scale gardening activities) and also upstream supply chain activities.
- Entrepreneurship: To foster female entrepreneurship in the sector, 20% of start-up grants are to be earmarked for female-headed or majority-female-owned businesses. In addition, women's associations (such as Association des Femmes d'Affaires du Burundi) would receive development support would be provided to women entrepreneurs in the sector.
- Women customers: Diversified subsidies will be provided to companies in the mini-grid sector that are willing to provide access to off-grid systems (asset ownership) by vulnerable households. Further, women's participation is expected at all levels of the project, from household and enterprise level, followed by an assessment of the positive impact on women as a result of adopting off-grid products (in terms of time savings, drudgery reduction, and livelihoods).

Togo

Entrepreneurs du Monde (Edim) is an initiative supported by the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) and the government of France, created in 1998, that works to provide microfinance services, including credit, insurance, savings, goods and services for their families. Based in Togo since 2012, Edim runs the Mvemo Energy project that aims to combat global warming and deforestation in Togo by facilitating access to clean, modern, and affordable energy products for vulnerable populations, especially women.

Malawi

FINCOOP is a financial cooperative that provides affordable financial services, serving micro-, small and medium entrepreneurs, farmers, and salaried employees. The company has been operating since 2013 in the country with its headquarters in Lilongwe using multiple channels such as Village Savings and Loan Associations (VSLAs), civil servants, and other informal savings groups. FINCOOP aims to meet the needs of women's requirements through the deployment of payment methods such as Pay As You Go models, cash, and group loans, and by offering longer repayment terms. From 2013 to 2015, more than 48% of women-headed households were able to access solar products through this program.

Lesotho

Lesotho has launched a Women In Sustainable Energy Lesotho (WISE-L) initiative that aims to promote gender equity and energy in the sector to create opportunities for women.

- The GEF-funded Africa Minigrids Program implemented by UNDP seeks to demonstrate productive uses of electricity to reduce costs, increase the viability and sustainability of minigrids. The program is currently being piloted in Burkina Faso with pilot projects that are small-scale farmers (women and youth), mostly who will benefit directly from the electricity supplied by the solar mini-grids. The project started in 2013 and is expected to be completed in 2018.

- The GCF-funded Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGFREENIF) - Greening Agriculture through Renewable Energy (GARE) and Scaling-up Extension Smalholder Agriculture in the Great Wall (GWGS) programs,47 are implemented by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and local financial institutions. The GARE program is being piloted in Burkina Faso. The program seeks to provide concessional loans to foster the best adaptation and integration of renewable energy technologies into existing value chains in each target country. Operated by local, national agricultural banks (LNABs) – the Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso - the facility will offer special lines of credit for women farmers (50% of total loan portfolio), youth (10%), women and youth organizations, cooperatives, and MSMEs (including agribusiness dealers and solar operators). Besides providing loans, the program includes a Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) to address capacity, knowledges, and policy gaps.

gaps hindering the uptake of green business projects in the selected countries: It will target LUMINs, central banks, and clients (FIs, MSMEs, and cooperatives), with a special emphasis on women and youth.

World Bank project for $295M - Chad Energy Access Scale Up Project (PAAEAT) - aims to increase access to electricity and clean cooking solutions via expansion of the main power grid, development of mini-grids, solar home systems, improved cookstoves, and natural resource management. # Democratic Republic of Congo

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

The Democratic Republic of Congo is one of the biggest countries in Africa, with a population of around 92 million people—50.1% of which are women—an annual population growth of 3.1%, with 33% of the total population under the age of 15.

| Indicator | Result | Source and date |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Life expectancy at birth | 61.5 | UNDP (2022) Gender Development Index |
| Maternal mortality ratio | 473 | UNDP (2022) Gender Inequality Index |
| Literacy rate (female aged 15 and above) | World Bank, 2019 | |
| Women's Labour Force Participation Rate (age 15 to 64) | 62% | World Bank, 2019 |
| Mean years of schooling ** | 5.6 | UNDP (2022) Gender Development Index |
| Estimated gross income per capita | $896 (PPP $) | UNDP (2022) Gender Development Index |

## Legal status of women

The DRC has ratified many UN Conventions relating to equity and inclusion, including the 1969 Convention on Eradication of Racial Discrimination, the 1966 Convention on Civil and Political Rights, and the 1966 Convention on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR). Further, DRC has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), which prohibits discrimination against women in its many forms and emphasizes gender parity in public institutions (Article 14) It also provides through Articles 5, 14, and 15 the foundations of legitimation of any policy of equality and equity. According to the Constitution, women and men have equal rights to work, education; vote, justice, and political participation.

**Note:** Average number of years of education received by people ages (25 and older), converted from educational attainment levels using official durations of each level.


---
**Page 229**
---

The DRC developed a National Gender Policy (2017-2021), which aims to achieve gender equality and social empowerment through five strategies: (i) the promotion of gender equality and the empowerment of women; (ii) strengthening the role of women in politics and women's rights and employment; (iii) increased access of women and girls to decision-making spheres; (iv) fight against forms of violence against women, including sexual violence; and (v) ensuring the implementation of the National Gender Policy. Despite an overall favorable policy framework for gender equality, implementation of policies remains a challenge, with a lack of appropriate budget, limited awareness, poor coordination, a limited capacity to monitor progress, and weak enforcement.

Political participation

The Constitution forbids discrimination against women (Article 14), but there is no law or policy that ensures women's just representation. The DRC demonstrates only 13% female representation in the National Assembly; 10% of women elected at the provincial level, no female governors in the provinces, and no unity under the government. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes because gender norms impact women’s political participation, and there is a strong notion that politics are for men and that women often need to follow their husband's opinions.

Social and cultural context:

Men dominate both the political and social spheres, and they are the culturally and legally appointed heads of the household. Women share with them decision-making power over most aspects of life. Women are defined by their household or reproductive responsibilities, which include maintaining the physical and moral well-being of their families. Men are perceived to be responsible for the financial health of their families. According to traditional gender roles, women are expected to be the heads of households beyond the age of 60; men in rural settings.

Sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV) has been a defining feature of the DRC’s history in the country and this is reflected by its status as one of the highest incidences of rape in the world, and there is no legislation addressing domestic violence. Domestic violence is widespread and is generally accepted as a response to women's alleged infidelity or disobedience. Women are also at risk from intimate partner violence, especially by a partner or household member. UN Women reports that 51% of Congolese women experience lifetime physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence, with 37% reporting violence in the last 12 months.

Household decisions are dominated by men, and the lack of agency within the home and within communities is reflected in women’s limited access to education, health care, credit, and other critical resources. A survey conducted by the World Bank found that men take decisions over a woman's income, health, major household investments, and social visits. Gender norms mean that there is an expectation for men to be the primary household breadwinners; consequently they are expected to pay for major


---
**Page 230**
---

Page 226

### Health

A high maternal mortality rate is a major health issue in the DRC (10 per 100,000 live births); it is caused by several factors such as lack of access to medical treatment, early marriage, and high birth rates. Throughout the country, the average age at first birth is 19 years, and 37% of women aged 20-24 were married before their 18th birthday; as a consequence, more than 6% of men in this age group are Congolese women suffer from poorer health compared to men, with higher rates being attributed to men's control over women’s bodies, low access to resources, and women’s lack of freedom to move.

### Education

One of the key drivers of inequality in the country is linked to gender disparities in education, directly impacting women’s participation in the labor market and wage earnings. Articles 43 and 44 of the Constitution stipulate equal compulsory education and the eradication of all forms of discrimination against children with regard to their right to education due to early marriage and pregnancy, which leads to a gender gap in education.

The average age at first birth is 19 years old for women compared to 8.4 years for men; as such, only 40% of women have attended secondary school compared to 69% of men. Nationally, an estimated 66% of Congolese women are literate compared to 88% of men. Among young women, literacy is even lower at 52%, compared with 73% for young men.

Traditionally, education for women and girls is less valued and households often prioritize education for boys compared to girls.


### Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

Sixty percent of the country’s population is under the age of 20, and this percentage will only expand, as DRC is projected to attain a population of 120 million by 2050. More than 60% of women live under the


---
**Page 231**
---

poverty threshold as compared to 51% of men. Women are mostly engaged in agriculture, predominantly subsistence farming, and small businesses in the informal sector.

The labor force participation rate for women is 29%, compared to 63% of men, and only 4% of women work in wage employment, compared to 32.9% of men. Gender gaps persist across different economic sectors, with women earning less than men in entrepreneurship profits (66.5% gap) and in wage earnings (77.3% gap). Less than 10% of women are wage or salaried workers compared to 25% of men. In the private sector, the wage gap is nearly 80%.

The underlying factors for these gender gaps include women's lower levels of education, training, and primary factor behind women’s lower labour force participation. Lower educational attainment, skills, and concentration in low-skilled jobs are also important factors that contribute to the gender pay gap.

The entrepreneurship gender gap indicates that businesswomen have lower levels of capital and lower numbers of workers. The female share of employment in senior and middle management is 6% while only 11% of firms have women as owners or directors.

Further, across all sectors, lack of control over land, lack of agency in the household, prevalent gender- based violence, and marriage have been found to be consistent drivers of gaps in economic empowerment. The first Family Code (1987) included legal restrictions to women's mobility, workspaces, marriage, and assets, but the 2016 reform of the Code included the elimination of marital authorization for women’s access to employment, education, and financial services, as well as the right to participate in economic opportunities. While family laws have been updated, many have yet to be ratified. Specifically, Article 448 of the DRC Family Code (1987) provides that a husband is head of the household and the revised Family Code (2016) still maintains this provision. Women are also required by law to obtain their husbands’ consent for a married woman to obtain her husband’s permission to open an individual bank account, or access financial services in general. The 2016 Family Code does not provide women with the right of inheritance over half of their husbands’, who retain control of all household, financial, and medical decisions in both statutory and cultural law. Widowed, divorced, or single women face ostracization and most often must submit to the will and authority of male family members.

The Women Business and the Law Index Score for the DRC is 78.8 (2021). The overall score for the Democratic Republic of Congo is higher than the regional average observed across Sub-Saharan Africa (71.5).

Gender and energy in DRC

Only 10% of the population have access to electricity. Gender norms restrict women’s access to resources and assets, which directly impacts energy poverty. Because women often do not make decisions alone on household spending, they are likely to have less access to energy products and technologies. Sex-disaggregated data on access to energy as well as women's participation in the sector is absent.

**Page 232**
---

# Uganda

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Uganda has a total population of 34.8 million of which 17.21% are women. The country has one of the world's youngest and fastest-growing populations with 55% of people under 18 years of age and a very high fertility rate of 6.0 births per woman.

The Gender Development Index in Uganda is 0.492 and it ranked 166 out of 189 in Human Development Index, placing the country among other Group C countries with a high absolute deviation from gender parity, of 5–7.5%. This is largely driven by a gender gap in mean years of schooling and gross national income per capita.

Uganda is ranked 131 in the Gender Inequality Index which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women

Uganda has ratified various UN conventions demonstrating a commitment to equity and inclusion, including the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1985, the International Convention on Civil and Political Rights (ICPR) in 1995, and the International Convention on Social Economic and Cultural Rights (ICESCR) in 2003. Regionally, Uganda has also ratified the Maputo protocol, i.e., the African Union (AU) Protocol to the African Charter on Human and People's Rights on the Rights of Women.
