---
**Page 233**
---

in Africa. This protocol specifies provisions for civil, political, economic, social and cultural rights as well as the rights to development and peace, as well as reproductive and sexual rights for women.68

Uganda also compiled a Gender Policy in 1997 (revising it in 2017). The National Policy on the Elimination of Gender-Based Violence (GBV) was adopted by Parliament in 2015 and 2017 respectively (2017) with a commitment to eliminate all forms of gender inequalities in the long-run.69,70 The policy is meant to serve as a guiding framework for all levels of planning, resource allocation and implementation of development programs and projects. The National Policy on GBV was developed based on the analysis that has identified 4 priority areas of focus including:

1. Economic empowerment: The aim are to address three areas of inequality including gender differences in livelihoods, time poverty, and access to and control over productive resources.
2. Promotion and protection of rights: by committing to eliminating gender differences in women’s access to justice and equal enjoyment of human rights.
3. Participation in decision-making and governance: wherein the country aims to overcome customary practices that continue to deny women their full potential, and to increase effectively in leadership and decision-making, low presence of women in technical positions.
4. Gender and Macro-economic Management: The government commits to women’s economic empowerment by ensuring that women have access to credit, land titles, and other resources and domestic/local revenue generation systems and building the overall capacity of the national government to include gender in macroeconomic policies.

The Constitution of the Republic of Uganda, under Article 21, prohibits gender discrimination generally and under Article 33 specifically provides for equality between men and women with respect to marriage and women during marriage and divorce. The Article 33 pertains specifically to the rights of women and requires that the government take all necessary measures to ensure that women have access to education so they can reach their full potential, provide women equal rights to men in political, economic, social activities, and Article 33 also prohibits laws, customs, traditions, and cultures that are “against the dignity of women.”70

While the above measures create a conducive policy environment in Uganda to address gender inequality, restrictive socio-cultural practices continue to confine women’s potential in the country. This has been further exacerbated by COVID-19 during which increased incidences of violence against women have been reported.71

Political participation

With women representing 34.9% of parliamentary seats in Uganda, the country ranks high in Africa with regard to women's political participation68. To ensure the representation of women in local governments (LGs) under the Local Government Act No.20 of 1997, the government has established a quota system based on locally determined priorities72 The Ugandan government under the 1997 LG Act stipulates that women should constitute a third of any local council. This affirmative action has led to women constituting


---
**Page 234**
---

### Social and cultural context

In Uganda, Women’s rights on land have been recognized in the Land Act (Cap 227) and Land Acquisition Act (Cap 228) and the country aims to promote tenure security in women's access to land under their title National Land Policy (NLP). However, despite these laws, women still face challenges in accessing land by law and in joint ownership between the period 2013 to 2017, compared to male dominance in ownership prior to 2012-13. However, the country continues to be a patriarchal and patrilineal society where women's domestic responsibilities are still high and women have limited participation in economic work. Further, research has found that in Uganda, as women attain increased access to social networks and empowerment, they are more likely to experience intimate partner violence from their husbands or former partners. Almost 50% of women report experiencing some form of physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence in their lifetime and about 30% reported experiencing it in the last 12 months. Uganda has developed a National Strategy on GBV, finalized in 2017 with the aim of engaging men and boys in prevention and response to GBV.

### Health

Uganda has a maternal mortality ratio (MMR) of 375 per 100,000 live births (as of 2017) which is extremely high compared to the Sustainable Development Goal target of achieving no more than an MMR of 70 by 2030. The country has a 14-element policy framework for the achievement of the 15th Millennium Development goal, to reduce MMR; the failures in referral policies, inadequacies in healthcare facilities and trained personnel and transportation issues remain. Unsafe abortions account for about 5% of maternal deaths in Uganda. Abortion is illegal in Uganda and termination is allowed only in cases the pregnancy threatens the life of the woman.


---
**Page 235**
---

life of the mother, including in cases of cervical cancer and HIV-positive status. Further, pregnancies from rape, incest, and defacement can be terminated.## Education

In Uganda, men are expected to complete 6.7 mean years of schooling compared to 4.3 years completed by women. As of 2018, 52% of adult males are literate and 71% of adult females are literate. Interestingly, however, among the young population, there is gender parity in literacy levels with 90% of women in the age group of 15-24 being literate and 89% of men attaining literacy which shows an improving trend for the next generation. However, at the secondary level, the lower secondary completion rate is low among both men (27.7%) and women (25.1%) in Uganda.## Under its National Strategy for Girls Education (2015-2019) the country has created a policy implementation Framework to promote girls' education by aligning to harmonize education programs on Girls' Education for effective implementation of the strategy. The framework aims at mobilizing political will, commitment and financial resources to promote girls’ education, institutionalization of Girls’ Education into policy development, and build the capacity of educators to effectively address gender inequalities in education. Uganda has also made significant progress by the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women for the strides made in the education sector including, a particular focus on building infrastructure and providing quality education. The 2015-2019 National Strategy for Girls’ Education and Education Policy (2004) under which physics, chemistry, and biology have been made compulsory in lower secondary schools**, and 75% of government sponsorships in tertiary institutions are for science-based courses. However, the number of women pursuing science at university level are few; hence they are unable to enjoy state-funded science education at this level.## Although the country has introduced key legislation to ensure improved access education for women the country is still failing behind in effectively transitioning women from school into work. The share of women who have been a slow grower over time, falling from 45% of the population aged 15-24, from 74.6% in 2013 to 54.2% in 2017. While some of this decline can be explained by improved access to schooling, the share of inactivity is also influenced by other factors such as women being more likely than men to both be out of school and out of a job due to familial responsibilities and lack of job opportunities.## Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship


---
**Page 236**
---

As of 2018, the formal Labour force participation rate for women in the age group 15 to 64 in Uganda is 69%. As per the Mastercard Global Index of Women Entrepreneurs (2021), all of the business leaders in Uganda, 25.3% are women. Further, women outperform men in undertaking entrepreneurial activity: In 2021 was 39.9% for women compared to 38.1% for men. However, it is crucial to note that 21.9% of women entrepreneurs have no access to finance and only 45.7% are able to access the mentorship that is desperately needed. In the Report also found that there has been a slight decrease in the entrepreneurial activity of women in the country between 2020 to 2021 from 42% to 39.9% which may be an impact of COVID-19.

In salaried employment, there are evident gender gaps in median monthly nominal wages earned by men and women, with women earning $24.1 USD while men earning more than double the income, i.e., $67.52 USD. Further, there are glaring differences in women's representation at the senior management level across sectors. Women are underrepresented in leadership positions in finance, information technology, education and nursing, but have limited representation in financial service industries and IT sectors.

About 83% of Ugandan women are also engaged in unpaid care work, the most common forms being cleaning, cooking, and other household tasks. While the least percentage of persons report engaging in unpaid work is 10%, there was a disproportionately higher percentage of women reporting spending time on this activity (42%) compared to men (22%).

Gender and energy in Uganda

It is widely recognized that consistent and reliable access to energy is critical for both economic growth and social development. Further, access to clean energy can improve health & well-being as well as the status of time poverty. The number of households with electricity access has increased. Over the past decade, Uganda has increased its electricity generation capacity from 600MW in 2010 to 1839MW in 2020. In 2020, 54% of the population had access to electricity compared to only 27.8% of the population with 72.8 % using firewood and 21.4% using charcoal. Further, wood use is 3% higher among female heads of households than male heads. Overall 95% population has access to electricity, with the majority of this being in urban areas. However, only 60% of rural households have access to solar home systems. While 51.8% of women-headed households have electricity access in the country, 59% men headed households have electricity access.

There is high potential for women to be employed in the clean energy sector, as last mile agents generating demand and consumption, which are currently a challenge to be addressed in Uganda. At 100 kWh per capita, Uganda currently has one of the lowest rates of electricity consumption per capita in the world. However, currently, the participation rate of women in Distributed Renewable Energy is 28% of the total workforce in this sector.

| Page 232 |


---
**Page 237**
---

the sector. The highest share of jobs held by women in the DRE sector is in mini-grid technology and women continue to be concentrated in unskilled work, making up 30% of unskilled mini-grid workers. Evidently, women face myriad social and cultural barriers as well as in the DRE sector, reporting workplace discrimination and stating that household responsibilities discourage their effective participation in the sector. On the other hand, there are some organizations that have taken steps to provide training subsidies as installation agents and engineers. While the DRE sector in Uganda is not yet an equal opportunity employer, there is positive progress made towards achieving pay parity, where women are remunerated at 90% of men’s wages for similar work. The Women in Renewable Energy (WiRE) initiative was launched by the Solar Energy Association of Uganda, which aims to build the technical capacity of women in the field of solar, biomass, and wind energy, organizing mentorship programs, and engaging with communities on the adoption of renewable energy.

The country currently has a program to Operationalize the National Content policy to enhance local Content and participation of nationals including women and youth in the oil and gas sector and is planning to launch a project on Climate Resilient Livelihood Opportunities for Women’s Economic Empowerment (CRIWE) in Karamoja and West Nile Regions of Uganda (October 2019 - 30 September 2020).

# Benin

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Benin has a population of 12.8 million people, with a young population of 42% below the age of 14. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.6 which is significantly high compared to other countries in the region.

The Gender Development Index in Benin is 0.890 and the country is placed with Group 5 countries with a high attainment level on gender equality. The country has a low score of 0.312 for female participation rate, a gap in expected and mean years of schooling. Benin has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.602 and is ranked 152, which shows the disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women

Benin has ratified various UN conventions which aim towards ensuring equality and inclusion, including the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW in 1986), and the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC). In this regard, Benin has signed the African Union Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa.

The Constitution of Benin accords equal rights to all to avoid discrimination based on origin, race, gender, religion, political opinion or social status. The country also has a Code of Persons and the Family in the Republic of Benin, the country also sets the legal age of marriage at 18 for both men and women.

promulgates that men and women are guaranteed the same inheritance rights under the law. Further, Berlin has also established a policy framework to realize the legal commitments to action through its National Gender Equality Promotion Policy for the Promotion of Women in Agriculture, and the National Action Plan to Combat Gender-Based Violence. Berlin, under its National Gender Promotion Policy (considered as a model for other German states), has set out 100 concrete measures. However, the implementation strategies laid down under the policy was only articulated in 2015 and is yet to be reviewed and assessed which raises questions on how successful the country's progress is towards the envisioned goal of the policy. Berlin also established a Gender Equality Commission for Women and Girls, as well as other bodies such as a National Commission for the Promotion of Women, a National Institute for the Promotion of Women, and the Ministry of Social Affairs and Minorities.

Although the legal and policy institutionalization in Berlin is robust, policy implementation is replete with enforcement challenges. The lack of political will and the backing of heavily patriarchal social structures still prevent in the country.


Political participation

Women in Berlin remain underrepresented in political positions. In the 2019 legislative elections, only 6% of National Assembly members were women. Similar to this trend, in the 2020 municipal elections, 4.3% of councillors were women and there were four women mayors out of 77 elected representatives. Berlin has now deployed a quota system for its city council elections. The quota is set at 50%, which means that it will be 109 seats for women, at the rate of one woman per electoral district.[12] At the communal counselor level, women had only 3.8% representation in 2020.[12]


Social and cultural context

There is a continued problem of Gender Based violence experienced by women in Berlin with 23.8% of women reporting having been subjected to violence at some time in their lifetime.[14]

There is a wide regional disparity in women's access to land across Berlin. While women in central and southern Berlin have more control over land, for example; 20.7% in Cölln, and 17.8 % in Potsdam, women in other regions have significantly limited access like in Altona: (10.9 %) & Alseron (12%).[14]


Health

Education

The literacy rate among women in Benin is 31% compared to 54% for men (2018) and only 16% of females in primary school complete their education by age 17. The country has made significant progress, with the world's highest percentages of women graduating from Engineering and ICT courses at 54% and 51%, respectively. However, girls are still less likely to be enrolled in secondary education than boys (60% vs. 82%). Benin relies on Literacy and Adult Education which was adopted in 2001 and aimed at reducing the literacy rate of illiteracy among men and women by half between 2001 and 2010; however, World Bank data suggests that between 2002 and 2015, the female literacy rate increased from 34% to 68%, while male literacy rates increased at a similar level even surpassing that of boys. There is an evident gender gap in secondary education enrollment which is 40.9% for girls compared to 34.1% for women (2017). Benin has committed itself to a post-2015 Sustainable Development Plan (2016–2020) to promote the enrollment and retention in education for girls, free school fees at the secondary level for girls. The country has budgeted USD 6.4 million for the year 2019 for this fee waiver for girls. Furthermore, Benin is planning to implement a law by 2020 and 2021 to provide scholarships for promoting girls' enrollment in agricultural and industrial education.

However, to improve the enrollment and retention rate of women at the secondary level, Benin needs to address structural gaps in its education system. The World Bank-supported Global Partnership for Education (GPE) has identified several challenges including a lack of qualified teachers, increasing high administrative burden among staff, slow-moving and highly political decision-making processes that interfere with managerial decisions, improper division of roles and poor coordination across Ministries, rapidly changing priorities, and the need to improve the quality of education in order to ensure learning takes place.

# Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

Benin has seen a growing Labour Force Participation among women in the past 2 decades. While the disparity between men’s and women’s labor force participation (LFP) rate is not as high as it was about 69.3% LFP to 58.4%, women's participation in the economy is largely informal. As per the Enquête Harmonisée Sur le Conditions de Vie des Ménages survey in Benin, 95.5% of women workers concentrated on agriculture and livestock activities. Women are also more likely than men to be among the majority of unemployed people, with 37.5% of 15-24 old being without a job, and 24.6% of them being women.

While the high share of female graduates in Engineering and ICT fields should create a thriving entrepreneurship environment in the country for women, as per data only 29.5% had female directors and 36.7% of the companies were owned by women. Women entrepreneurs also earn less than their male counterparts with an average estimated gross income per capita among women of 2,998$, compared to other Sub-Saharan African nations [1]. Women-led businesses are concentrated in service industries such as accommodation and food services, while men dominate the manufacturing sector. The proportion of female employees is 47% (CIF). Female-headed businesses in Benin remain unregistered with only 13% having certification compared to 21% of men-headed businesses having requisite registration [2].

## Gender and energy in Benin

According to ERI-ESI 2019, 65.4% of households use charcoal compared to 22% for gas and 12.2% for wood, while only 38% cook using electricity. About 44% of all households have access to electricity in Benin [3]. Only 35% of women have access to electricity, compared to 69% of men.

The Energy Ministry in Berlin has prepared a gender profile for the energy sector in 2020 and a 2021-2025 National Action Plan for the Policy for Gender Mainstreaming and Energy Access in Benin and operationalized a Gender and Development Unit to ensure gender mainstreaming across the Ministry’s projects.

ECREEE is implementing a project entitled: Promoting Agricultural Competitiveness through Solar Energy (PAC-Solar) in Benin with the aim to enhance women's access to renewable energy. As part of this project, women are trained on how to install and maintain solar panels regularly, and maintain solar PV installations [4].

However, overall, women in Benin are unable to access training and finance to learn high-skills trades in the energy sector and there continues to be a higher preference for employing and financing men in the energy sector.

# Zambia

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Zambia has a population of over 19 million,[18] of which 50.5% are women, and where over 55% of the population lives in rural areas—the majority of them being women.[19]

Zambia has a comparatively equal development index when compared with other Sub-Saharan African countries, with a Gender Development Index of 0.365. The country is placed with 'Group 2' countries with an absolute gender gap of 48% and a relative gender gap of 7%. Zambia is the only country achieving equality in SDG 4-4 at mean years of schooling with both girls and boys receiving 7.2 years of schooling.[19] The country has a relatively high level of gender parity, but there are still disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.[18]

## Legal status of women

Zambia has a favorable policy framework to advance gender equality in the country. The Constitution (Amendment) Act No. 2 of 2016 provides equal rights to women and men to freely participate in, determine and build a sustainable political, legal, economic and social order. The Constitution also underlines national values and principles that promote gender equality and non-discrimination.[14] Zambia has also enacted the Gender Equality and Equity Act, 2015, to strengthen the legal framework for the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women and girls as committed under the Maputo Protocol; and put in


---
**Page 244**
---

place affirmative action to eliminate all barriers which prevent women from participating meaningfully in all spheres of life and create a conducive environment for such participation. The Act also provides that "an employer shall not discriminate against a woman by dismissing or demitting 17% on the basis of sex, marriage, disability, pregnancy or maternity leave or subject the woman to any other disadvantage in employment or occupation." The Act also prohibits discrimination based on sex in respect of terms and conditions of employment, seniority or social allowances. Progress has also been made with regard to women's human rights with the revision of their penal code in 2005, 2010, and 2012 as per which punishments for sexual harassment have been increased.

As a result of Zambia’s policy environment, the country has made strides across the board with respect to women’s health status, labor force participation, and literacy rate as is highlighted in the following sections.

### Political participation

Women's participation in political and economic spheres in Zambia is low; however, it has continuously risen. The proportion of parliamentary seats held by women increased from 14% in 2011 to 18.9% in 2016, in line with the country’s commitment under the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. At the August 2021 election, women now holding 13% of parliamentary seats and 21% of cabinet positions compared to 17% and 40% of seats in the executive cabinet in 2016. This decline is a result of the recent limited skills and resources available for women candidates. The country has also made progress by requiring political parties to fulfill mandatory quotas for the representation of women within their cadre. Further, the election code of conduct requires political parties to ensure that women are not discriminated against at any point; which dissuades women candidates and also impedes voter turnout among women voters.

### Social and cultural context

There are 73 ethnic groups in Zambia, categorized into three social systems which include matrilineal patrilocal, and bilateral social structures. However, overall, Zambian society is based on patriarchal values and is highly conservative. Zambia has a dual legal system consisting of civil law and customary law, with customary law being the predominant source of law in rural areas. In terms of gender equality, data suggest that Zambia has a very low gender disparity in ownership of a house with 35% of women owning their home alone or jointly and 20% renting it.

The country is ranked 148 out of GSVI, with more than one-third (36%) of women age 15-49 having experienced physical violence at least once since age 15, and 14% have experienced sexual violence. [1]

### Health

---
**Page 245**

The MMR in the country is much lower than in other Sub-Saharan African countries. Maternal mortality rates have been declining over years, from 512 per 100,000 live births in 2013-14 to 322 in 2018 and 84% of deliveries by women took place at health institutions. Further, 97% of women who delivered children in the 5 years preceding the survey received antenatal care (ANC) from a skilled provider. The country has also introduced several policies to improve maternal and child health and promotes women's sexual and reproductive health for girls, and reinforces women's right over family planning. The country has also added Comprehensive Sexuality Education to the school curriculum and there have been efforts to increase access to contraception. The country has also launched training programs for healthcare providers and peer educators in adolescent-friendly sexual reproductive health services in some districts. However, there continues to be an urgent need for family planning among 20% of married women in the country.

Education

As per World Bank estimates, about 83% of adult women in the country are literate and this estimate has been steadily rising over the past 2 decades. This is a result of the country's holistic Girls Education and Women Empowerment (GEWE) project which provides scholarships to girls from extremely poor households with school requisites and fees to enable them to complete secondary education. The project aims to increase access to secondary education for 25,000 adolescent girls coming from extremely poor households. However, the country still faces challenges in increasing access to tertiary education as only about 1% of women and 8% of men attain higher education. However, in an effort to increase women's access to tertiary education, the country has introduced 30% or state-owned bursaries to support women. Further, to encourage women's admission in STEM fields at the tertiary level, the country has reduced the cut-off score for girls for entry into these courses.

Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

As per ILO estimates, the Labour force participation rate for women is at 71%, which is higher than comparable countries in Sub-Saharan Africa. However, women from the country points out a lower wage compared to men. The survey indicates that at the national level, females accounted for a higher percentage of the working-age population (15 years or older) at 52% as compared to males at 47%. However, women are more likely than men to be self-employed and work in agriculture, fishing, forestry, and unskilled labor. The largest proportion of women works in agriculture, followed by sales professions, domestic services, and transport. Women account for only 10% of formal employees while they make up 81% of informal employees in the Agri sector. However, the country has made progress on social security


---
**Page 246**
---

cover to the informal sector including occupational safety, health, and protection for women, the youth, and the disabled.186

Within the agriculture sector, the country has put in place plans and measures to enhance women's participation, productivity and to ensure food security. For instance, the Agricultural Development and Value Chain Enhancement (ADVANCE) Project targets the women and youth in 286 Communities of the country by providing tractors and tillers. Furthermore, the Government has launched the Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CCGAP) which seeks to address gender inequalities across the country's districts, including in funding and programming.187

Gender and energy in Zambia
Zambia has experienced a high rate of deforestation, posing an increasing challenge to women and girls who are primarily responsible for fuelwood collection and need to travel long distances to collect firewood for household use. Women spend on average 20 hours per week and up to 15 days per year collecting firewood. Overall, only 32%89 of population of Zambia has access to electricity.190 However, compared with the national average, women in rural areas are more likely than men to be responsible for energy consumption at 27.6 in 2017. However, this share has seen a slight decline from 29.4%.191

The Ministry of Energy conducted a Gender Assessment of the Energy Sector in Zambia in 2018 and is in the process of developing a Gender Mainstreaming Strategy and Action Plan.192 Zambia's Energy Regulatory Authority (ZERA) has also been working with the United States Agency for International Development (USAID) and Power Africa to provide education and training opportunities and foster a gender-equitable workforce in the energy sector.193

# Mozambique

Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Mozambique is an East African nation that has a population of 33.1 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 34% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.6. [1]

The Gender Development Index in Mozambique is 0.592 and the country is placed with Group 4 - countries with a moderate gender gap (Gender Inequality Index: 0.387 to 0.500). The GDI score for Mozambique is 4 – 5 percent). This is primarily driven by a gender gap in expected and mean years of schooling of 2.4 years among young women aged 15–24 years old. [167] Mozambique has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.337 and is ranked 136, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment. [168]

## Legal status of women

Mozambique has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1987 and signed on the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action in 1995. Further, the country signed The Declaration of Gender in Africa in 2005 and the SADC protocol about Gender and Development in 2008, and the Maputo Protocol.[169] The country also has a conducive framework for women to have access to land and other resources. Women can own property and inherit their family's wealth or enter into themselves for their land use and the 2005 Amended Commercial Code under which women can engage in business without permission from her husbands. Further, under its constitution, the country also formulates a Law on Domestic Violence in 2009 to protect women from abuse and violence.[170]


---
**Page 248**
---

also has a Gender Policy which was approved in 2018 and its corresponding Implementation Strategy was operationalized in the National Action Plan for the Advancement of Women (NAPAW) 2019-2023. The NAPAW aims at achieving promotion of gender equality in all spheres of development of the country, adopting a transversal approach to gender dimension, becoming an instrument of inter-sectoral coordination, to ensure equal opportunities for women and men in all areas of life. In Mozambique, marriage is considered as civil marriage under the country's penal code (only 2 to 6 years) whereas it is 2 to 8 years outside of marriage. While the legal framework of the country creates a mechanism for gender-equal laws to be implemented, the process of implementation has been slow and fragmented. The Gender Policy aims at ensuring equality among women of their rights under the Mozambique Constitution and associated laws.174

### Political participation

Mozambique has a comparatively high representation of women in politics and has a smaller gender gap, compared to even some developed countries, with 42.4% of parliamentarians being women (compared to 37.6 men).175 However, women are still underrepresented in the political process of many local councils and associations. However, the country is far from achieving the target of 50% representation as committed under the Gender Policy. The lack of women leaders in politics has been attributed to a number of factors such as creating a conducive environment for women to participate in the leadership and decision-making bodies of political parties in the country. Even at the municipal level, women leaders have limited representation with only 12 out of 30 mayors being women (40%) and 57 out of 98 councillors being women (59%). In 53 municipalities in 2018, assembly elections.176 However, under the Gender Policy and its Implementation Strategy, the country has put in place a number of measures to ensure equal representation as well as negative measures to ensure the equitable inclusion of women in top positions in the electoral lists of the political parties and groups of citizens and in decision-making bodies. A study by USAID suggests that women (especially younger women) face a lot of party resistance when entering political life and public decision-making.

### Social and cultural context

While legally, men and women are accorded equal rights, the Mozambican society is patriarchal and gender-unequal customs and social order continue to be reinforced across family, community, and institutional levels. Women in Mozambique face a number of challenges that limit their participation in their lifetime.178 However, studies suggest that there is severe underreporting of violence due to the perception that it is a private matter and women being reluctant to use formal mechanisms to report and seek help and most survivors reaching out only to community leaders and families.

### Health

Mozambique has a maternal mortality ratio of 289 per 100,000 live births which is lower than other Sub-Saharan African regions.180 Further, women's agency of their fertility is also reflected in the increasing use


---
**Page 249**
---

Education

The country’s literacy rate for females aged 15 and above is 40%. As per the Ministry of Education and Human Resources’ 2018 survey, girls represent around 46% of the pupils across levels of education. However, there are vast regional differences in educational attainment with gender parity decreasing as one looks at higher grades, across the central and northern provinces of the country (Mimba 2018 as cited in UNICEF 2019). Girls also have lower completion rates than boys, especially for secondary school drop-out rates for girls. This issue of adolescent pregnancy is also prevalent in the country, causing low attendance rates among girls.

Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

As per the Gender Inequality Index, there is a negligible disparity in Labour force participation (LFP) with women’s share at 58%. However, when looking at the quality of employment, the gender gap is evident when one looks at the quality and intensity of employment, with women working predominantly in the informal and subsistence sectors as self-employed or unpaid workers and working fewer hours in productive employment. Women are also less likely to be employed in decision-making positions within their firms (44% for women). Agriculture remains the main sector of employment for both men and women followed by sales

Gender and energy in Mozambique

Currently 30-35% of the population in Mozambique has access to electricity. About 80% of the energy use is from biomass which places a disproportionate burden on women to collect firewood. To expand the country's energy sector, the government launched the Government's National Energy for All Programme in 2016 which targets full access to electricity by 2030.

The share of modern renewables in Mozambique’s final energy consumption has significantly increased between 2014 and 2017 from 18.7% to 30%. Vast gas reserves have been discovered in the Rovuma Basin of Mozambique, which could be a major source of energy for the country as well as exportable natural gas. However, the power, gas, and mining sectors combined have not been able to generate much employment in the extractive industry, creating no more than 1-1% of jobs in 2018.

In the country, there are existing initiatives and organizations that focus on expanding women's representation and participation in the energy sector. Some examples include:

1. The Mozambique Women Of Energy is a platform for women in the energy sector who identify as experts or leaders for gender equality.
2. BRILHO programme, an SNV - Netherlands Development Organisation program in partnership with TechnoServe aims to increase the number of women with access to modern energy solutions in off-grid areas and to improve their economic empowerment by employing women in the sector as collaborators and leading businesses. They do this through technical training of companies on gender-forward company policies and practices

# Sierra Leone

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Sierra Leone is a country in West Africa, on the Atlantic Ocean. The country has a population of 8.3 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 33% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate of the country stands at 3.9 which is somewhat lower compared to some of the other West African nations.

The Gender Development Index in Sierra Leone is 0.893 and the country is placed among 'Group 5' countries with a high absolute deviation from gender parity, of more than 10%. This is primarily driven by a gender gap in education. The UNDP (2022) Gender Inequality Index places Sierra Leone at 0.467 which is one of the highest values in the world. Sierra Leone has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.533 and it ranked 162, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women

Sierra Leone has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1986 and the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990. The country has also ratified the Beijing Platform for Action (1995); UN General Assembly Resolution 52/86 on Crime Prevention and Criminal Justice; the African Union Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol) (2003). Sierra Leone has also committed to the Solema Declaration on Gender Equality in Africa adopted by African Union Heads of States and Governments in 1998. The country is also committed to ensuring that all laws relating to prostitution, brothel keeping, and impurity for sexual violence and ensure that all victims have equal protection under the law. Under Article 15 of Sierra Leone's constitution the foundations for gender equality are laid; with the commitment that


---
**Page 252**
---

". . . every person in Sierra Leone is entitled to the fundamental human rights and freedoms of the individual, that is to say, has the right, whatever his race, tribe, place of origin, political opinion, color, creed or sex." (200)

The country has a solid legal foundation for gender equality with the recent Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment Policy. Sierra Leone has also adopted two national policies: the National Policy on the Advancement of Women and the National Policy on Gender Mainstreaming adopted in 2003. Apart from this, the country has three other Gender Justice Acts, including the Child Rights Act; the Anti-Human Trafficking Act; and the Domestic Violence and Early Wedlock Elimination Act (DVEWEA). The DVEWEA has four broad objectives which it aims to meet through a multisectoral approach.108

* Achieve gender equality and equity in all sectors and enable women to take their rightful position in national, local, and community decision-making.
* Ensure satisfactory quality of life for women and girls (including the elimination of female genital mutilation).
* Eliminate all forms of GBV and discrimination.

Taking cognizance of the need to educate and sensitize men and boys as change agents and allies in changing power relations to champion women's and girls' rights.109 However, a study has indicated that the donor-funded (UNFPA) objective intended approach to male engagement is not being fully implemented by the government and other stakeholders including local organizations. And while the involvement of men towards maternal care is rising dramatically, the training provided to them tends to encourage women's empowerment; may in fact run a risk of reinforcing patriarchal structures that dictate that men are responsible for protecting women, taking the agency away from women.110

Political participation

In Sierra Leone, the position of women's political representation remains dismal with only 12.3% of women MPs being women. Under the Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment Policy, Sierra Leone's cabinet is quoted to ensure equal gender participation in political leadership positions at all levels by setting aside 30% of elective and public offices for women.111 To enforce compliance with this policy, the government has established a National Women's Political Party Registration Commission with the Political Parties Registration Commission, enhancing their statutory powers to penalize parties that fail to comply with quota provisions.112 However, despite these measures, women continue to face various barriers in political participation and leadership roles including violent hostility, intimidation, limited fund availability, low literacy, and confidentiality.113 Further, customary

laws are also in contrast to statutory laws especially at the local levels, with women not being recognized as Chieftains in the Northern Regions and in Kailahun and Kono Districts in the Eastern Region.[23]

### Social and cultural context

The presence of customary laws in Sierra Leone undermines women's position as owners of land tenure. Since the 1970s, when women are patrilineal, women are not entitled to land. And while it is a criminal offense to evict widowed women from their land, there continues to be ignorance about the legal rights of women to land tenure and reported dispossession of land among widows or divorced women.[24] In Sierra Leone, 30% of women have experienced Physical and/or Sexual Intimate Partner Violence in their lifetime.[25]

### Gender-based violence remains a critical area of concern in Sierra Leone, with the President declaring a State of Emergency on rape and sexual violence in February 2019 which led to the amendment of the Sexual Offences Act (SOA) 2007. The SOA was amended by the Sexual Offences (Amendment) Act 2018, the maximum penalty for rape and sexual penetration of a child has been increased from fifteen years to life imprisonment, and provisions have been made for the introduction of prosecution for aggravated sexual assault under this ACT.[26]

### Health

Sierra Leone has one of the world's highest maternal mortality ratios of 1:120 per 100,000 live births as per the UN database, just behind Chad and South Sudan.[27] The country's infrastructure withstands stresses under the best of circumstances. Sierra Leone is ranked last in the World Health Organization's list to take challenges of inadequate health practitioner staff, low skills among health workers, limited availability of medical equipment and supplies, as well as delayed referrals from lower level facilities. Further, while almost all women (98%) have access to a health facility for delivery, only 40% attend antenatal care (ANC) visits.[28] Women's agency to decide their age of marriage and use contraception for family planning is also limited in Sierra Leone with 35% of women aged 15–19 married before the age of 18. 21% of women aged 15-19 had a birth or were pregnant (in 2019) and 25% of married women reported use of family planning, which has not changed between 2013 and 2019. Women also have poor nutritional status with 47% of women aged 15-49 being anemic.[29]

### Education

Literacy rate among women aged 15 and above is only 40% in Sierra Leone. Regionally this is lower than other African countries, with the lowest literacy rates for women in West Africa at 38%. The national literacy rate for men is 67%, while the national literacy rate for women is 29%.[30] Literacy among women aged 15 and above was estimated to be only 40.5% in Sierra Leone, compared with 55% as per World Bank data.[31] The gap is even wider as per the country's Demographic and Health survey, with 62% literacy among women compared with 82% among men. Data from 2015 indicates that

attributed to harassment and abuse from teachers and male students, poor attendance, dropouts, lack of skill in health education, and limited support for mothers. Further, the government has recently imposed restrictions on pregnant girls from attending school curtailing the scope to complete their education which was only recently overturned in 2017.

A UNESCO study cites that in 2017 almost a third of girls who dropped out of school did so because of pregnancy. This finding also highlights an encouraging trend based on analysis of the country's tertiary institution census; the share of women enrolled in tertiary education has increased from 43% to 48%, between 2015 and 2017. The number of institutions offering courses for women (from 69 per 1,000 inhabitants) has halved from 350 to 159. Further, the study points out that a majority of students enrolled in Technical, Vocational Education and Training (TVET) are women (54%), and 39% of Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) courses are also female. The Government has recently introduced a pilot to take STEM studies in contrast with nearly one in three men; and enrolment to a variety of TVET courses is increasing at a faster rate than that for women in other sectors (e.g., health, education, tourism, catering, and housing) compared to men in manufacturing and construction related training. To encourage STEM education, the Government has established a grant-in-aid policy for providing scholarships to all female students.

### Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

While World Bank estimates point to similar LERF among men (58%) and women (59%) aged 15 to 64 in the country’s DHS points to a disparity with man more likely to be employed than women (72% versus 69%). Although the trends around women's tertiary and technical education are encouraging, these don't necessarily translate into employment. The gender pay gap is also significant, with women earning less salaries and discrimination underlining young women's competitive position compared to men. In 2018 for instance, the Employment Service of the Ministry of Labour reported that women earned an average salary of $35 per month versus $47 for men; and the gap widens to 12 percentage points in higher education. Tertiary educated women who are NEET (not in employment, education, or training) is 24% compared to 17% men. However, women with tertiary education are more likely than men to be employed in agriculture, livestock work much more effectively than men; with young female NEETS who receive any formal vocational training relatively more likely to be employed (defined by a state where they are not even looking for work) (50%) than their male counterparts (74%).

Sectorally, women are mainly concentrated in agriculture (54%) and sales (37%) jobs, and are less likely, compared to men, to be in skilled manual work (4% versus 9%) or in managerial/technical/professional roles (17% versus 28%). Women's position at the workplace remains dismal; as an extensively high percentage of women in agricultural work (69%) are unpaid and only 31% are found in non-agricultural work remain unpaid, up from 20%.


---
**Page 255**
---

While there are no recent estimates of women entrepreneurs in Sierra Leone, an IFC study from 2015 estimated 2.1 million women entrepreneurs engaged in economic activities. However, these women entrepreneurs are often constrained by access to finance for business expansion and limited informal credit chance from personal sources such as gifts or personal savings or rely on informal community-based fund mobilization for petty cash through the Self-Rotating Saving and Loan Scheme locally known as cusu.230

Gender and energy in Sierra Leone

Electrification rates in Sierra Leone are low compared to other countries in the region. Only a small portion of the population has access to electricity, with 41% having access to grid power (World Bank, 2020)231 and 23% (DHS, 2020). About 64.4% of cooking fuel comes from wood, and 32.8% is charcoal, putting Sierra Leone at a high risk for indoor air pollution and respiratory diseases.

Sierra Leone has an ECOFADAS and its implementation is critical for gender mainstreaming in Energy Access. Under ECOFADAS, The Ministry of Energy is responsible for supervising the Barefoot Solar Engineering Training Centre, established to undertake rural electrification projects and to provide solar training.232 In 2014, a group of women from the Barefoot Solar Engineering Training Centre of Sierra Leone, started by women trained at Barefoot college India on the installation and maintenance of small PV systems, were able to install solar panels in 50 clinics in rural areas of Sierra Leone. They have been able to play a critical role in last-mile access to energy for households in 32 towns and villages.234 The Barefoot women have also provided solar connections to 60 community clinics in northern Sierra Leone, providing electricity to the clinics and enabling them to provide better health care services at all of these clinics. With solar panels, Barefoot women have been able to ensure cheap, clean, and stable connection for the institutional deliveries at these facilities much safer for women.235 Between 2014 to 2017, 50 solar women entrepreneurs installed 60 PV systems in 50 clinics, generating 98kWp installed in 3,500 households.

Among off-grid energy providers operating in Sierra Leone, the women-led company Lighting Through the girl founded by a woman named Kamara is leading the way in deploying women to provide clean energy access through the distribution and sale of solar products, focusing on the last mile. The company has a 100% female sales agent force and 85% of the entire organization including leadership is made up of women. Starting with a small grant from the World Bank, Lighting Through the Girl used storytelling to build trust in a storytelling approach and provided solar lamps to 400 school children in off-grid communities under their One Child, One Lamp campaign.237

# Guinea

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Guinea is a country in West Africa with vast nature reserves. The country has a population of 13.9 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 34% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country, at 4.4, is very high compared to some other countries in the West African region.[2]

The Gender Development Index in Guinea is 0.850 and the government is placed with 'Group 5' countries with a high absolute deviation from gender parity, of more than 10%. While the country does not have a very high fertility rate compared to some other countries in the region, it has one of the highest rates of high gender gap in mean years of schooling with 1.3 years among females and 3.2 years among males.[3] Guinea has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.621 and is ranked 157, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.[4]

## Legal status of women

Guinea, similar to many other Sub-Saharan and West African countries recognizes customary law along with statutory laws. This, in some areas of social and cultural life, impedes women's ability to exercise their agency over their own lives.[6] In the absence of a legal framework that protects women's rights, customary law grants women 1/8th of wealth and equal rights to land; customary law prevents women from claiming these.[7] On the international arena, Guinea has committed to gender equality through ratification of various UN and regional instruments. These include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1982, the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women to

# Social and cultural context

Customary and religious practices in Guinea severely limit women’s ability to realize their rights as citizens of Guinea. In addition to poverty and families, women's position is undermined under traditional and customary law. Child marriage is also rampant in the country with 48.5% of girls married off before the age of 18.

Even though polygamy is prohibited under statutory law,72 among married women in age 15 to 49, 42% of women are in a polygamous union. The practice of female genital mutilation (FGM) is also widespread with general mutilation (FGM) with 94.5% of women in age 15-49 reporting that they have experienced FGM traditionally.73 In the community, there is a strong belief that girls must be circumcised before marriage and marriage which is ceremonially celebrated. On the other hand, patriarchal norms dictate that women must be chaste before marriage, and forced marriage (especially for widows) is common. At the community level, there is a lack of awareness about FGM and its harmful effects on health.

Health

The state of reproductive agency among women is poor in Guinea. The high rate of FGM highlighted in the previous section is also coupled with inadequate post maternal care women receive. While at 576, Guinea has a lower MMR than some other countries in the region, Guinea has a long way to go to reach the SDG target of 100 deaths per 100,000 live births by 2030. The high rate of maternal mortality is also due to the lack of skilled birth attendants and poor access to health care services in rural areas, leading to delays in seeking care.

In this could be due to a variety of factors, such as high rates of anemia among women, 46% of women in the 15-49 age group are anemic. Limited usage of and access to modern contraception, with 36% of unmarried women reporting that they do not know their family planning needs are unmet and 22% of all women reporting unmet family planning needs.

Education

Guinea has the lowest mean years of schooling among girls in the world at 1.3 years as per Gender Development Index (GDI). As per the country’s DHS, there is a wide gender gap in education between young


---
**Page 259**
---

Page 255

females and males with only 39% literate women in the age group, compared with 65% young men in the same age group.[204] Despite world development indicators, the gender gap ratio of boys to girls significantly is a learning transition from primary to tertiary levels. At the secondary level, it stands at 45: which is less than the Sub-Saharan African region on average.[205] As per the 2018 DHS, 63% males vs 59% females attend primary school and 70% males vs 64% female in secondary school. In rural areas, only 11% of girls attend secondary school vs 18% of boys.[206] The country has rural second-chance education schools known as NAFCA centers that cater to school dropouts who are unable to complete their studies and then provide them with vocational training skills and as of 2019 there were 13,000 students enrolled in these centers.[207]

### Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

As per the country’s DHS, the Labour force participation rate among women is 60%, while among men it is 81%. Agriculture remains the main sector of employment for both men and women. Interestingly however, unlike a lot of other Sub-Saharan African nations, 64% of women in Guinea are self-employed in agriculture instead of working as family members on farms. However, only 29% of female entrepreneurs have access to finance compared with 50% of male entrepreneurs.[208] A percentage points less productive than men in Guinea due to disparity in land ownership and men being much more likely to own livestock and machinery that can be used for high-quality agricultural work; women there has been a steep decline in the proportion of those employed in agriculture from 52% to 37%. There has been a transition of women from agriculture to sales professions, with 32% of women working in sales and services.

Culturally entrepreneurship is not seen as a respectable work avenue for women in Guinea.[209] Regionally, Guinea is much behind average in terms of firms entrepreneurship with only 1% of firms having female founders, compared to 29% in the Sub-Saharan African region, as per the 2016 World Bank Enterprise Survey (the survey sample size was 350 enterprises and the number of women-owned enterprises are largely concentrated). Further, about 25% of enterprises in the country had no female employees.[210]

As per Global Findex women are behind men by 13 points in bank account ownership, with 24% of women having accounts, compared to 37% of men. Similarly, in terms of mobile money account holding, women are behind men by 9 percentage points and only 58% of women have access to financial services.[211] There has been significant growth of MFIs and fintech in the country which could be way to tackle gendered norms and empower women financially; tedious KYC processes, power connections in the hinterland, and weak

Gender and energy in Guinea

Currently, only 44% of households in Guinea have access to electricity, and only 5% of the population has access to clean cooking technologies.[20] In Guinea, cooking is predominantly done outside the home and 60% of households used wood for cooking.[21]

Among notable initiatives in the country, the Women’s Fund for Gender Equality (FGE) developed a training program on solar energy and irrigation systems. The project was implemented by the FGE in partnership with a local grass-roots organization Partenariat - Rechercheur - Environnement - Médias (PREM), started in 2013 to promote the use of renewable energy sources, particularly solar energy, starting from mango plants using solar polytunnel dryers. This resulted in 25,000 mango trees being planted and greenhouse gas emissions being reduced by 40 tonnes as a result of solar drying technology, between 2013 to 2016.[22]

Currently, there is an ongoing Research Project on Energy Transition for Women’s Economic Empowerment through the Horticultural Value Chain in Guinea, to understand and address mechanisms for use of solar irrigation systems in the horticultural value chain, for irrigation, processing, and preservation. The project aims at increasing access to energy services and improving women’s economic empowerment by building the capacity of women to use solar technologies and access finance. The program is being implemented by the initiative prospective agricole et rurale (IPAR) in collaboration with the Centre d'étude et de coopération internationale (CECI) and is funded by the International Development Research Centre (IDRC).[23]

# Burundi

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Burundi is a country in East Africa, south of the Equator. The country has a population of 12.6 million people and one of Africa's highest population densities. Burundi has a young and adolescent population of 52% between the age group of 10 to 29. The fertility rate in the country stands at 5.1.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) is a composite index that measures gender gaps in three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, and the labour market. Burundi ranks as one of the lowest performers among Group F countries with medium equality in HDI achievements between women and men, with an absolute deviation between 5% and 7.5%. Although there is a gender gap in mean years of schooling, with 2.3 years among females and 3.9 years among males, the country has a high female literacy rate (68%). The country ranks lowest on national income per capita, compared to other East African countries ($668 for women and $797 for men). Burundi has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.5124, which is ranked at 177; the index mainly compares between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women

Several national and international policy instruments are in place in Burundi to advance gender equality. Burundi ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1985, the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights (ACHPR) in 2003, and the African Union (AU), the AU's Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment 2018-2030 (GEWE) strategy is part of Burundi's regional regulatory framework. The GEWE strategy focuses on four key pillars, namely:

* Maximizing opportunities, outcomes and E-TECH dividends
* Digital inclusion and access
* Effective laws, policies, and institutions

* **Leadership, voice, and visibility**<sup>289</sup>
The Burundian government also adopted the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the Sale of Children, Child Prostitution, and Child Pornography in November 2007, reinforcing the legal protection for children.

Within the sub-region, the Pact on Peace and Security in the Great Lakes region was adopted during the International Conference on the Great Lakes region in 2005, which established measures to address violence against women and children through its Protocol on the Prevention and Suppression of Sexual Violence in Conflict<sup>290</sup>.

Regarding the national legislation, the Constitution of the Republic of Burundi (2005, Art.13) recognizes gender equality stating that "All Burundians are born equal in mind and dignity. All citizens enjoy the same rights and are entitled to the same protection by the law. No Burundian shall be excluded from the social, economic or political life on account of sex."<sup>291</sup> The 2005 Constitution also prohibits discrimination "in all areas of public and private life".<sup>292</sup> The Public Administration Statute (2006) prohibits discrimination in recruitment, pay or dismissals based on sex, in the civil service Labour Code.<sup>293</sup> Recently (2020 – 2021), Burundi mandated equal remuneration for work of equal value<sup>294</sup>.

**Political participation**

The 2005 Constitution sets a quota of 30% to ensure the participation of women in decision-making bodies such as the National Assembly and government (Art. 129 and 164) to address women's underrepresentation in public life<sup>295</sup>. This milestone was achieved through the leaders of women's associations, who spent time on the Gender Agenda<sup>296</sup>. According to the World Economic Forum Gender Gap report, women hold 38.2% of the seats in Parliament and 37.5% of the ministerial positions.<sup>297</sup> Burundi had its first women Prime Minister, Sylvie Kinigi, in 1994.

**Social and cultural context**

Burundi is considered as a patriarchal society where women have very little decision-making power compared to men. These norms determine power dynamics between men and women from an early age and are reflected in the high rates of physical and economic exploitation of women.<sup>298</sup> Women's primary responsibilities remain domestic, including child-rearing and household chores<sup>299</sup>, resulting in a disproportionate amount of the burden of domestic work, leaving them with little time to accomplish anything else.

Women's rights over land are governed by customary law, which discriminates against women. In the context of patrilineal rules that prevail among the Burundian, "women do not inherit the land; they are only usufructuaries and their right to use it depends on the goodwill of their parents, brothers and sometimes their in-laws."300 This is particularly problematic for women because it leaves them unprotected in times of hardship.301

GBV is a pervasive problem in Burundi. Nearly close to half of all women in the country (46.7%) have suffered intimate partner physical and/or sexual violence.302 Domestic violence "is a mainstream expectation" among Burundian men, and it is often justified as being necessary for maintaining order and as a good thing; it reflects the woman's status as being inferior.303 Economic violence is also common in Burundi. It is exerted by prohibiting victims from accessing or using household assets, the crops harvested by them and, consequently, their access to health care, employment, and excluding them from the household expenditures decision-making.

### Health

Women of reproductive age (15-49 years) often face barriers with respect to their sexual and reproductive health and rights. In 2017, 39% of women had their need for family planning satisfied with modern methods. The maternal mortality ratio in Burundi is estimated at 680 deaths per 100,000 live births or 45 deaths per 1,000 in 2011. HIV/AIDS is also a problem that disproportionately affects women in Burundi. There are 85,000 people in Burundi living with HIV, from which 45,000 are adult women and 30,000 are adult men.

### Education

Gender norms in Burundi limit women's access to continued education, resulting in a higher rate of illiteracy in women. While 68% of women aged 15 and above are literate, only 47% of their male counterparts are literate. Women who have completed primary school produce less food and report lower levels of wealth than those that assume. Girls are also expected to look after their younger siblings and help with domestic and agricultural work.304 Young women are more likely to be married off early, while young men have time for studying and gaining vocational skills. Girls are far more likely to be withdrawn from schools and retained at home. Some reasons for girls' premature withdrawal from school

### Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

Women in Burundi play an active role in the agricultural sector, which provides 90% of the country’s food production. They are responsible for most of the agricultural labour and carry out 70% of farm work. Despite women producing most of the food crops used for family consumption, they often do not have the decision-making power over the use of the harvest.[308]

Women are highly involved in informal trade, particularly in rural areas, which generally require low qualifications and little capital investment and commerce sector, which are better paid[317]. Further, Burundian women are expected to carry out a disproportionate amount of the burden of household work, limiting their opportunities to find employment or start a business.

Regarding laws that promote or hinder women's entrepreneurship, there are no differences between the opportunities for men and women. Women in Burundi since they can sign a contract, register a business and open a bank account on unequal terms. However, [Burundian law] still does not prohibit discrimination in access to credit based on gender, hindering women’s ability to pursue entrepreneurial activities.[312]

### Gender and energy in Burundi

Only 11.7% of the population in Burundi has access to electricity[309], a rate four times lower than the average in East Africa (48%) [310]. And access to clean and modern energy in Burundi remains extremely low.[311] Almost all cooking relies on traditional fuels (95% of the time), and around 90% of rural households depend on conventional three-stone stoves.[312]

Gender norms and gender dynamics play an important role in purchasing energy-related products and appliances. Women are often responsible for the purchase of users of energy appliances, and the use of fuels and technologies may directly impact their health. However, they may not be able to decide to buy cleaner energy technologies since many of them do not have control over their earnings and household purchases.

Some of the gender-focused programs implemented in the region include the Luminor program, implemented by local NGO FVS-Amade in partnership with UNICEF. Active since 2015, FVS-Amade works with Village Savings and Loan Associations (VSLAs) to serve the last mile consumers with clean energy[313].

products. UNICEF subsidizes around 60% to 70% of the purchase price, the purchase of solar lanterns by the NGO FVVS Amade which sells them either directly to households or via VSLA. By replacing battery lamps, solar lanterns can reduce indoor air pollution and improve lighting, and contribute to women's health by preventing them from inhaling kerosene fumes. In addition, the program also positively impacts women since VSLA model can help increase access to financial services among women.

In 2020, the Republic of Burundi received a grant amounting to USD 100 million from the World Bank for the Solar Energy Access Project (SEAP). The project aims at increasing access to solar energy. The Assistance facility has a significant gender focus, highlighted below:

- **Education:** Establishing a scholarship program and capacity development in the energy sector: Establishment of a 100% scholarship program for 40 women studying STEM-related courses, supported by the General Director for Energy, who wish to join the energy workforce after graduation. Further, the project will support the establishment of a gender focal point at the national level and at least one in the sector.

- **Establishment:** Enabling 30% employment of women in overall jobs via mini-grid development by supporting private sector companies in mapping out relevant employment opportunities, such as during construction, and O&M (i.e. solar panel cleaning and small-scale gardening activities) and providing training to women on these activities for the purpose of job creation.

- **Entrepreneurship:** To foster female entrepreneurship in the sector: 20% of start-up grants are to be earmarked specifically for women entrepreneurs. Further, a gender-sensitive tendering process ensuring targeting female business associations (such as Association des Fermes d'Affaires du Burundi) would be hosted and business skills development support would be provided to women entrepreneurs.

- **Women customers:** Differentiated subsidies will be provided to companies in order to target female consumers and ensure that the products are affordable for low-income, vulnerable or disadvantaged households. Further, women's preferences and affordability constraints will be mapped at the household and enterprise level, followed by an assessment of the positive impact on women as a result of adopting off-grid products (in terms of time savings, drudgery reduction, and livelihoods).

Togo

Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Togo is a country in western Africa. The country has a population of 8.7 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 32% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.1.###



The Gender Development Index in Togo is 0.849, and the country is placed in 'Group S', countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men, with a high absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10%. This is primarily driven by a gender gap in mean years of schooling, with 3.4 years among boys compared to only 2.5 years among girls (the difference being 0.9 years). The Gender Inequality Index (GII) stands at 0.186; the index measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.###

Legal status of women

In the international arena, Togo ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1983 but has not yet ratified the Optional Protocol on violence against women. The country also ratified the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 2005,###



The Beijing Platform for Action was adopted by Togo in 2006.###



Togo incorporated the principle of gender equality in the Constitution in 1992, which states: "All human beings are equal in dignity and in right. The man and the woman are equal before the law. No one may be favoured or disadvantaged for the reason of their familial status."


---
**Page 267**

Page 263

ethnic, or regional origin, of their economic or social situation, of their political, religious, philosophical or other convictions (Art. 2).

Following the recommendations of CEDAW, Togo enacted the reforms in 2012 and 2014 to the Persons and Family Code, making significant progress towards gender equality. Before the reform, the Family Code restricted women's rights by not granting a woman an equal right to choose where to live, to get a job without her husband's permission, and to be named head of household. However, despite these progressive reforms, businesswomen in Togo still face many challenges. The government set forth its vision for gender equality with the enactment of a new Labor Code in 2021, that no longer broadly prohibits the dismissal of pregnant women leading to a decrease in its WBL score from 84 in 2017 to 63 in 9 in 2020.

Political participation

Over the years, the participation of women in politics has increased, with women occupying high-level government positions including the Prime Minister's Office, the National Assembly, and the Ministry of Defense. However, women remain under-represented at the upper levels of power and decision-making: in the Executive Council, 10% of the members are women; in the National Assembly, women hold only 7% of the National Assembly seats and 31% of the ministerial positions. Togo currently has a woman Prime Minister, Victoire Dogbé Gbagbi, who has held the position since September 26, 2020, and is the first woman to hold this office.

Social and cultural context

Despite the progress made in recent years in top legal status of women and their political participation, culture, religion, and strong traditions still allow discriminatory practices against women. While the practice of child marriage has declined in recent years, this practice still occurs in some regions of the country, particularly among ethnic groups such as the Mina, who continue to marry girls at a young age (18% of women). This practice is partly due to cultural factors such as the dowry that can be an economic motivation for low-income families to get their girls married off early or for strengthening bonds between families. The law prohibits child marriage and sets the minimum age of marriage at 18 years, and the Law on Reproductive Health 2007 prohibits forced marriage (Art. 11, 2007).

The Civil Law in Togo establishes that marriages can be monogamous or polygamous (Personal and Family Code, Art 42). Women still face discrimination regarding family laws in Togo; for instance, women have to wait until their husband's death before they are allowed to remarry. The law does not provide explicitly addressing domestic violence in Togo. Widows also face discrimination and violence in Togo. Despite existing provisions under the civil law for widows that prohibit granting mourning rites, such as tests to make a woman prove she didn't kill her husband, their delay access to food and income are

Page 204

generating activity, and prohibit widows from inheriting from their husbands or fathers under customary law in Togo (Art. 19). Civil law provides both women and men equal rights to inheritance (Personal and Family Code), although it also stipulates that these rights may be waived if the spouse opts out for customary rules in matters of succession (Art. 404). Further, civil law grants equal rights and access to land among women and men. However, despite this legal framework, gender-based violence remains a serious issue, with traditional practices relating to land ownership vary across ethnic groups. In some cases, women can only purchase land through a man; while in others, women are excluded from ownership altogether.

Gender-Based Violence (GBV) is another factor that hinders gender equality. In Togo, 21% of ever-partnered women have suffered physical partner violence at least once during their lifetime. The Togolese government's efforts to reduce the risk of GBV include the adoption of the Politique Nationale de l'Equité (National Equality Policy) in 2013 and the National Strategy for the Prevention and Control of GBV, which aims to create an economic environment favorable to the achievement of gender equality and equity in Togo; and to address forms of violence against women. FGM is also prohibited in the country since 1986 through Act No. 98-015, which criminalizes it as a harmful practice and prohibits it under the Law on Reproductive Health 2007 and the Child Code 2004.

Despite the existence of a legal framework for FGM, there is still a lot of work to be done in terms of education and sensitization. A government study found that more than 20% of the population believes that it is acceptable and justifiable for a man to be violent against his wife.36

Health

Togo's national health policy, adopted in 1986 and revised in 1997 includes improving women’s health and children’s access to primary health care by providing improved geographical coverage as one of its key objectives. Togo started taking into account the reproductive health component, and the gender dimension of health services delivery at the national level following recommendations of the International Conference on Population and Development held in Cairo in 1994.

Despite having equal rights to use contraception, women of reproductive age (15-49 years) in Togo face barriers to accessing sexual and reproductive health information and services. In 2017, only 29% of women used modern methods of contraception compared with 83.6% of men. The contraceptive prevalence rate for
