In recent years, Togo has increased its efforts to fight HIV/AIDS due to its rapid spread. In the country, 1.9% of the total population (% of the population ages -15-49) has HIV, of which 65% are women. As part of these efforts, the National AIDS Programme (NAPS) was reinforced in September 2001 with a permanent secretariat and a budget of $3 million. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the United Nations Programme on HIV/AIDS (UNAIDS) has supported Togo in its fight against HIV/AIDS.

Education

One of Togo's most significant challenges in terms of education is reducing the existing gender gaps. Only 50% of women aged 15 and above are literate, compared to 80% of men. Many girls drop out of school before completing their primary education, especially those from poor families or girls. According to UNESCO, the lower secondary completion gap between boys and girls is 10.7, more significant than the gap in the Sub-Saharan Africa aggregate, which is 4.2.

The Togolese government has made progress in reducing the gender gaps through the implementation of initiatives aimed at improving access to education for girls. These include:

- Togo joined the Global Partnership for Education in 2010 and received support to implement measures that would help reduce the existing gender gap, including addressing social norms affecting girls throughout their education to strengthen their school enrollment.

Under this initiative, Togo received a $7 million COVID-19 grant from the World Bank, one of which was used to provide financial assistance for 250,000 students in 34 schools through sensitization programs on early marriage, gender-based violence, early pregnancy and other issues affecting girls.

The country has also developed an education strategy for 2014 to 2025 to "develop a quality basic education to achieve universal primary education by 2022."

Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

In Togo, women's participation in the labor force has remained roughly the same since 1990. In 2021, women's labor force participation rate was 55.5%, compared to 59.4% for men. However, women are


---
**Page 270**
---

primarily engaged in the informal sector, where 90% of workers are women, which directly impacts their ability to access the benefits of formal employment.

In the past decade, Togo made significant progress in supporting women's entrepreneurship and economic empowerment. The country has enacted reforms under all eight indicators in the WBL index (Mobility, Togo has made significant progress since 1970—the first year covered by the Women, Business, and the Law [WBL] index). The country has enacted reforms under all eight indicators in the index (Mobility, Workload, and Gender Equality). One of the most significant reforms was the enactment of the 2006 Labor Code that prohibited the dismissal of pregnant workers. This law also prohibits employers from discriminating against women in terms of wages or benefits.

One of the latest setbacks regarding labor laws was enacting a new labor code that no longer broadly prohibits the dismissal of pregnant workers.

Under one of Togo's most recent reforms, women were granted equal access and participation in all spheres of life, including economic life. This right allows women and men to open individual bank accounts and access formal credit. However, despite these reforms, there are gender gaps in access to financial services and products because of barriers that women typically face, such as lack of collateral. Married under the marriage community of property regime are prohibited from providing their own assets for loans.

Regarding leadership and entrepreneurship, according to the Global Gender Gap report 2023 report, only 11.4% of the firms in Togo have female top managers, and 15.7% have a majority of women ownership. The same report highlights that Togo has the highest share of women in senior roles with 70.1%. Further, the percentage of women business owners has increased in the last few decades, from 22% in 2014 to 30% in 2020.

Women in Togo spend disproportionately more time on unpaid care work than men, which negatively impacts the time they can spend pursuing formal economic activities. As of 2016, women entrepreneurs in Togo spent an average of 5 hours per week on care work compared to 8 h for male entrepreneurs.

### Gender and energy in Togo

Only half (54%) of the Togolese population has access to electricity. The country has a relatively diversified energy mix, and more than 13% of its final energy consumption comes from renewable supplies of energy, mainly hydropower. In Togo, as in other countries of West Africa, women are the most vulnerable to the negative impacts of energy poverty because they bear the primary responsibility related to the use of polluting fuels they are responsible for cooking. This burden also limits their participation in other activities, especially income-generating activities, causing a vicious circle by not having the means or


---
**Page 271**
---

The decision-making to invest in clean energy products that can allow them to improve their living conditions and that of their families.

Among notable initiatives is the sector, Entrepreneurs du Monde (EdM) is an initiative supported by the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) and the government of France, created in 1988. This works with people living in fuel poverty by facilitating access to essential goods and services for their families. Based in Togo since 2012, EdM runs the Mivo Energie project that aims to combat global warming and deforestation caused by traditional biomass use while providing clean energy solutions at an affordable price point for vulnerable populations, especially women.

Under the CIZO programme, the Government of Togo (GoT) has granted licenses to two companies to provide solar home systems in the country. The GoT is an off-grid solar energy provider, with a focus on providing solar home systems and cooking solutions through a Pay-As-You-Go (PAYG) model. Recently Bboxx secured an $11 million loan from the International Finance Corporation (IFC) to expand its operations in Togo by offering clean energy and support smallholder farmers in Togo to access solar-powered irrigation.

One of the key pillars of the company is Female Empowerment through the provision of affordable and safe alternatives to traditional biomass. Bboxx also provides training for women on how to use the technology, as well as providing disaggregated data from customers and analyze their repayment rates. In other countries such as Nigeria, Bboxx is also working with local partners to train women entrepreneurs in building businesses and leveraging local women's workforces across their distribution networks to reach target regions.

# Malawi

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Malawi is a South-East African country bordering Mozambique, Zambia and Tanzania. The country has a total population of 20.2 million people, with a demographic division skewed toward the young and adolescent population (45% are aged between 15 to 39 years old) and women make up 54% of the population. The fertility rate in the country stands at 3.9 which is very high compared to regional averages.[**77**]

The Gender Development Index in Malawi is 0.968 and the country is placed among 'Group 2' countries with an absolute deviation from gender parity between 2.5 percent and 5 percent. The disparity in mean years of schooling between men and women is 4.1 years, which is higher than any other country in the region.[**79**] Malawi has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.554 and is ranked 142, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.[**80**]

## Legal status of women

Malawi's constitution of 1994, upholds gender equality, prohibiting gender-based discrimination under Article 20 and accord women the same rights as men to enter contracts, maintain property, retain citizenship and custody of children. Further, the Constitution principally outlines gender considerations for national policy making in all spheres of society with a view to: (i) full participation of women in all spheres of Malawian society on the basis of equality with men; (ii) the implementation of the principles of non-discrimination and such other measures as may be required; and (iii) the elimination of any form of discrimination against women, including but not limited to violence or the person, lack of maternity benefits, economic exploitation and rights to property.[**82**] The country has also demonstrated its commitment to furthering gender equality by ratifying international treaties such as the:

Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1987 and the CRC: Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1991. Further, Malawi is also a signatory to The Protocol to the African Charter Human and People's Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (2005), The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPFA), and the Southern African Development Community (SADC) Protocol on Gender and Development.

The country has adopted the Gender Equality Act as a Law in 2013 with the aim to “promote gender equality, women’s empowerment, influence, development, dignity, opportunities, for men and women in all functions of society, to prohibit and provide redress for sex discrimination; harmful practices and sexual harassment; and to ensure that women have equal access to justice.” The Act also provides a “right for connected matters” with a focus on Human Rights, Employment, Education, Sexual and Reproductive Health and Civic Awareness.382

Malawi’s Ministry of Gender Children, and Social Welfare consolidated a National Gender Policy in 2015, with the aim to address gender inequalities in priority areas including: education and training; health; agriculture; food security; and nutrition; natural resources; environment; and climate change management; economic development; governance and human rights. The following objectives have been identified by the policy:

1. To advocate for increased access, retention, and completion to quality education for girls and boys.
2. To ensure women, men, boys, and girls’ sexual and reproductive health rights, and HIV AIDS status are addressed.
3. To strengthen gender mainstreaming in all sectors of the economy.
4. To reduce poverty among women and other vulnerable groups (Orphans, widows, PLHA, persons with disabilities).
5. To promote women’s participation in decision-making positions in both politics and public life; vi.
6. To ensure access to justice for women.

The policy commitment is complemented by a Monitoring and Evaluation Plan and has been developed in alignment with various other national policies such as the country's larger Growth and Development Strategy, National Health Policy, Education Policy, Agricultural and Environmental policies as well as the National Youth Policy.384

Political participation

Malawi boasts of electing the second African female president, Joyce Banda (from 2012-2014). However, the country still lags behind in women’s political participation.385 As per the Global Gender Gap report, 23% of the parliament is composed of women; compared to 71% of men. The gap widens for ministerial leadership, with 17.29% of women and 62.61% of men holding ministerial positions.386

Studies have suggested that Malawian voters view women as less credible leaders than men, expecting women to conform to gender norms; however, women are also held to a much higher standard than men parliamentarians in terms of their religious and moral values. Women are also more likely to be excluded from party leadership roles, the "women's wings" which do not have a clear mandate within political parties. Further, women candidates face discrimination, negative campaigning, and increasing expenses during campaigning, which causes high turnover rates among women candidates. The result is yet another affirmative action on women’s participation in politics, with no legislative quotas for women’s representation.

## Social and cultural context

Malawi's social life is dictated by customary law which is patriarchal in tradition. While the country has areas with matrilineal communities, social norms across these still continue to favor men. Women also have limited access to land and other resources. Women are less likely than men to be employed in formal jobs and are also smaller than that of men (by 12%) with 25% low productivity as a result of limited access to farm inputs. The incidence of violence against women is very high in Malawi with 37.5 % of women having experienced physical or sexual violence at some point in their lives. The rate of female genital mutilation has gradually risen between 2004 to 2016 from 15% to 33%. This continues even as the country's National Plan of Action (NPA) on Violence against Women and Children (VAWC) was launched in 2007. The Child Protection Act and the Domestic Violence Act (2008) underlines the penalization of violent offenses against women.

The country also has one of the world’s highest rates of child marriage, with 42% of girls married before the age of 18. The National Plan of Action on Violence Against Women and Children (NPA-VAWC) launched in 2007 by the country has developed a National Strategy to Eliminate Child Marriage, which aims to reduce the prevalence of child marriage through awareness raising campaigns, education, and legal reforms. This is to be coordinated by a multi-sectoral task force and through the establishment of community child protection committees, community victim support units (CVSUs), Community-Based Child Care Centres (CBCCs) and Children Comers (CCs).

The maternal mortality rate in Malawi stands at 348 per 100,000 live births.[59] The country has seen great progress in expanding women's access to institutional care, and as of per the country’s last DHS, Malawi has a very high rate of institutional deliveries, increasing significantly from its 55% in 1992 to 97% in 2016.[60] Although only half (51%) of women had at least 4 ANC check-ups in their last pregnancy,[61] further, as per the country’s most recent DHS, 83% of births were attended by skilled health personnel, with 79% delivered at home or in private facilities while 28% were assisted by doctors or clinical officers.[59] There is also high access to family planning methods among women, with a lower unmet need reported among all women (13%) as compared to other countries in the region. However, only about half of married women aged 15–49 years report having used any method and only one-third report making a joint decision regarding family planning methods with their husbands.[62] However, there has been no significant change in the proportion of women who are currently living with HIV as of 2018.[63] The incidence of HIV among women in Malawi is high (27.5%), and HIV remains one of the leading causes during pregnancy, they are the leading cause of death among girls of age 15–19 in Malawi.[64]

Education

Malawi has a universal primary education policy that is free as of 2003.[65] As per the Integrated Household Survey (IHS), 87% of females are literate.[66] The high incidence of child marriage among Malawians also reflects attitudes toward girls’ education. Among dropouts from secondary school, 32% of females mentioned marriage being the primary reason they had to drop out.[67] There is a gender gap of 6.87 percent in the country’s literacy rate as per the most recent DHS (2015–19). [68] According to the World Bank report (2022),[69] Access to higher education beyond secondary schooling is limited in Malawi among both men and women, with only 3.7% of females having tertiary education qualifications, and men having a somewhat higher rate of tertiary education at 2.7%.

The country has developed a long-term National Education sector Investment Plan to improve enrollment and quality of education in the country and a particular objective is to increase access to equity in secondary education with a focus on girls’ education.[70] The Ministry of Education, Science and Technology (MoEST) introduced affirmative action by mandating 50-50 selection criteria in secondary schools, construction of girl’s hostels, provision of burarisies, and provision of health, nutrition, physical education, and sports. In Technical, Entrepreneurship, Vocational Education and Training, a code of conduct has been introduced to combat gender-based violence (GBV).[71]

The Ministry of Labour, Youth, Sports, and Manpower Development (MoLYSMD) has also released The Malawi TEVET Policy, and the first priority area under this policy is to increase access and equity in Malawi’s TVET system with a focus on girls’ education.[72] As per the World Bank report (2015),[73] 48% of students are female. ### Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

While Malawi has a comparatively high Female Labor Force Participation in the sub-region, their conditions of work are far from ideal. As per the country’s Fifth Integrated Household Survey (2019-20), 91.6% of women aged 15 years or older were economically active compared to 87.4% for men. The highest proportion of women (81.7%) in Malawi have engaged in agriculture or fish culture and wage work is the second most common activity with only a 6% share among employed women. Wage work is more common (10.6%), and casual employment is also a larger proportion among women (58%) than men (47%). Unpaid work is also more common among women, with the 2015-16 DHS revealing that 59% of women in Malawi were engaged in unpaid work compared to only 38.5% for men.

In terms of earnings, women earn less than men in all sectors except agriculture and livestock; with women earning only €4 cents per dollar paid to men. The Integrated Household Survey also finds that across the board, women wage earners work fewer hours, and spend more time on non-market activities such as unpaid care work.

As per the Global Gender Gap report, only 14.2% of firms in the country have female top managers, and only 11.4% of firms have female majority ownership. While women entrepreneurship is proportionally high in Malawi compared to other countries in sub-Saharan Africa, there are still significant barriers limiting their productivity. As per IHS 5, the most common form of non-farm enterprise in Malawi is in the Wholesale and retail trade; Accommodation, and food service sectors. A higher percentage of enterprises in the transport and storage sector are owned by women compared to men. Women also hold a higher proportion in the transportation and storage, Information and communication sector, men hold a higher proportion of enterprises in agriculture, manufacturing, and construction.

In terms of business management, sales managers: sales are about 65% less than those of male managers, however, the difference reduces to 45% when controlled for household and enterprise-level factors which indicated that return on resources is the major reason for this gender gap. Women in Malawi are more likely to use saved-up agricultural income as start-up capital; women rely on gifts from friends or family.

### Gender and energy in Malawi

As per World Bank Data, a very small minority of households in the country have access to electricity (14.9%) as of 2020. As for the country’s IHS, 75% of the population use firewood as the main source of fuel.

In Malawi, women have been at the forefront of various private sector and policy advocacy initiatives to expand access to solar energy. A notable example is the project implemented by Christian Aid in empowering women in Karonga district, Malawi, to advocate for increased rural electrification and increased job opportunities and incomes for women. In 2019, women from Breaking the Barriers engaged with the Malawian government to lobby for a tax amendment that would remove import duty on solar products.

FINCOOP is a financial cooperative that provides affordable financial services, serving micro- and medium-sized enterprises (MSEs) in rural areas of Malawi. FINCOOP targets low-income households across Malawi using multiple channels such as Village Savings and Loan Associations (VSLAs), civil services, and technology partners. FINCOOP has tailored its payment terms to fit women's needs by offering flexible repayment options, including the use of Go mobiles, cash, and group loans, and by offering longer repayment terms. From 2018 to 2020, more than 1212 female-headed households were able to access solar products through this program.

# Lesotho

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in Lesotho

Lesotho is a landlocked country in Southern Africa. The country has a total population of 2.2 million people, with a young and adolescent population (in the age group 10-24) of 29%. The fertility rate in the country stands at 3 per woman, which is lower compared to other countries on the continent.

The Gender Development Index in Lesotho is 0.585 and the country is placed among Group 1 countries with high gender equality (the average for Group 1 is 0.627). The country has a female literacy rate of 94% (versus 83.5 percent). Lesotho is the only country among those categorized as low Human Development Indexed countries that has a female literacy rate higher than its male literacy rate (94% versus 83%). Women are more likely to be employed in the informal sector and earn less income than males (0.6 years).

Lesotho has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.557 and is ranked 144, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women

Lesotho achieved democracy in 1993 and the country's constitution's Bill of Rights enshrines non-discrimination and equality with respect to legislation, governance, sustainable development considerations, and democratic institutions of the State. The country, like many others in the region, has a pluralistic legal system, recognizing customary law. Roman-Dutch Law (Common/Civil Law) along with Statutory Law is recognized as the primary source of law. The Constitution of Lesotho 1993, as amended, provides equal rights to men and women; customary and common laws often relate women to minority status. Lesotho has ratified a number of international instruments on human rights and gender equality rights including The Beijing Declaration and its Platform for Action (in 1995), and the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1995. To empower women, and


---
**Page 279**
---

mitigate violence against them, Lesotho has also put in place legal frameworks such as The Legal Capacity of Married Persons Act, which entitles both men and women to the same rights and obligations regarding their marital duties, property, and Companies Act which reinforces women's rights on own property and control productive assets.424

The country has a strong gender focus as part of its National Strategy Development Plan, and aims to mainstream gender across the 4 focus areas of the Plan including inclusive and sustainable economic growth and development, social inclusion, strengthening governance and accountability, as well as governance and accountability system strengthening.

The country also has an ambitious Gender and Development Policy outlining 12 gender objectives to be implemented through a rights-based, multi-sectoral approach. These include measures ranging from legal protection of women's rights in the workplace, to women's access to water, to address issues related to gender and climate change as well as gender-based violence; providing food security, health, and sanitation services for women; and ensuring that women have equal access to land and property.

The government has also charted out a robust mechanism for the implementation of this policy with cross-cutting strategies including effective research, coordination, capacity building of the governing machinery, and Gender Responsive Budgeting.

The World Bank's Women, Business, and Law index scores Lesotho 78/1 out of 100. The country scores particularly low in the 'Parenthood' indicator since the country is yet to institute legislation mandating maternity leave for women. Further, the country does not have any provisions for criminal prosecution or civil penalties against sexual harassment faced by women in the workplace.425

Political participation

With a strong and articulated policy objective around gender and governance, Lesotho demonstrates a high commitment to ensuring women's political participation. However, women still remain underrepresented in both national and local level political positions, with only 26.4% of women in the National Assembly and 24.0% of women in the local council level. This is significantly lower than the global average between women and men's representation in ministerial positions with women holding only 22% of positions.

Lesotho has introduced affirmative action with a 30% quota for women at the local council level and a 'zebra-list' model for the 40 proportional representation (PR) seats in the National Assembly where women must be included. However, despite these measures, there is still a significant gap between the number of women in the National Assembly to women. However, regardless of these actions, there has been a steady decline in the political representation of women, falling from 58% to around 40% between 2003 and 2017 in local councils, and from 27% in 2012 to 29% in 2016 in the National Assembly.426

# Social and cultural context

The progressive nature of Lesotho’s institutional framework to address gender inequalities is evident in key legal Amendments made by the country in the previous section, however, the country is still patriarchal. The law states that a child born on or after 26th April 1973 whose mother was married at the time the male child becomes the principal heir of his father’s property. Further, Gender Based Violence and Child Marriage are still pervasive in the country. As per the country’s Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey (MICS) from 2015, 48% of women aged between 15-49 years were married before they turned 18 years; 63% were married before the age of 18. To reduce child marriages in the country, Lesotho launched a nationwide campaign called “No More Child Brides” in 2017. The campaign is aimed at raising awareness on girls, reinforcing the need to eradicate it. The Gender and Development Policy (2018-2030) has committed to zero tolerance towards all forms of violence against women and the country’s Sexual Offense Act (2003) also recognizes domestic violence as a crime. Further, in 2017, Lesotho passed a law supporting UNIFEM Country Domestic Violence legislation that criminalizes different types of violence, with the support of UNFPA. However, even within this framework, some reports indicate that: as many as 86% of women experience GBV in their lifetime.

## Health

Lesotho has faced multiple catastrophic droughts including the El Nino, which have led to crop failures, food shortages, violence, malnutrition and displacement. These climate shocks often have a disproportionate impact on women and girls who are responsible for collecting water and firewood. Data suggests that while the country expanded access to essential healthcare for women, quality provision of care remains poor in rural areas, particularly in the Eastern Districts and the Central Districts of the country. While the rate of institutional deliveries in the country is high (99.4%) and so is the rate of antenatal care (ANC) received by pregnant women, with 81% of women receiving at least ANC check-ups at least once, the country has a low rate of unmet need for family planning, with 20% of married women reporting either delay or negligence at the health facility level. Similarly, with respect to reproductive and sexual healthcare, compared to other countries in the region, Lesotho has a low rate of unmet need for family planning, with

only 11% of married women reporting unmet needs.** However, cervical cancer is the most common cause of death among women aged 40-49, due to late detection and treatment initiation.*** Lesotho also has one of the world's highest prevalence rates of HIV among women with 30.4% of women infected with HIV reporting having had multiple sexual partners in their lifetime as well as GBV, with a significantly higher prevalence in women who had been forced into sexual relationships (39.3%).**** By 2016, the country also set an ambitious target to end HIV/AIDS by 2020 under their National HIV & AIDS Strategic Plan (2015-2020). The plan aimed at strengthening national capacity for HIV and AIDS response; multi-sectoral coordination; and adoption of results-based planning language and accountability frameworks. The strategy had a strengthened focus on gender, with objectives around the reduction of Gender and Gender-Based Violence, along with a reduction in gender inequalities and reducing cultural and normative barriers for women.*****

Education Lesotho has an overall high fertility rate among women compared with other countries in the region; and has a higher fertility rate among women than men. As per the country's 2015 MICS, 52% of women are literate vs. 36.5% having attended secondary education and 13.2% of women having attended higher education. Comparatively, only 76% of men are literate; with only 43% having attended secondary education and just 8% having attended higher education. The country's Gender and Development Policy notes a gendered divide in STEM education, with the proportion of female students to male students being 0.4% in the faculty of Agriculture, Science, and Technology at the National University of Lesotho.*****

The country has a progressive education policy, under the Education Act (2010) which provides for free and compulsory primary education up to Grade 7. The law also stipulates that girls who have become pregnant while in school must be integrated back into schooling post after the delivery of their child.**** The country's Gender and Development policy also recognizes the gendered dimension to choice of disciplines, with girls being more likely than boys to choose subjects such as home economics, arts, crafts, health; and nutrition while the hard 'disciplines' such as sciences' have more male students. To correct this imbalance, the policy recommends that schools should provide equal opportunities for both genders in all other vulnerable groups to study Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM) subjects**** The Technical, Vocational Education and Training courses in Lesotho are government-sponsored to enable girls to complete these training****

---

# Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

As per Lesotho’s 2019 Labour Force Survey, the country has a female labor force participation rate (LFPR) of 45.3%, which is lower than other countries in the region and about 16 percentage points below the LFPR of men in the same age group. Women are more likely to be engaged in household-related activities as employers for undifferentiated goods and services producing activities of the households for their own use (70.4%) compared with men who are more likely to be engaged in market-oriented activities (58.2%). Women were engaged in agriculture, forestry, and fishery-related activities (16.7%) and construction (16.6%). Further, the mean earnings for women are much lower than men, i.e., M337.22 and M492.92 respectively.

As per the Global Gender Gap report 2022, there is also a wide gender gap with regards to the representation of women as legislators, senior officials, and managers, with only 33.7% women and 66.2% men as well as professional and technical workers, where women make up 39% workers.

To encourage women’s participation in the labour force, Lesotho undertakes very ambitious policy goals under the National Gender Policy (2015) which aims to achieve gender equality by ensuring women's control over productive resources, including land, information, technology, safe water, energy and finance, harmonizing policies with international standards, improving working conditions for women, putting in place public procurement quotas to source directly from women businesses, as well as advocating a 30% quota for women in decision making positions in the private sector.

The country also has a Science and Technology policy from 2008, which aims to achieve high participation of women in science and technology education and training and to promote gender equality in employment. Further, the policy places special focus on women entrepreneurs by providing them access to training, technical assistance, credit, and export development support, as well as increased access to technology-based incubation. However, the success of this policy are yet to be evaluated.

## Gender and energy in Lesotho

In Lesotho, the majority of households do not have access to electricity. As per the MICS, only 43.8% of people have access. The Department of Energy along with the UNDP, have collaborated on a gender mainstreaming strategy for the sector. The strategy has three main objectives:

1. To build an inclusive energy sector with institutions free of discrimination and inequality
2. To build the capacity of women entrepreneurs to participate in the energy sector supply value chain
3. To increase access to energy services by women

The energy policy (2015-2025) recognises gender mainstreaming as one of its guiding principles and states that gender equality is an integral part of the formulation and implementation of energy programmes and activities.


---
**Page 283**
---

A recent report found that within the Department of Energy, women represent a very small minority of the workforce (5%) overall. Further, in The LEC Transmission and Distribution Unit, only women were found to represent only 3.5% of staff in technical roles. However, in the private sector, there seems to be a high representation of women in senior management positions. In addition, most private sector energy companies are family businesses with couples representing Founding Directors. The overall representation of women in private sector energy companies is very high compared to regional and global averages (42%), however these women are predominantly found in non-technical roles such as marketing, finance, and administrative/secretarial roles.647

Among notable organisations active in the energy sector, Women In Sustainable Energy Lesotho (WISE-L) is an organisation that advocates for and promotes a gender balance in the energy sector as well as promotes entrepreneurship amongst women in this sector.648

# Burkina Faso

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Burkina Faso is a landlocked country in West Africa. It has a population of 22.1 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 33% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.5 births per woman.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) in Burkina Faso is 0.902, and the country is placed with Group-F countries with medium low time equality in HDI achievements between women and men (absolute deviation of 7.5 to 10 percent). This is partly due to the country's significant gender gap in women’s and men’s estimated gross national income per capita, which stands at $284 for women and $369 for men as of 2021, with a GII of 0.821 and is ranked 157, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.[4]

## Legal status of women

At the international level, Burkina Faso ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1987, including the Protocol which was signed against women, and the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (the Maputo Protocol) in 2006.[4] The country also ratified the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990. Burkina Faso signed the Beijing Platform for Action Conference recommendations and conclusions and committed to implementing them.[5]

**Page 285**

At the national level, the 1991 Constitution (Art. 1) prohibits any discrimination on the basis of sex, stating that all citizens are equal in law. In 1997 was created a Ministry dedicated to women and gender issues (formerly the Ministry of Women, Family Solidarity & Family Humanitarian Action). Burkina Faso adopted a National Gender Policy in 2009, which specific objectives are:

- Promote equal legal rights and equal opportunities in terms of access to and control over basic social services.
- Promote participatory economic development, access, and distribution of more equitable resources and income.
- Develop equal participation of men and women in decision-making spheres at all levels.
- Promote the empowerment of women.
- Promote a dynamic partnership for gender and development.
- Develop information and awareness-raising mechanisms aimed at all actors for a change in behavior and attitudes towards equality between men and women.

In terms of laws and regulations that affect women's economic opportunity in Burkina Faso, the country got a score of 82.5 out of 100 on the Women Business and the Law’s 2022 Index. This score is higher than the regional average observed across Sub-Saharan Africa (71.5). However, when it comes to restrictions on freedom of movement for women, Burkina Faso scores only 38/100 compared with a regional average of 64.

(Code des Personnes et de la Famille, Art. 294). In addition, the current legislation affects women's employment in the public sector and their access to social security benefits (Code des Personnes et de la Famille, Arts. 240 and 395) and parental leave. The existence of a law that prohibits women from working in an industrial job in the same way as men (Code des Personnes et de la Famille, Arts. 246 and 395) also limits women's opportunities.

Political participation

Despite the quotas of 30% for women for legislative and local elections, implemented in 2009 to promote women’s representation in the political sphere, women’s representation in the Assemblée Nationale is still low, with women holding only 17% of the seats. The original Law on Quotas from 2009 established financial sanctions (losing 50% of public funding for non-compliance with the 30% quota), but these were removed by a constitutional court decision in 2014. Women’s representation is also low at the local level, as prescribed by the regulations related to public funding of political parties (Law on Quotas, Article 5 §6). Burkina Faso has one of the lowest scores in the Global Gender Gap's Political Empowerment sub-index, just 20%, and it ranks 138 out of 146 globally.

Social and cultural context

Discriminatory practices towards women in Burkina Faso still prevail due to the variations in social relationships between men and women in the different ethnic groups, as each one has its customs and culture, as well as the social and economic conditions of women in urban and rural areas; and women are generally regarded as having a lower status than men. Often, society and the authorities disregard the


---
**Page 286**
---

Family and Penal Codes favoring the customary law, which perpetuates practices that discriminate against women.

Despite the Burkina Faso criminal code and civil law establishing that forced marriage is illegal,473 early marriage is still prevalent in Burkina Faso. Half of the women aged 20–24 (51.5%) were married or in a union before age 18.474 The Family Code475 recognizes polygamy as legal and allows men to marry up to four wives.476 Gerry motivates families to arrange marriages for their daughters as soon as they reach puberty.477 Polygamy is legal under the Family Code.478 A large part of the population is married under a polygamous regime (i.e., 39% of men and 10% of women).479 The Family Code does not provide for the rights of widows, nor does it allow them to inherit any property. According to Burkina Faso law, widows are entitled to inherit nothing under articles 742–744 of the Family Code. However, the customary law grants no right of inheritance to widows or minor children, so they cannot inherit land either.480 Although civil law does not discriminate against women, women's access to land is still limited in Burkina Faso. In many cases, men have exclusive rights over land and women have access to land, and in most cases, they share it with others. Only 0.12% of women own land alone or jointly with their husbands.481 The Family Code does not provide for the inheritance of land in which the land is generally the family's property and only reserves rights of use to the woman, and the fact that inheritance is the primary means of access to land.482

In Burkina Faso, 50% of women have experienced intimate partner violence (11.5%) not less than the world average of 27%.483 However, gender-based violence (GBV) continues to manifest in many ways, such as female genital mutilation (FGM). According to the World Health Organization's data from 1996, 76% of Burkina Faso women have had the procedure done.484 The country is one of the few in the region to have prosecuted perpetrators under legislation banning FGM (Penal Code Art. 5:13-7; 5:13-8; 5:19-9).485

Health

While marriage is in place that grants the right to women's reproductive health, more than half of the Burkinaise women (54%), are excluded from the decision-making process relating to their own health.486 The national family planning policy, adopted in 2004, guarantees access to contraception to all women. However, women face challenges accessing contraception mainly due to the disagreement of husbands and problems related to the lack of information on reproductive health services. Women are more exposed than men because they are still highly exposed to infection due to practices such as early or forced marriage.

The unwillingness of their partners to wear condoms, levirate marriage, and polygamy[48] it is estimated that there are 51 million women aged 15 and over with HIV compared to 37 million.[49]

Social norms tend to exclude women from the family planning/contraception process. Although most of the people surveyed said that men must be involved in family planning decisions, between a quarter and a third of the people say that women do not have to take part in the use of contraceptives, birth spacing, and the number of children they want to have.[50] In Burkina Faso, 23% of married women aged 15–49 are using modern methods. In Burkina Faso, it is estimated that 23 women abort per 1000 women ages 15–49 in 2020.[51] Most of the time, they are performed using non-recommended methods by untrained providers, causing a greater risk of complications for women[52].

Education

In terms of education, the literacy rate is higher in men (49.2%) compared to 31% in women.[53] The primary completion rate is higher in girls (57.3%) than in boys (67.7%), but this advantage is reversed in secondary school where only 20.8% of girls complete the course by the age of 19, while for boys it is around 40%. Only 20.59% of women graduate with STEM degrees in Burkina Faso compared to 79.4% of men.[54]

The Directorate for the Promotion of Girls’ Education (DPEF) was established within the Ministry of Basic Education and Literacy (MEBA) to promote activities to reduce gender-based inequalities. The DPEF also leads activities to raise awareness to keep girls in school.[55]

Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

60% of Burkinabé women (% of women ages 15–64) participate in the labor force participation compared to 70% of men (% of men ages 15–64).[56] Women's participation in the informal sector is high in Burkina Faso, with 82.3% of women working informally.[57]

Regarding entrepreneurship, there are no legal restrictions for women. Under civil law, women can access credits, sign contracts, register a business, and open a bank account like men.[58] However, formal financial institutions consider women as "high-risk" applicants as they often do not have financial or material security to guarantee the repayment of loans. This is also due to the lack of collateral for women's entrepreneurship. While there are no legal restrictions on Burkinabé women’s access to bank accounts, it remains difficult in practice.[59]

### Gender and energy in Burkina Faso

Burkina Faso's access rate sits at 19%, one of the lowest in the West African sub-region.[51] The expansion of renewable electrification faces critical barriers, including inadequate policy and regulatory frameworks, lack of access to finance, lack of local technical capacity, and high cost of new connections, among others. Women are disproportionately affected by these challenges due to their greater vulnerability and limited access to resources. This is partly due to gender gaps in education, access to finance, land tenure, and cultural norms that prevail in the country.

The Africa MiniGrids program supported by UNDP and financed by the GEF, seeks to demonstrate productive uses of electricity to reduce costs, increase the viability and enable mini-grid development at scale. The project will target 100,000 households (85% women) living in rural areas of Burkina Faso (south, mostly) who will benefit directly from the electricity supplied by the solar mini-grids. The project started in 2022 and is expected to be completed in 2024.

Among other initiatives, the GCF-funded Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGF/ENFI): Greening Agricultural Banks & the Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Great Green Wall (GGW) countries program is implemented by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD). The program targets farmer organizations and cooperatives in Burkina Faso. The program seeks to provide concessional loans to foster the best adaptation and mitigation practices in agriculture, while also promoting gender equality and women's empowerment. The program is backed by local national agricultural banks (LNABs) – the Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso–the facility will offer special lines of financing for green business projects prepared by farmer’s organizations (FOs), women and youth groups, cooperatives, and other financial intermediaries (banks, microfinance institutions, etc.). Besides providing loans, the program includes a Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) aimed at addressing capacity gaps in the sector and gender gaps hindering the uptake of green business projects in the selected countries. It will target LNABs, central banks, and clients (FOs, MFIs, and cooperatives), with a special emphasis on women and youth.[52]

# Guinea-Bissau

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Guinea-Bissau is a coastal country in West Africa, has a population of 2.1 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 32% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.2%. 

The Gender Inequality Index in Guinea-Bissau is 0.567, and the country is placed in "Group F" countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men, with a high absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10%. The country particularly has a wide gender gap in mean years of schooling, with 2.4 years for girls compared to 3.6 years for boys.

The UNDP (2022) Gender Inequality Index is 0.567 and is ranked 159; the index measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women

Internationally and regionally, Guinea-Bissau has made several commitments to equal rights for women. The country is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1981; as well as the Protocol to the Charter of Human Rights and Peoples on the Rights of Women in Africa<sup>**</sup>. Further, the country's "Social Protection Framework Law," Law 4/2007 defines the rights or the protection of women's rights in the labor market, including informal work provisions. However, it does not contain specific protection regulations for the informal labor market.

Legislation exists in the country to protect women from violence, however, the implementation of these has been weak with regard to violence against women, as discussed in the next section. The Law on Domestic Violence (Law Nbr. 6/2014 of February 13) prohibits all forms of physical, psychological, property, and sexual violence. The country also has a Law to combat Female Genital Mutilation (Law Nbr. 14/2011 of January 8), which is discussed in the next section.[75] While the country's Land Law guarantees the right to private use of land without discrimination, the Constitution does not have any specific provisions to protect women’s right to use land, and their access to land remains limited. Women are also less likely than men to own or inherit property and household land, while women live under the looming threat of having their land taken away.[76]

As per the Women, Business and Law index, the country has a score of 42.5 out of 100, with the poorest performance in the area of inheritance laws (score: 38). According to the UNDP’s Gender Inequality Index (GII) of the country's legislation, no provisions were found to grant women equal rights to an industrial job, same as men, or equal pay. Under the country's Civil Code, Art. 160, women do not have the same right to register their marriage and divorce as men. Women are also subject to a range of criminal penalties, or civil remedies for sexual harassment in employment, nor does it have maternity or paternity benefits in place for working mothers.[77]

The country has adopted the National Policy for Gender Equality and Equity, aimed at addressing gender inequalities and combating discriminatory practices; however, this policy has not been adapted into implementation yet. [78] With technical support from UNDP, the country's National People’s Assembly is currently drafting a law to amend the constitution with the integration of a gender lens.

### Social and cultural context

Guinea-Bissau is extremely diverse with numerous sub-groups, all with these having a patriarchal social structure (except the Bijagó group of the Bijagós Archipelago) and customary law aligned with traditional gender roles.[79] Social life among these ethnic groups is characteristically male-dominated, under the culture of patrilineality. Women are considered second-class citizens in society; they have no rights to own or guarantee over property, placing them in decision-making roles, and institutionalizing exalted men above the law.[80] The country has a high prevalence of polygamy (52%), with women having an average of 3 wives in polygamous unions,[81] with men exerting their dominance over multiple women under such unions, where men must prove their masculinity by producing more children.[82] Gender-based violence, including Female Genital Mutilation or "Fianada di mulher" is a critical aspect of the multicultural culture, and is considered

# Political participation

Guinea-Bissau has experienced political instability for decades. The political system is semi-presidential.

The country’s affirmative action in 2019 by introducing legislation demonstrating its commitment to uplifting women's position in political decision-making. The country's Party Law establishes that there must be a minimum of 35% of women in party lists for elective positions at the National Parliament and in local municipalities. In the 2019 elections, the ruling party, the Partido da Libertação de Guinea-Bissau (PLG), which is led by President José Mário Vaz, fielded a record number of female candidates. Of the 57 parties that participated in the election completed with the Party Law's 35% election saw an increase in the number of women elected to parliament. The government has indicated their commitment women’s political participation by conferring 11 of the 31 ministerial portfolios to women and a total of 35% female participation in the Executive of Guinea-Bissau.

# Health

Years of political instability and unrest have impacted women's well-being in Guinea-Bissau. The country's deep-rooted customs that legitimize violence against women, forced marriages, and early initiation into reproductive labor, have adversely impacted women’s health. As per the MICS (2020), 27% of women in the age group 20-24 reported that they had their first pregnancy before the age of 15. Further, women have to travel far for access facilities, which combined with poor road infrastructure makes the situation


---
**Page 292**
---

precarious for women11 close to a majority of women in the country had fewer births (48%), and only 50.4% of women had access to living births facilities. The country has a high maternal mortality rate of 607 deaths per 100,000 live births, which is one of the highest in the world.12 Further, women’s access to contraception is limited, and 21% of women aged 15-49 who are currently married or in a union have an unmet need for family planning.

The country also has Sub-Saharan Africa's second-highest burden of HIV among the reproductive population, with 5.3% of the reproductive-age population living with HIV, and women making up 70% of these cases.13

## Education

In a country, where about half of the households spent more than 65% of their incomes on food, education becomes a non-essential expense. While the country's Basic Law of the Educational System (2011) despite its shortcomings, such as the lack of a national curriculum and the absence of a teacher training program at the 6th year, there is a long way to go in achieving gender parity in education at the secondary and above levels. The country has made some progress in terms of access to primary education, with 98% of children enrolled in classes. Bissau has a ninety percent point literacy gap between boys and girls. While the difference at the primary school level is not that stark, at the secondary school level there are only 11.7% females compared to males. The country's female students have access to education up to the third grade of elementary and higher education, with 2% of women having a technical education and 4% of men with technical education, and 2.1% women with a university education compared to 4-8% of men.

The country has an education sector plan (2017–2025) that promotes schooling and encourages the retention rate in all levels of the educational system. However, there is a lack of qualified teachers, equipment, and infrastructure is a looming issue in the country, with most schools, not having basics such as instructor's manual, and other teaching materials, and the students not having access to pencils, paper, schoolbooks, etc.

## Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

Guinea-Bissau’s economy is predominantly based around agriculture, with cashew farming being the primary source of income and employment for most of the country’s population. As per the country's latest Regional Survey on Employment and Informal Sector (2019), 86% of women in Guinea-Bissau work in the informal sector. Women in Guinea-Bissau are relegated to informal work and they compose the vast majority of the informal workforce. According to the same survey, 54.7% of women were employed in agriculture in the ESI survey, 51.9% of women are in vulnerable employment as part-time, family workers, or self-employed, compared with 33.4% of men. On the other hand, women are almost non-existent in high productivity informal sectors such as construction and transport. Women are also less likely to be employed in commercial establishments, and far distance and regional trade (10%) differences' among disillusioned youth is also higher among women than men. The proportion of women who have been dismissed from their employment or in unemployment, compared to 18.6% of men. Further, only 0.7% of women have formal remunerated work in the agriculture sector, while 27.5% of men have salaried work in the formal agricultural sector.

Gender and energy in Guinea-Bissau:

Currently, access to electricity among Guinea-Bissau’s population is low. As per the MCBS (2003), 19% of households had access to electricity with a connection to a public grid and 17.7% of households have electricity through a non-public network connection. Only limited access to modern energy sources. Women have to spend more time on the collection of firewood and exposure to smoke from cooking fires. Notably, women are engaged in various biomass-based processing industries including charcoal production, fish smoking, and street-food vending, and hence are important agents of change in the shift towards renewable energy.

The country has released a National Action Plan for Gender Mainstreaming in Access to Energy and The Guinea-Bissau Ministry of Natural Resources and Energy developed a National Action Plan (NAP), with a specific focus on mainstreaming gender into national development plans. The NAP aims to create an institutional framework, develop a gender action plan, and establish a monitoring and evaluation framework. The plan highlights the need to train women and young people and raise awareness around gender mainstreaming in energy, to enable a diverse and inclusive labor market in the energy sector. The NAP also seeks to ensure that women are involved in decision-making processes.

UNDP is currently implementing a project aimed at promoting better access to modern energy services through sustainable mini-grids and low-carbon bioenergy technologies among Guinea-Bissau’s forest-dependent communities. As part of the project trainings, it was found that there is tremendous scope for clean off-grid energy solutions in the country, with particular focus on women's engagement in the food processing and fishing sector (which is the second largest subsistence activity in Guinea-Bissau) and as a source of income.

# Niger

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Niger is a West African country with vast desert terrain. The country has a population of 26.1 million people, with a young and adolescent proportion of 33% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years.[598] The country is ranked as one of the lowest in terms of gender equality.

The Gender Development Index (NGDI) in Niger is 0.635, and the country is placed in Group C countries with low equity in HDI achievements between women and men, with a high absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10 points.[599] The average age at first marriage for girls is 17 years compared to 24 years among girls and 2.8 years among boys, and in gross national income per capita. Niger has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.611 and is ranked 153; the index measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.[600]

## Legal status of women

The Constitution of Niger which was adopted in 2010 upholds equal rights for men and women and guarantees the same rights and duties for women and men in Articles 8 and 10. Further, Article 22 explicitly mandates that the State shall take all necessary measures to ensure the full participation of women in public institutions, and in national development, through the country's policies.

The country’s quota law defines minimum representation percentages for women in public affairs and the commercial and social codes prohibit gender-based discrimination in hiring, work allocation, wages, and promotion practices.564

However, Niger is one of the few countries in the region, which has not yet ratified the African Charter on Human and People's Rights on the Rights of Women.565 The country ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1998; however, Niger has expressed reservations about its implementation.566-567

Further, the country’s legal and regulatory system accords supremacy to customary law over statutory law. For instance: the Civil Code, denies men as heads of the household with marital authority over wives, the Family Code, which is based on Islamic law, does not recognize women's property rights; and the Rural Code fails to provide promote and protect women’s ownership rights over land.568-570

The country has articulated its gender commitment in some key country policies and strategies documents including: (i) the National Gender Policy, the National Strategy for Disempowerment of Women (NDDC), Niger 2013; the National Gender Policy, the National Women’s Economic Empowerment Strategy, National GBV Prevention and Response Strategy, and the Gender and Islam Strategy.570 The country's Gender Policy has set out a vision to achieve gender equality in all spheres of life by creating an enabling cultural environment in relation to demography, peace, and security; (ii) strengthening of the institutional and legal framework for women’s empowerment; (iii) promoting the participation of women in decision-making bodies and women in the management of power; (iv) economic empowerment and inclusive growth in relation to sustainable management of the environment, disaster risk management, migration, and humanitarian emergencies; (v) strengthening of the capacity of women to access justice and protection from violence through training, evaluation, and partnership. The country also has a National Observatory for Gender Promotion under the

Prime Minister’s office with a mandate to monitor, and promote gender equity and equality.[58] Apart from this, the country also has gender cells and focal points within sectoral ministries, which are responsible for gender mainstreaming in policy formulation and promoting decision-making at ministerial and institutional levels; however, these cells are mostly non-operational.[59]

### Social and cultural context

The prevalence of Gender Based Violence in Niger is at 28.4%,[60] Women displaced in some regions of the country due to conflict or natural disasters are particularly vulnerable to GBV, as they often find themselves in refugee camps. Niger revised the Penal Code in 2003 to make provisions for certain forms of violence against women, particularly sexual harassment, female genital mutilation, slavery, pimping, incitement to debauchery, and forced marriage.[61] The country has also passed laws that criminalize rape, domestic violence, punish and manage cases of gender-based violence (GBV) and child marriage.[62] Niger also has the world’s highest fertility rate at 7.3 children per woman[63], and 40% of women in Niger are married before their 18th birthday or were married before the age of 15. To combat this, in 2021, the government issued a decree institutionalizing communal and village committees as formal child protection committees.[64] This further impacts girls’ ability to remain in school and complete their education. Polygamy is widely practiced in the country, and high fertility is often a tool for women to command the respect of their husbands.[65]

### Political participation

Niger has implemented quotas to enable the effective participation of women in decision-making by passing Law No. 2000-009 in 2000, and further revising the quotas in 2019 up to 25% for elective positions and 30% for ministerial positions.[66] As a result, at the national level, women hold 48 out of 175 seats in the National Assembly. 30.72% of MPs are women, as per their December 2020 election.[67] In the central administration, out of 1,784 decision-making positions only 20% i.e., 446 are held by women and, at the decentralized level, there is a total of 359 elected positions. Out of these, 10% or 36 seats are held by women.[68] At a total of 1,007 decision-making positions, 8% are held by women. At the decentralized level, there are 17 women out of 244 local government councilors and 5 women out of 94 mayors. In Regional Councils, 34 women were elected out of a total of 260 councilors.[13] There is a wide gender gap in Niger’s ministerial representation, with only 15.75% women ministers compared to 84.21% men.[69] The country has supported the establishment of the Niger Chapter of the

### Health

The high fertility rate in Niger has adverse impacts on women's health and well-being. Niger has a maternal mortality ratio of 599 per 100,000 live births, one of the highest in the world. The country also has very poor coverage of ANC and PHC checkups, with only 27.8% of women having 4 or more ANC visits in 2016-18 and 39.4% of women receiving at least four postnatal care (PNC) visits within the first six weeks after delivery.[17] Only 50.6% of births were attended by skilled birth professionals. Anemia among women is also highly prevalent with 46% of women being anemic.[17] The uptake of contraception in Niger is low due to religious beliefs under Islamism, and the lack of access to family planning services from public health facilities remains a concern.[17] Further, 18% of married women have an unmet need for family planning and only 20% of that demand is met with modern contraceptive methods.[17]

### Education

As per the World Bank data, Niger has a very low female literacy rate of 29%. [17] Further, girls in rural areas have low enrollment rates in schools. In 2016-17, the Gross Enrollment Ratio for girls in primary school was 72%, and only 59% of girls completed schooling in rural areas. This further declined at the secondary schooling level to 48%. The government has identified several constraints that contribute to low female access: socio-cultural constraints, inadequate financial resources available for their education, as well as early marriage. Within schools, poor infrastructure, gender-based violence, and sanitation further exacerbate these constraints.[17] In response to these challenges, the government of Niger in 2018 developed a National Strategy for Accelerating Girls and Women's Education and Training (SNAEEF). Under this strategy, the government has launched several initiatives such as the "One Child, One Learning" and "second chance" education programs for girls who have dropped out of schools by providing assistance to parents, food rations, scholarships, and deliberation of a decentralization policy for secondary education. The government also launched a program called "Marriage Ban" in 2018 which aims at early marriage and early pregnancy often leads to girls dropping out of schools, integrating women back into the schooling system.[17] In addition, the government has introduced a law that allows pregnant students who fall to falling pregnant. In 2019, the government of Niger adopted an order directing schools to allow married and/or pregnant girls to return to school, continue their studies, and return after they have delivered their babies.

For promoting vocational and technical education, the country has developed a Gender Strategy in Vocational and Technical Education and established 103 community-level training centers and 11

# Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

Female labor force participation rate (LFPR) in Niger stands at 61.73%, and the country has a gender disparity of 20 percentage points between men and women, with LFPR of 84.27% among men.[59] The high fertility rate, constraining social norms, and women’s roles in managing their households are some reasons for low female labor force participation rates.

According to the World Bank Group (WBG) survey from 2016, women work an average of 28 hours a week compared to men who work 43 hours. Agriculture is the dominant sector, and most women work as informal agricultural workers. World Bank estimates that about 95% of women in rural areas are involved in agriculture.

Entrepreneurship in the informal sector is a crucial phenomenon among women in Niger, and 23% of women aged 16-64 own an enterprise in the country. However, there is a vast gender gap of 61% in profits accrued by female entrepreneurs compared to male entrepreneurs.[59] This is due to factors such as limited availability of labor to help run enterprises, literacy and numeracy gaps, and informality of enterprise registration.

Textile manufacturing followed by wholesale retail trade and motor vehicle repairs are two main sectors where women are active in profits, 82%, and 68%, respectively.[59]

## Gender and energy in Niger

Currently, there are only 16.8% of households in Niger with access to electricity. The main source of fuel in the country is wood. This causes a lot of time burden on women who are primarily the ones responsible for firewood collection. Butane gas is another alternative source of energy in the country; however, the use is expensive for poorer Nigerians.[59] In urban areas, 82% of women-headed households have access to grid electricity.

# Chad

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Chad is a Central African country, with a population of around 17 million people – 49.8% of which are women—an annual population growth of 3.2%, with 48% of the total population under the age of 15.

The Gender Development Index for Chad is 0.77. According to the GDI scoring, Chad belongs to the group of countries with the lowest human development (countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men). The country ranks last on the Global Gender Gap Report 2020, which measures gender inequality gaps (GII) which measure disparities between men and women in the areas of economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment. The country is ranked among the highest in the world (165).

Chad also scores low on human development, with women being in worse conditions compared to men (0.339 HDI for women versus 0.441), and Chad ranks 190th out of 191 countries on the Human Development Index (2019).

## Legal status of women

The Republic of Chad is a member of the United Nations and the African Union. It has ratified a number of UN Human Rights Conventions and thus has made binding international commitments to adhere to the standards laid down in these universal human rights documents. All inhabitants of Chad may turn to the UN Human Rights Committee for individual complaints or petitions regarding violations of specific human rights or to ECOSOC for women's rights violations.<sup>590</sup> Women in Chad also have full voting rights. However, despite improvements over the years in the legal framework, there are still important challenges in reflecting women's legal rights in practice. Chad has a strong constitutional framework for equality which

<sup>589</sup>Average number of years of education received by people ages 25 and older, converted from educational attainment levels using official durations at all levels.
<sup>590</sup><https://www.humanrightslaw.org/countries-human-rights-in-chad


---
**Page 301**
---

includes the State’s obligation to eliminate all forms of discrimination against women, and has made some progress towards achieving women’s right to equality. For example, Chad has taken the necessary legislative steps to ban early marriage and child marriage. Unfortunately, implementation of these laws is inadequate. Women and girls continue to face the prospect of lives gender-based violence.

There are signs of positive steps. In 2022, the Ministry of National Education and Civic Promotion (MENPC) of the Republic of Chad, a member of the Gender at the Center Initiative (GCI), sought international support to provide training for teachers on gender equality in education and to develop national policies.

Political participation

In Chad, women represent more than 50% of the total population and are potentially a decisive electorate. A one-party system exists, though there is currently less than 30% representation of women in seats of national and local governments. As in other countries in the continent, gender norms impact women’s political participation, and women’s groups within the country have called on the government to guarantee their right to participate fully in public life. Women’s access to education has been very low. Despite the challenges, 32.3% of seats in Chadian Parliament are held by women, which is higher than the sub-Saharan Africa average of 25.7%.<ref>

Social and cultural context

The social and cultural context of women is shaped by factors such as religion, traditional beliefs, and societal norms. Despite legal protections, women in Chad often face discrimination in various domains, including education, employment, and inheritance rights. Harmful practices like child marriage and female genital mutilation (FGM) are prevalent, particularly among the Muslim population. Religion, predominantly Islam, significantly influences the social and cultural context of women’s rights in Chad. Customary laws and traditions also play a role in shaping these contexts.

Traditional gender roles and norms also play a significant role, with women often expected to conform to traditional roles as wives, mothers, and caregivers, constraining their opportunities for education and employment. Women in Chad have made progress in advocating for their rights, with active women’s rights organizations and civil society groups working towards gender equality and women’s empowerment.

<ref>UN OHCHR (2017). UN experts say poverty and culture cannot justify discrimination against women https://www.unocha.org/news/press-releases/un-experts-say-poverty-and-culture-cannot-justify-discrimination-against-women</ref>

**Page 302**

Chadian government has also taken steps to promote gender equality through policy reforms and legal protections, although challenges persist in implementation and enforcement.

### Health

A high maternal mortality rate is a major health issue in Chad; 1,140 women die per 100,000 live births, which is more than double the sub-Saharan African average, although it has improved from 1,420 in 2000.###93

Chadian women suffer from poor health compared to men, with only 31% of pregnant women receiving adequate prenatal healthcare, and 34% of women being subjected to female genital mutilation.

Moreover, women are disproportionately affected by HIV/AIDS, lack access to sexual and reproductive health rights; despite progress in 2019, only 17.5% of women had their need for family planning satisfied with modern methods, and 30% of married women had an unmet need for contraception. In addition, 48% of pregnant women gave birth outside a health facility, and 60% of abortions performed clandestinely, usually in unhealthy conditions.

### Education

The education situation for girls in Chad faces numerous challenges, including limited access to quality education, low enrollment rates, high dropout rates, early marriage and pregnancy, gender-based violence, cultural norms, and lack of parental support. These challenges often result in lower levels of educational attainment for girls compared to boys, which can have long-term consequences on their empowerment, well-being, and economic opportunities.

The average Chadian girl will be between 15 years of formal schooling compared to 3-7 years for men.###93

Only 40% of women have attended secondary school compared to 58% in men. Adult literacy in Chad is also lower among women than among men, with 18.2% of women being literate and 35.4% of men being literate. The gap in adult literacy between men and women is 17.2%, which is larger than the gap for the sub-Saharan African aggregate of 13%.


---
**Page 303**
---

Traditionally, education for women and girls is less valued and households often prioritize education for boys compared to girls. However, the Government of Chad has committed to mainstreaming gender equality in its national education policies, which is an encouraging step in promoting education for girls and women in Chad.[607]

## Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

The labor force participation rate among females is 48.4% and among males is 70.8%, giving Chad a higher labor force participation gap than the rest of sub-Saharan Africa. Factors such as early marriage, early pregnancy, lack of access to education, limited financial resources, and cultural norms that discourage vocational training may hinder women's ability to enter the workforce. Furthermore, social norms and traditional gender roles often assign women with household responsibilities while men are expected to fulfill earning responsibilities. While men are expected to be the breadwinners, this can limit women's ability to participate in economic activities outside of their homes, and women may face discrimination, biases, and stereotypes in the workplace.

In Chad, 75% of employed people work in agriculture,[608] which includes subsistence farming, fishing, and animal husbandry. Women play a critical role in food production and contribute to the rural economy by cultivating crops, raising livestock, and engaging in other agricultural activities to sustain their households and communities.[609] Despite their significant contributions, women are often employed in informal, petty trade, and small-scale enterprises. Despite their important contribution, 58.8% of female workers are in vulnerable employment compared with 41.2% for male workers, which is higher than the sub-Saharan average. These are workers who are the least likely to have formal work arrangements, social protection, and safety nets to guard against economic shocks; thus they are more likely to fall into poverty, posing challenges to their ability to lift themselves out of poverty.[610] As such, 45% of the employed female population lives below the poverty line compared to 39% of men.[611]

There are also gender gaps in the formal sector. It is estimated that only 1% of female workers are salaried workers, compared to 13% of male workers,[612] and women constitute less than 18% of professional and technical positions.[613] Women's access to credit remains limited; they own only 40% of land in private ownership and only 12% of firms with female top managers.[614] Furthermore, female entrepreneurs may face challenges such as limited access to financial markets, and business development services due to gender-based discrimination.

The principle of non-discrimination on the ground of sex, age or nationality prevails within Chad's Labour Code (1996) with regards to employment, notably recruitment, hiring, terms and conditions, promotions, training, dismissal, and severance. Women are entitled to paid maternity leave for 4 weeks during which mothers are entitled to the totality of their wages, with a further 6 months of unpaid parental leave. However, women are mostly involved in the informal sector and precarious jobs and thus remain excluded from social protection in the formal sector. Furthermore, even women within the formal sector face gender bias. The Labour Code prohibits women from entering certain professions which could
