endanger their health, safety or morality, and the Civil Code stipulates that a woman can choose a profession on the condition her husband agrees.104

Women's economic empowerment is also threatened through challenges in accessing land, financial services, and credit. These factors limit women’s ability to invest which can limit their productivity and income generation potential. Chad's Civil Code stipulates that a woman can open a bank account at a formal institution for the funds given by her husband. However, the husband must be notified and must authorize withdrawals from the account.105 Women are also subject to customary laws and practices that may restrict their access to land or of collateral and lack of information regarding credit. Additionally, it is reported that in some parts of Chad, women cannot inherit land because they are considered minors under the law.106 Even though Law on the Limitation of Land Rights guarantees the right to everyone to own, use, make decisions and use as collateral, customary law and practices often prioritize male ownership and inheritance of land. Most women do not have access to land or other resources owned by their husband, and they generate income from those lands; it is usually given back to their father or husband107.

Gender and energy in Chad

Only 11% of the population has access to electricity in Chad. Women in Chad, particularly those in rural areas, often face additional challenges in accessing energy services due to factors such as poverty, social norms, lack of infrastructure, and limited access to financial resources. These challenges can disproportionately affect women's ability to access and benefit from modern energy services, such as electricity for lighting, cooking, and productive uses. Sex-disaggregated data on access to energy as well as women’s participation in the sector is absent.

In 2022, the World Bank approved the $259M Chad Access to Electricity (ATE) project (AAMET-11), aimed at increasing access to electricity by reducing poverty and expanding the reach of the national grid and mini-grids, standalone solar systems, deployment of improved stoves, and natural resource management. The project will include targeted interventions to improve access to electricity for female-headed households, including a refugee camp, and support enterprises that promote women entrepreneurship, and build awareness relevant to women and girls around energy services.108

# Somalia

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Somalia is an East African country, with a population of around 17 million people – 49.9% of which are women – an annual population growth of 3.1%, with 47% of the total population under the age of 15.[6]

There is a scarcity of socioeconomic data in Somalia, including on gender, because of years of political instability and civil war. This has led to lack of institutions and systems needed to collect, analyze, and disseminate reliable information about women’s status and needs. The absence of a national statistical system, an inability for researchers and organizations to access certain regions for data collection, and significant displacement of the population have all contributed to inadequate data collection and tracking of social and economic indicators. As such, Somalia does not have a Human Development Index, Gender Inequality Index or a Gender Development Index.[6]

It’s likely that Somalia would belong to the group with the lowest human development group for all three indices, and that gender inequality and deviation from gender parity is high.

## Legal status of women

Women in Somalia face difficulties in accessing justice due to cultural restrictions, differing interpretations of judicial regimes, and the absence of women within the judicial system. As of 2021, there are only two female judges out of a total of 295 and 14 female prosecutors out of a total of 65, highlighting the need for gender parity in the judicial systems.[6]

Page 302

Somalia has a history of Western and Human Rights Development, mandated to advance the promotion and protection of gender equality and human rights, including the rights of women, children and other vulnerable groups. Increased awareness of the rights of women and girls has been driven by the growing influence amongst international NGOs. The Federal Government of Somalia launched the Somali National Action Plan (NAP) in 2016 to implement the provisions of the United Nations Security Council Resolution 1325 (UNSCR 1325). The inclusion and participation of Somali women in peacebuilding and decision-making at all levels is the primary goal of the NAP, where these issues are recognized as a cross-cutting issue across government policy areas and development.

Despite progress in the formal recognition of women's legal status, the United Nations has reported that cultural factors and customary law can lead to more accepting of inequities or victimization of women and girls. In addition, women continue to be underrepresented in decision-making positions at all levels of government and in political groups (and those who do participate), cultural factors have limited their participation.

There is some hope the National Action Plan will be able to have a broad impact on women's lived experience in Somalia, including their legal standing. Conflict-related sexual and gender-based violence, access to justice, and the lack of representation for women in decision-making positions are among national reconciliation and transitional justice mechanisms, prevention of violent extremism, as well as climate change, natural disasters, and other development challenges. The NAP identifies three cross-cutting action areas for women's wellbeing and equity, and are to be addressed by the national action plan.

Political participation

In Somalia, women account for 50.13% of the population (2016). Somalia reached a milestone with nearly a quarter of parliamentary seats occupied by women. In January 2021, a quota was announced for a third of all parliamentary seats to be reserved for female lawmakers. There has since been limited progress in female representation in the national legislative bodies - the 325 seats in Somalia's lower and upper houses of parliament are overwhelmingly male-dominated. The 2021 election saw women's representation fall to 20% in the House of the People, which is lower than the 2016 figure of 24%. There were some gains made with 26% seats for women in the Upper House in 11th Parliament, compared to 24% in the previous parliament. Women remain underrepresented in political and public life, male-dominated. Somali women largely remain excluded from key political and decision-making processes.

Social and cultural context

Traditional customs, religious doctrines and societal expectations are among the various factors that contribute to the social and cultural environment that Somali women experience. Women and girls across the country continue to face a range of gender-based discrimination and violence. Somalia is one of the lowest countries in the world for gender equality, with high rates of maternal and infant mortality, as well as

**Page 307**

prevalence of early marriage. Stereotypes about women in the workplace, clan-based identity politics that center men and deep-rooted gender-based typologies are among the factors behind gender inequity and inequality. For example, girls receive less education than boys, which has implications for female participation in the economy. Certain social norms negatively impact women; for example, early marriage based on financial considerations, which can have severe physiological and psychological impacts throughout a woman's life, and the expectation of childbearing.

Despite the introduction of the Sexual Offences Bill (SOD) in 2018, which would provide greater protection for victims of sexual violence and help end the culture of impunity relating to GBV, women in Somalia remain under-represented within and excluded from key political and decision-making processes. Efforts by civil society organizations and international partners have helped some women display some awareness of the inequities and challenges existing in the sociocultural context. However, amid ongoing conflict, women continue to be disproportionately affected by GBV, particularly those living in IDP camps or by militant groups in both urban and rural areas, and with GBV being a feature of this conflict, there are even greater challenges for the government to adequately provide for the security, protection, social, environmental, and economic needs of the female population.

Health

A high maternal mortality rate is a major health issue in Somalia. 829 women die per 100,000 live births, which is higher than the sub-Saharan African average. The high maternal mortality rate is caused by several factors such as limited access to healthcare, poor healthcare infrastructure, socio-cultural factors like early marriage and childbearing.

Somali women suffer from worse health outcomes than men and face significant barriers to reproductive health, with only 24% of pregnant women receiving adequate prenatal healthcare, and 37% of married women with unmet need for contraception. Furthermore, 95% of women in girls and Somalia have undergone female genital mutilation (FGM), which is a harmful practice that can lead to long-lasting complications in children which can lead to maternal mortality. What's more, abortion in Somalia has been criminalized, except to save the life of the mother, and a cesarean is performed only if the husband and/or the mother-in-law give permission. Women are more malnourished than men and are less food secure.

Education

The education situation for girls in Somalia is characterized by various challenges and disparities, such as long distances between schools and homes, lack of infrastructure, and teachers' salaries have not seen improvements in recent years; girls' education in the country continues to face obstacles. Socio-cultural norms in Somalia often favor boys’ education over girls’, leading to gender disparities in access to


---
**Page 308**
---

education. The deeply rooted cultural and religious beliefs, coupled with gender stereotypes, contribute to the marginalization of girls in the education system. There is limited data on school enrollment and attendance in Somalia. Of Somalia's estimated population of over nine million children aged five or older, 40% are out of school.[658] There is also limited data on literacy rates in Somalia, although they are likely to be particularly low amongst women.

Early marriage and childbearing also prevent girls in Somalia from attaining an equal education to boys. Somalia has a high adolescent fertility rate of 118.0 births per 1,000 women aged 15–19.[659] This is also higher than the sub-Saharan average of 100.9, although the rate of adolescent fertility has decreased since 2010. For example, in 2010, 16.7% of women were married before the age of 15,[660] and 35% are married before the age of 18.[661]

Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

The labor force participation rate among females is 21%, and among males is 46.9%, giving Somalia a higher labor force participation gap than the rest of Sub-Saharan Africa.[662] An overwhelming majority of the women are self-employed and work in agriculture-related activities. Very few women take up economic activity outside their homes, especially in export-oriented industries such as fishing and fishing export industries; women are hardly represented. Female farmers also have less access to agricultural resources, capital and information than their male counterparts.[663]

96.5% of all women work in vulnerable employment in Somalia compared to 88.8% of men, both of which are higher than the sub-Saharan average of 74%. Women face a number of barriers such as lack of formal work arrangements, social protection, and safety nets to guard against economic shocks; thus they are more likely to fall into poverty, posing challenges to women's economic well-being and empowerment.

There are also gender gaps in the formal sector. Only 3% of women in the labor force are salaried workers, compared with 10% of men.[664] Moreover, women represented only 27% of those employed in senior and middle management positions in 2018, which is lower than the sub-Saharan average; these data are not available for Somalia.

Women are also less likely to be able to access capital. In 2014, 53.7% of women and 43.6% of men in Somalia had an account with a financial institution or with a mobile money service provider; both of which are lower than the sub-Saharan average.[665] The few institutions that provide financial services have demanding conditions and procedures to access funding, such as strict requirements for collateral securities (for example, land titles), which few rural women own as compared to men.[666]

Gender and energy in Somalia

Energy access in Somalia is limited, particularly in rural areas, and women are disproportionately affected by the lack of reliable and affordable energy sources. National energy access in Somalia is 18% and less than 5% for rural areas. Lack of access to energy hinders women's ability to improve their livelihoods, gain access to information, communication, and education, as well as constraining their economic opportunities. Sex-disaggregated data on access to energy as well as women's participation in the sector is absent.

Several programs and initiatives, led by international organizations, non-governmental organizations, and the Somali government, aim to address these challenges. These efforts focus on promoting renewable energy technologies, building capacity, and fostering gender equality in the energy sector. For instance, the Sustainable Charcoal Reduction and Alternative Livelihoods (SCREAL) project aims to reduce charcoal consumption by 50% through the promotion of improved cookstoves, creating self-efficient stoves, and creating alternative livelihood opportunities, particularly for women and youth.[68] The Africa MiniGrids Program (AMP) aims to bring new development opportunities to rural communities and to increase access to electricity for 137K people, half of whom will be women.[69]

# Conclusions and recommendation for the Gender Action Plan

This assessment provides an overview of the status of gender equality in the sixteen H2R countries. The H2R initiative has the opportunity to address some of the underlying causes of gender inequality in the countries by providing access to women to clean energy technologies and job opportunities in the sector. In particular, the following objectives are relevant:

## H2R's Gender Objectives:
1. Elevate women in the OGS sector as customers, employees and entrepreneurs
2. Do no harm to women’s rights and gender equality
3. Provide poorest and most vulnerable women and Women Headed Households with clean energy
4. Invest in women-led businesses and businesses with gender-forward business practices
5. Promote gender equality through the influencing portfolio of companies to adopt and/or expand gender business practices

## H2R's Gender Action Plan will adhere to its objectives through 4 components:
- The first is to apply a gender lens across investment processes to mitigate gender biases and identify hidden opportunities.
This involves integrating gender considerations at each step of the decision-making process to produce a portfolio that maximizes the potential for women’s empowerment. This will be achieved by applying post-investment gender-focused TA to investors to incentivize companies to adopt, expand, and/or implement gender-forward business practices. Post-investment TA support will focus on the development and implementation of gender strategies, as well as the creation of a gender-sensitive monitoring framework.
- The second is to build awareness at the level 1, 2, and 3 levels in order to bridge the gap in gender awareness at the investment level. Third, H2R will work to build opportunities for women in the broader energy sector by providing access to training, education, and skills development programs and readiness in OGS markets.
- Finally, H2R will catalyze OGS ecosystems in H2R markets by crowding in a variety of stakeholders and sharing lessons. H2R will invest in insights, data aggregation, and OGS ecosystem development, with the goal of building an active working group to address common goals.

These components are further detailed in the GAP.

# H2R Gender Action Plan

## Impact Statement:
If the appropriate investment capital and TA is provided to OGS companies, then they will be able to deliver clean energy access to women customers and women-headed households and to increase diversity in their workforce, increasing companies' growth opportunities and contributing to women economic empowerment because they will be adequately incentivized and capacitated to operate in "Hardest-to-Reach" (H2R) contexts.

## Outcome Statement:
* Access to clean and decentralized energy to 94,067,623 women (47% of 192,441,750 total beneficiaries)
* Sustainable and inclusive growth of OGS businesses
* Reduction in 5.1 Mt CO2eq to preserve and improve women's health
* Increased resilience of women and girls via improved health, safety and wellbeing
* Reduced vulnerability of women

## Output Statement:
Funding and technical assistance lead to increased capacity of OGS companies to serve low-income women customers and to be able to retain women employees, as well as creating gender-inclusive workplaces.

### H2R's Gender Objectives:
1. Elevate women in the OGS sector as customers, employees and entrepreneurs
2. Do no harm from a gender perspective
3. Provide poorest and most vulnerable women and Women-Headed Households with clean energy
4. Invest in women-led businesses and businesses with gender-forward business practices
5. Position impactful gender culture for women employees through the influencing portfolio companies to adopt and/or expand gender business practices
